Outliers of perturbations of banded Toeplitz matrices
Charles Bordenave, Mireille Capitaine, and François Chapon

Abstract
Toeplitz matrices form a rich class of possibly non-normal matrices whose asymptotic spectral analysis in high dimension is well-understood. The spectra of these matrices are notoriously highly sensitive to small perturbations. In this work, we analyze the spectrum of a banded Toeplitz matrix perturbed by a random matrix with iid entries of variance σ n 2 / n \sigma_{n}^{2}/n in the asymptotic of high dimension and σ n \sigma_{n} converging to σ ≥ 0 \sigma\geq 0 . Our results complement and provide new proofs on recent progresses in the case σ = 0 \sigma=0 . For any σ ≥ 0 \sigma\geq 0 , we show that the point process of outlier eigenvalues is governed by a low-dimensional random analytic matrix field, typically Gaussian, alongside an explicit deterministic matrix that captures the algebraic structure of the resonances responsible for the outlier eigenvalues. On our way, we prove a new functional central limit theorem for trace of polynomials in deterministic and random matrices and present new variations around Szegő’s strong limit theorem.



1
Introduction

1.1
Perturbation of banded Toeplitz matrices

Let
T_{n}(\bfa)
be a
n\times n
banded Toeplitz matrix, with symbol
\bfa\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{C}
given by the Laurent polynomial

(1)

{\bf a}(\lambda)=\sum_{k=-r}^{s}a_{k}\lambda^{k},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=1\},

where we assume without loss of generality that
r\geq 0
,
s>0
and the
a_{k}
’s are complex numbers with
a_{s}\not=0
(the symmetric case
r>0
and
s\geq 0
readily follows by considering the transpose). For integer
n>\max(r,s)
, the matrix
T_{n}(\bf a)
is thus the matrix
(a_{j-i})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
whose diagonals are constant:

(2)

T_{n}(\bfa)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}&a_{1}&\cdots&a_{s}&0&\cdots&0\\
a_{-1}&a_{0}&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
a_{-r}&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&a_{s}\\
0&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&a_{0}&a_{1}\\
0&\cdots&0&a_{-r}&\cdots&a_{-1}&a_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

In this paper, we are interested in random perturbations of the matrix
T_{n}(\bfa)
in the asymptotic of large dimension
n\to\infty
. That is, we consider the matrix

(3)

M_{n}=T_{n}(\bfa)+\sigma_{n}Y_{n},

where
\sigma_{n}>0
and
Y_{n}=(Y_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
is some noise matrix normalized so that its normalized Hilbert-Schmidt norm is of order one:

\E\frac{1}{n}\|Y\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}=\E\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j}|Y_{ij}|^{2}=1,

(see below for the precise assumptions on
Y_{n}
considered in this paper). With this scaling, the matrices
Y_{n}
and
T_{n}
are of comparable Hilbert-Schmidt norms. We will assume that the following limit exists: for some real
\sigma
,

(4)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma_{n}=\sigma.

We will distinguish two distinct regimes:

(i)

\sigma>0

(ii)

\sigma=0
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln\sigma_{n}=0
.

The first case corresponds to a macroscopic perturbation of
T_{n}(\bfa)
. The second case corresponds to a microscopic perturbation which, as illustrated notably
[
SV21
,
BZ20
]
, is however large enough to have an important effect on the spectrum. We will refer in the sequel these two cases as
\sigma>0
and
\sigma=0
.

There are multiple motivations to study random matrices of the form (
3
). One of them comes from numerical matrix analysis. The eigenvalues and eigenvectors of non-normal matrices (such as
T_{n}(\bfa)
when
a_{i}\neq\bar{a}_{-i}
for some
i
) can be very sensitive to matrix entries and rounding errors. As a consequence numerical outputs for eigenvalues can be completely wrong even for matrices of low dimensions, see Figure
1.1
for an illustration. Attempts to capture this phenomenon include the notion of pseudo-spectrum, see Trefethen and Embree
[
TE05
]
. Von Neumann and Goldstine
[
vNG47
]
initiated the idea to study numerical rounding errors in matrix computations by random noise. This idea has now a very rich history, see for example Spielman and Teng
[
ST01
]
or Edelman and Rao

[
ER05
]
.
As pointed in
[
TE05
, Chapter 7]
, non-normal Toeplitz matrices are the best understood non-normal matrices and they are the most obvious choice of matrices to study spectral instability in great details. This fruitful approach has notably been followed in
[
BC16
,
SV16
,
SV21
,
BPZ20
,
BZ20
,
BVZ23
,
AK24
]
. As pointed in
[
SV16
,
SV21
]
, beyond numerical analysis, another motivation for studying random matrices of the form (
3
) comes from non-Hermitian quantum mechanics, see
[
HN96
,
GK00
]
.

(a)
ST(\bfa)S^{*}
,
n=10
.

(b)
ST(\bfa)S^{*}
,
n=2107
.

(c)
FT(\bfa)F^{*}
,
n=10
.

(d)
FT(\bfa)F^{*}
,
n=2107
.

Figure 1.1:
Output for eigenvalues in Python using
numpy.linalg.eig
for the nilpotent matrix
\bfa(\lambda)=\lambda
. (a)-(b):
S
is a (random) permutation matrix which hides the upper-triangular structure, and (c)-(d)
F
is the Fourier basis, see (
5
), which introduces rounding errors in matrix entries.

1.2
Assumptions on the noise matrix

Our matrix
Y_{n}
will have independent and identically distributed entries in an orthogonal basis. More precisely, we assume that

Y_{n}=U_{n}\frac{X_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}U_{n}^{*},

where
U_{n}
is a
n\times n
unitary matrix and
X_{n}=(X_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
will satisfy the assumption:

(H_{0})

(X_{ij})_{i,j\geq 1}
are independent and identically distributed complex random variables with
\E X_{ij}=0
,
\E|X_{ij}|^{2}=1
,
\E X_{ij}^{2}=\varrho
, for some
0\leq\varrho\leq 1
.

To be precise, the common distribution of the random variables
X_{ij}
is assumed to be independent of
n
. Throughout this paper, we implicitly assume that
(H_{0})
holds. For technical reasons, some of the following assumptions will also be needed in the statements of our results. Below, we say that the distribution of a complex random variable
X
is symmetric if
X
and
-X
have the same distributions. For real
k>2
, we consider the assumptions:

(H_{k})

\E|X_{ij}|^{k}<\infty
.

(H_{\mathrm{sym}})

The distribution of
X_{ij}
is symmetric.

(H_{\mathrm{ac}})

The law of
X_{11}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
or on
\omega\mathbb{R}
,
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

We conjecture than none of the assumptions
(H_{k})
-
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
are necessary for the main results below of this introduction to hold (that is,
(H_{0})
should suffice), this conjecture is notably supported by
[
BPZ20
,
BZ20
,
BCGZ22
]
where closely related results are established without these assumptions.

There are two particularly interesting choices of unitary matrices
U_{n}
which correspond respectively to the canonical basis and the discrete Fourier basis: that is,
U_{n}=I_{n}
and
U_{n}=F^{*}_{n}
with
F_{n}=(F_{n}(i,j))_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
given, with
\omega_{n}=e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi/n}
, by

(5)

F_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1&\cdots&1\\
1&\omega_{n}&\omega_{n}^{2}&\cdots&\omega_{n}^{n-1}\\
\\
1&\omega_{n}^{2}&\omega_{n}^{4}&\cdots&\omega_{n}^{2(n-1)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\\
1&\omega_{n}^{n-1}&\omega_{n}^{2(n-1)}&\cdots&\omega_{n}^{(n-1)(n-1)}\end{pmatrix}.

The Fourier basis is especially natural because it is the basis which diagonalizes the circulant matrix
C_{n}(\bfa)
associated to the symbol
\bfa
(see below). Note finally that if the distribution of
X_{ij}
is the complex standard Gaussian distribution on
\mathbb{C}
(corresponding to
X_{ij}
Gaussian and
\varrho=0
) then the distribution of
Y_{n}
does not depend on the unitary matrix
U_{n}
.

1.3
Convergence of the empirical spectral distribution

Before turning to the main focus of this paper, we describe the asymptotic behavior of typical eigenvalues of
M_{n}=T_{n}(\bfa)+\sigma_{n}Y_{n}
defined in (
3
).

In the case
\sigma=0
, under mild assumptions on
Y_{n}
,
[
BPZ20
]
proved that the empirical measure of the eigenvalues (or empirical spectral distribution) of
M_{n}
converges weakly, in probability to
\beta_{0}
defined as the law of
{\bf a}(U)
with
U
uniformly distributed on

\mathbb{S}^{1}
(see also
[
SV21
]
for the case of Gaussian noise). Their assumptions are for example met if
\sigma_{n}=n^{-\gamma}
,
\gamma>0
and if assumptions
(H_{0})
holds. Beware that if
Y_{n}=0
, that is
M_{n}=T_{n}(\bfa)
the empirical spectral distribution does not converge toward
\beta_{0}
in the non-normal case, see Subsection
2.2.2
below.

In the case
\sigma>0
, if Assumption
(H_{0})
holds, the empirical spectral distribution of
M_{n}
converges towards a probability measure
\beta_{\sigma}
on
\mathbb{C}
. The measure
\beta_{\sigma}
is the Brown’s spectral measure of
{\bf a}(u)+\sigma c
where
c
and
u
are free noncommutative random variables in some tracial noncommutative probability space
(\mathcal{A},\phi)
such that
c
is a circular noncommutative random variable and
u
is Haar distributed. This result will be explained in Section
3.1
below and is a consequence of the combinations of the main results of
[
Ś02
]
and
[
TV10
]
.

For
\sigma>0
, the measure
\beta_{\sigma}
has a relatively explicit expression (see Section
3.1
). We will prove the following result valid for all
\sigma\geq 0
.

Lemma 1.1
.

For
\sigma\geq 0
, the support of
\beta_{\sigma}
is given by

\supp(\beta_{\sigma})=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{|{\bf a}(e^{i\theta})-z|^{2}}\geq\sigma^{-2}\right\}

where by convention
1/0=\infty
, so that
\supp(\beta_{0})=\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
.

Figure
1.2
shows an example, for the symbol
\bfa(t)=t^{2}+2t+it^{-2}-0.5it^{-3}
and
\sigma=0.8
, of the curve
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and the support of the Brown measure
\beta_{\sigma}
.

(a)
The curve
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
.

(b)
The curve
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and the boundary of
\supp\beta_{\sigma}
.

Figure 1.2:
Example of the closed curve
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and the support of the Brown measure
\beta_{\sigma}
, for the symbol
\bfa(t)=t^{2}+2t+it^{-2}-0.5it^{-3}
and
\sigma=0.8
.

The objective of this paper is to describe the eigenvalues of
M_{n}
in closed regions of
\mathbb{C}\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
which we will call the outlier eigenvalues. For
\sigma=0
,
U_{n}=I_{n}
(canonical basis) this objective has essentially been completed in
[
SV21
,
BZ20
]
and, with a very different approach, for
\sigma>0
,
r=0
,
s=1
in
[
BC16
]
(in the Fourier basis).

1.4
Stable region

The infinite dimensional Toeplitz operator

T({\bf a})
is the operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
defined by

(T({\bf a})x)_{i}=\sum_{l=-r}^{s}a_{l}x_{l+i},\;\mbox{for $i\in\mathbb{N}$, \; where}\;x=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)

and we set
x_{i}=0
for non-positive integer values of
i
.
The spectrum of
T({\bf a})
is given by, see
[
BG05
, Corollary 1.11]
,

\spectrum T(\bfa)=\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})\cup\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1}):\wind(\bfa-z)\not=0\right\},

where
\wind(\mathbf{b})
is the winding number around
0
of a closed curve
\mathbf{b}:\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{C}
. If
b
extends to a meromorphic function, it is equal to the number of zeroes of
\mathbf{b}
in the unit disk
\mathbb{D}
minus the number of its poles in
\mathbb{D}
.

The following result asserts that there are typically no outliers of
M_{n}
in
\spectrum(T(\bfa))^{c}
as
n\to\infty
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that
(H_{4})
holds.
Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\backslash(\supp(\beta_{\sigma})\cup\spectrum T(\bfa))
be a compact set. Then, almost surely, for all
n
large enough, there are no eigenvalues of
M_{n}
in
\Gamma
.

Note that if
\sigma=0
then
\supp(\beta_{0})=\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})\subset\spectrum(T(\bfa))
. In the language of
[
BC16
,
SV21
]
, the region
\spectrum(T(\bfa))^{c}
is stable. The random perturbation
M_{n}
of
T_{n}(\bfa)
has typically no outliers in this region. Our moment condition is probably not optimal: when
U_{n}=I_{n}
, if
\bfa=0
or
\bfa\neq 0
,
\sigma=0
,
\sigma_{n}=n^{-\gamma}
for some
\gamma>0
, then
[
BCGZ22
]
and
[
BZ20
]
prove respectively that Theorem
1.1
holds on the sole condition
(H_{0})
.

1.5
Unstable region

To complement Theorem
1.1
, we are now interested in the individual eigenvalues of
M_{n}
in a compact subsets of
\spectrum T(\bfa)\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
. In the language of
[
BC16
,
SV21
]
, the region
\spectrum(T(\bfa))
is unstable and the study of outliers exhibits many interesting phenomena.

When
U_{n}=I_{n}
,
\sigma=0
,
\sigma_{n}=n^{-\gamma}
for some
\gamma>0
and the entries of
X
satisfy a density assumption, Basak and Zeitouni
[
BZ20
]
proved that, for each
-r\leq\mathfrak{\delta}\leq s
,
\delta\neq 0
, the process of outliers in the region

\mathcal{R}_{0,\sigma}=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1}):\wind(\bfa-z)=\mathfrak{\delta}\right\}\subset\spectrum T(\bfa)\backslash\supp(\beta_{0})

converges to the point process described by the zero set of some random analytic function which differs across the above regions indexed by
\delta
. When
U_{n}=F_{n}
is the Fourier basis,
\sigma>0
and
r=0
,
s=1
,
[
BC16
]
established the convergence of the point process of outliers inside
\spectrum T(\bfa)\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
toward the zero set of a Gaussian analytic function.

In the present paper, we are both interested in the cases
\sigma=0
and
\sigma>0
. We establish the convergence of the point process of outliers of
M_{n}
in
\spectrum T({\bf a})\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
toward the point process of the zeroes of some random analytic function with an explicit correlation kernel. We clarify when this random analytic function will be Gaussian or not. In particular, when
\sigma=0
, in comparison to
[
BZ20
]
, we give an alternative expression for the random analytic function.

Banded Toeplitz matrices as perturbed circulant matrices.

To state our main results in the unstable region, we start with a basic but nevertheless key observation. As in
[
BC16
]
, we see banded Toeplitz matrices as small rank perturbations of normal matrices (that is a matrix with an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors). More precisely, given a row vector
(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, the associated circulant matrix
\text{circ}(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1})
is the
n\times n
matrix whose first row is
(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1})
and such that each row is rotated one element to the right relative to the preceding row, that is:

\text{circ}(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1})=\begin{pmatrix}c_{0}&c_{1}&c_{2}&\cdots&c_{n-1}\\
c_{n-1}&c_{0}&c_{1}&\cdots&c_{n-2}\\
c_{n-2}&c_{n-1}&c_{0}&\cdots&c_{n-3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
c_{1}&c_{2}&c_{3}&\cdots&c_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

Circulant matrices are special cases of (not necessarily banded) Toeplitz matrices, but are much simpler from the viewpoint of spectral theory.
To the Toeplitz matrix
T_{n}(\bfa)
with symbol the Laurent polynomial
\bfa
, is then associated, for
n>r+s
, the circulant matrix
C_{n}(\bfa)
defined by

(6)

C_{n}(\bfa)=\text{circ}(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{s},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{\mathclap{n-(r+s+1)\text{ times}}},a_{-r},\ldots,a_{-2},a_{-1}).

Our study of outliers of noisy banded Toeplitz matrices
is based on the following decomposition of
T_{n}(\bfa)
:
if
n>r+s
, we write

T_{n}(\bfa)=C_{n}(\bfa)+B_{n},

where

B_{n}=-\begin{pmatrix}0_{r\times s}&0_{r\times(n-r-s)}&D_{r}\\
0_{(n-r-s)\times s}&0_{(n-r-s)\times(n-r-s)}&0_{(n-r-s)\times r}\\
E_{s}&0_{s\times(n-r-s)}&0_{s\times r}\end{pmatrix}

with
0_{p\times q}
denoting the
p\times q
zero matrix and

D_{r}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{-r}&a_{-r+1}&\cdots&a_{-1}\\
0&a_{-r}&\cdots&a_{-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&a_{-r}\end{pmatrix},\quad E_{s}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{s}&0&\cdots&0\\
a_{s-1}&a_{s}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&\cdots&a_{s}\end{pmatrix}.

Moreover, we write
B_{n}=-P_{n}Q_{n}
, with
P_{n},Q_{n}^{\top}\in M_{n,r+s}(\mathbb{C})
defined as

(7)

P_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}0_{r\times s}&I_{r}\\
0_{(n-s-r)\times s}&0_{(n-s-r)\times r}\\
E_{s}&0_{s\times r}\end{pmatrix},\quad Q_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0_{s\times(n-s-r)}&0_{s\times r}\\
0_{r\times s}&0_{r\times(n-s-r)}&D_{r}\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly to
M_{n}
, we introduce the random matrix:

(8)

S_{n}=C_{n}(\bfa)+\sigma_{n}Y_{n}.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
outside
\spectrum(S_{n})
and
\spectrum(C_{n}(\bfa))
, these resolvent matrices will play an important role:

(9)

R_{n}(z)=(z-S_{n})^{-1}\quad\text{ and }\quad R^{\prime}_{n}(z)=(z-C_{n}(\bfa))^{-1}.

The following matrices in
M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
will be ubiquitous in formulas. For any
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we set

(10)

\mathcal{F}(\omega)=(\omega^{p-q})_{1\leq p,q\leq r+s}\;,\quad\mathcal{D}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&D_{r}\end{pmatrix}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{E}=\begin{pmatrix}E_{s}&0\\
0&I_{r}\end{pmatrix}.

The master matrix field.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be a bounded open connected set such that
\bar{\Omega}\subset\mathbb{C}\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
, where
\bar{\Omega}
is the closure of
\Omega
. We describe the limiting point processes of zeroes in
\Omega
through a matrix field
W
which is a random variable on the set
\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega)
of analytic functions
\Omega\to M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
equipped with its usual topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets of
\Omega
, see Subsection
7.1
in appendix. The set
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega))
of probability measures on
\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega)
is endowed with the weak topology. If
W
is a random variable in
\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega)
which is square integrable, we define its covariance kernel as the functions
\Omega\times\Omega\to M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
, defined for
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega
by

K(z,z^{\prime})=\E[W(z)\circledast\bar{W}(z^{\prime})]\quad\hbox{ and }\quad K^{\prime}(z,z^{\prime})=\E[W(z)\circledast W(z^{\prime})],

where
A\circledast B
is the tensor product of the matrices unfolded as vectors. That is, in coordinates,

(A\circledast B)_{ij,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=A_{ij}B_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}.

We say that
W
is Gaussian if for all finite subsets
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\}\subset\Omega
, the random variable
(W(z_{1}),\ldots,W(z_{k}))
is a Gaussian vector, seen as an element of
M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})^{k}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2(r+s)k}
. A Gaussian law in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega))
is characterized by its covariance kernel.

If
\Omega\cap\spectrum(S_{n})=\emptyset
, we set for
z\in\Omega
,

(11)

W_{n}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}Q_{n}\left(R_{n}(z)-R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\right)P_{n}.

Otherwise, we set
W_{n}(z)=0
. The law of
(W_{n}(z))_{z\in\Omega}
is an element of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega))
. As we will state in the next paragraph, weak limits of
W_{n}
govern the point processes of eigenvalues in
\Omega
.

A last definition: we say that the sequence of unitary matrices
U_{n}=(U_{ij})
is
flat at the border
if for any fixed integer
i\geq 1
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\max_{j}|U_{ij}|\vee\max_{j}|U_{n+1-i,j}|=0.

For example,
U_{n}=I_{n}
is not flat at the border but
U_{n}=F_{n}
is flat. A sequence of Haar distributed elements on the unitary or orthogonal group is flat at the border with probability one.

We are ready to state our main result (in a weaker form) related to the matrix field
W_{n}(z)
. We recall that if
A,B\in M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
, their tensor product
A\otimes B\in M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
has entry
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
given by

(A\otimes B)_{ij,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=A_{ii^{\prime}}B_{jj^{\prime}}.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume
(H_{0})
and
(H_{k})
for all
k>2
and either
(H_{\text{sym}})
or
U_{n}=F_{n}
defined in (
5
). Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be an open bounded connected set such that
\bar{\Omega}\cap\supp(\beta_{\sigma})=\emptyset
. Then the random variables
W_{n}
is tight in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega))
and any accumulation point
W
is centered and satisfies for all
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega
,

\E[W(z)\circledast\bar{W}(z^{\prime})]=\frac{\mathcal{A}(z,z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}(z,z^{\prime})^{\intercal}}{1-\sigma^{2}\theta(z,z^{\prime})},

with

\theta(z,z^{\prime})=\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{d\omega}{\omega(z-\bfa(\omega))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega))}},

\mathcal{A}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathcal{D}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{-(r+s)}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}(\omega)\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{r+s}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\frac{d\omega}{\omega(z-\bfa(\omega))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega))}}\mathcal{D}^{*},

and

\mathcal{B}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathcal{E}^{*}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\mathcal{F}(\omega)\frac{d\omega}{\omega(z-\bfa(\omega))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega))}}\mathcal{E}.

If
\varrho=0
, we have
\E[W(z)\circledast W(z^{\prime})]=0
. For any
\varrho
, if
U_{n}=F_{n}
with
\varepsilon=1
or if
U_{n}
is in the orthogonal group with
\varepsilon=-1
, we have

\E[W(z)\circledast W(z^{\prime})]=\varrho\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})^{\intercal}}{1-\varrho\sigma^{2}\theta_{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})},

with

\theta_{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})=\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{d\omega}{\omega(z-\bfa(\omega))(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega^{\varepsilon}))},

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathcal{D}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{-(r+s)}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\omega)\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{-\varepsilon(r+s)}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\frac{d\omega}{\omega(z-\bfa(\omega))(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega^{\varepsilon}))}\mathcal{D}^{\intercal},

where
\mathcal{F}_{-1}(\omega)=\mathcal{F}(\omega)
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}(\omega)_{p,q}=\omega^{p+q-2}
,
and

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathcal{E}^{\intercal}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\omega)\frac{d\omega}{\omega(z-\bfa(\omega))(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega^{\varepsilon}))}\mathcal{E},

where
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{-1}(\omega)=\mathcal{F}(\omega)
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}(\omega)_{p,q}=\omega^{2s+2-(p+q)}
.
If
U_{n}
is flat at the border, then any accumulation point is Gaussian (and thus
W_{n}
converges in the above examples). If
U_{n}=I_{n}
then
W_{n}
converges weakly toward an explicit matrix field
W
.

We postpone to Theorem
5.1
for a more precise asymptotic equivalent of
W_{n}(z)
. We will state that

W_{n}(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}P_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)U_{n}X_{n}U_{n}^{*}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)Q_{n}+\sigma_{n}\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}(z)+o(1)=\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}(z)+\sigma_{n}\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}(z)+o(1),

where
\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}
is a tight sequence of Gaussian fields, independent of
X_{n}
with an explicit covariance kernel. In particular if
\sigma=0
only
\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}(z)
survives. If
U_{n}=I_{n}
, this linear term will generically be non-Gaussian (if
X
is non-Gaussian). The condition that
U_{n}
is flat at the border is a sufficient condition to guarantee that
\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}
is asymptotically Gaussian. The examples depicted in Theorem
1.2
are the simplest to consider for computing the limiting covariance kernels
\E[W(z)\circledast W(z^{\prime})^{\intercal}]
. Interestingly, the basis
U_{n}
plays an important role in the asymptotic distribution of
W_{n}
. This is transparent in the expression for
\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}
but it is also the case for the covariance kernel of
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(2)}
when
\varrho\neq 0
where the matrix
U_{n}U_{n}^{\intercal}
appears, see again Theorem
5.1
for more details.

Convergence of outlier eigenvalues.

We now explain the connection between the accumulations points of the matrix field
W_{n}
and the point processes of zeroes. This is the place where the winding number
\delta
plays a central role.

We first need to recall some standard definitions (see
[
HJ13
,
Mne89
,
BJ09a
]
for instance). . For a
n\times n
matrix
A
, we define its
k
-th compound matrix as the
\binom{n}{k}\times\binom{n}{k}
matrix, denoted by
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(A)
, with rows and columns indexed by subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
of cardinal
k
ordered by lexicographic order, and where for
I,J\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
of cardinal
k
, its
(I,J)
-coefficient is given by
\det A(I|J)
, where
A(I|J)
is the submatrix formed from
A
by retaining only those rows indexed by
I
and those columns indexed by
J
(that is the
(I,J)
-minor of
A
). By convention,
\bigwedge\nolimits^{0}(A)=I
, and we have
\bigwedge\nolimits^{n}(A)=\det A
. Note also that
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(A)
is exactly the
k
-th exterior power of
A
.

Now, the
k
-th adjugate of
A
, denoted by
\adj_{k}(A)
is the
\binom{n}{k}\times\binom{n}{k}
matrix whose
(I,J)
-coefficient is given by

\adj_{k}(A)_{I,J}=(-1)^{\sum_{i\in I}i+\sum_{i\in J}j}\det A(J^{c}|I^{c}).

It can be expressed in terms of compound matrices as follows. Let
S
be the
n\times n
sign matrix, given by
S=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,\ldots,(-1)^{n-1})
. Let
\Sigma
be the
\binom{n}{k}\times\binom{n}{k}
exchange matrix, with
1
on the anti-diagonal, and
0
elsewhere:

\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\
&\iddots&\\
1&&\end{pmatrix}.

Then one has

\adj_{k}(A)=\Sigma\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-k}(SAS)^{\intercal}\Sigma.

In particular,
\adj_{0}(A)=\det A
and
\adj_{1}(A)
is the usual adjugate matrix of
A
, that is the transpose of its cofactor matrix. We will notably use the following remark:

\adj_{k}(A)=0
if and only if
\text{rank}(A)\leq n-k-1
.

For
z\notin\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, we set

(12)

\mathcal{H}(z)=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\mathcal{D}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{s}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{-r}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\frac{\mathcal{F}(\omega)}{\omega(z-\bfa(\omega))}d\omega\;\mathcal{E},

where
\mathcal{F}(\omega)
,
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{E}
are defined in (
10
).

For
\sigma\geq 0
and
\delta
relative integer, let

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,\delta}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma}):\wind(\bfa-z)=\delta\}.

The following theorem is our main result on the outliers eigenvalues of
M_{n}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.2
, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be an open bounded connected subset such that
\bar{\Omega}\subset\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,\delta}
for some
\delta\neq 0
. Let
W
be an accumulation point of
W_{n}
such that

\varphi(z)=\Tr\left(\adj_{|\delta|}\left(I_{r+s}+\mathcal{H}(z)\right)\bigwedge\nolimits^{|\delta|}\left(W(z)\right)\right)

is a.s. not the zero function on
\Omega
. Then, along this accumulation sequence, the point process of eigenvalues of
M_{n}
in
\Omega
converges weakly toward the point process of zeroes of
\varphi
. Moreover,
\varphi
is a.s. not the zero function on
\Omega
when
W
is Gaussian or when
U_{n}=I_{n}
and
(H_{\mathrm{ac}})
holds.

We will prove in Proposition
5.2
that
\adj_{|\delta|}\left(I_{r+s}+\mathcal{H}(z)\right)
is non-zero for all
z\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,\delta}
. We emphasize the non-universality of this result, since the random analytic function whose zeroes approximate the outliers of
M_{n}
depends on the distribution of the coefficients of
X_{n}
. Moreover, even when
X_{n}
is a Ginibre matrix, this random analytic function is in general not Gaussian, unless
|\delta|=1
. These phenomena were already uncovered in
[
BZ20
]
.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 1.3:
Eigenvalues (blue dots) of
T_{n}(\bfa)+\sigma\frac{X_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}
for
n=2000
,
\sigma=0.6
and various symbols: (a)
\bfa(t)=t^{2}
, (b)
\bfa(t)=t+2t^{2}+t^{-1}
and (c)
\bfa(t)=-\frac{3}{4}t+\frac{5}{4}it^{2}+\frac{3}{4}t^{3}+\frac{3}{4}t^{-1}-it^{-2}-\frac{3}{4}t^{-3}
. Note that there are no outliers in the bounded region of zero winding number in subfigure (c).

Figure
1.3
shows simulations of eigenvalues of the model
M_{n}
with
\sigma>0
and
U_{n}=I_{n}
. In region with non-zero winding number, some eigenvalues detach from the support of the limiting measure
\beta_{\sigma}
.

1.6
Overview of the proofs

We outline here the proofs of Theorem
1.1
, Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
.

Step 1: Sylvester’s determinant identity.

We fix a compact connected set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
. As shown in
[
BC16
]
, normal matrices have a stable spectrum with respect to noise. As we will see with Proposition
4.1
in Section
4
, it will follow that a.s. for all
n
large enough,
S_{n}
has no eigenvalue in
\Gamma
. On this last event, since
M_{n}=S_{n}+B_{n}=S_{n}-P_{n}Q_{n}
, we may write

\displaystyle\det(z-M_{n})

\displaystyle=\det(z-S_{n})\times\det(I_{n}-B_{n}R_{n}(z))

(13)

\displaystyle=\det(z-S_{n})\times\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n}),

where the second equality follows from the classical Sylvester’s determinant identity: for
A\in M_{p,q}(\mathbb{C})
and
B\in M_{q,p}(\mathbb{C})
,

(14)

\det(I_{p}+AB)=\det(I_{q}+BA).

Since
\det(z-S_{n})\not=0
on
\Gamma
,
z
is an eigenvalue of
M_{n}
if and only if
z
is a zero of the
(r+s)\times(r+s)
determinant

\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n}).

Step 2: Taylor expansion for the determinant.

We thus have to understand the zeroes of the above determinant. This will depend on whether
z
is in
\spectrum T(\bfa)
or not. We introduce the difference matrix

(15)

G_{n}(z)=\frac{\sigma_{n}W_{n}(z)}{\sqrt{n}}=Q_{n}(R_{n}(z)-R^{\prime}_{n}(z))P_{n}.

It follows from Proposition
4.2
below, that a.s.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|G_{n}(z)\|=0.

By expanding the determinant, we have,

(16)

\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n})=\sum_{k=0}^{r+s}\frac{1}{k!}d^{k}\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n})(G_{n}(z),\ldots,G_{n}(z)),

where
d^{k}\det(A)
denotes the
k
-th derivative of the determinant at
A
.
The
k
-th derivative of the determinant can be expressed in terms of compound and higher adjugate matrices (
[
Mne89
,
BJ09b
]
). The determinant of the sum of two
n\times n
matrices
A,B
can be expressed as:

\det(A+B)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\Tr\left(\adj_{k}(A)\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(B)\right),

hence, the above development (
16
) writes

(17)

\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n})=\sum_{k=0}^{r+s}\Tr\left(\adj_{k}\left(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}\right)\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\left(G_{n}(z)\right)\right).

Step 3: Case
\delta=0
and Szegő’s strong limit theorem.

The first term
k=0
of the above development (
17
) is equal to

\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n})=\frac{\det(T_{n}(\bfa)-z)}{\det(C_{n}(\bfa)-z)},

by Sylvester’s identity. Since
\|G_{n}(z)\|
goes to 0, we deduce that, uniformly on
\Gamma
,

\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n})=\frac{\det(T_{n}(\bfa)-z)}{\det(C_{n}(\bfa)-z)}+o(1).

Now, in the region of spectral stability, i.e. if
\Gamma\subset\spectrum T(\bfa)^{c}
, Szegő’s strong limit theorem implies that the above quotient is uniformly lower bounded (we shall also give an alternative proof of this fact in Section
5
). We deduce that
\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n})
has no zeroes in
\Gamma
and this will lead to Theorem
1.1
.

Step 4: case
\delta\neq 0
, identification of the leading term of the Taylor expansion (
17
).

On the other hand, inside
\spectrum T(\bfa)
, one can show that
\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n})
goes to zero exponentially fast as
n
goes to infinity, so that the outliers will be described by higher terms of the development of
\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n})
in (
17
). In the spectral region
\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,\delta}=\{z\notin\supp(\beta_{\sigma}):\mathrm{wind}(\bfa-z)=\delta\}
, we will prove that this leading term corresponds precisely to
k=|\delta|
and get

(18)

\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n})\approx\Tr\left(\adj_{|\delta|}\left(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}\right)\bigwedge\nolimits^{|\delta|}\left(G_{n}(z)\right)\right).

The reason is that
Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}
converges toward
\mathcal{H}(z)
defined in (
12
) exponentially fast (this is Proposition
5.1
) and that the dimension of the kernel of
I+\mathcal{H}(z)
is equal to
|\delta|
. This last claim is the content of Proposition
5.2
and its proof has some subtleties since we do not have a closed-form formula for the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}(z)
when
r,s>0
.

Step 5: convergence of the matrix field.

We will prove that the
\Gamma\to M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
analytic random field
W_{n}(z)=\sqrt{n}G_{n}(z)/\sigma_{n}
defined in (
11
) is closed in distribution to some random field as
n\to\infty
. This is the content of Theorem
5.1
and its corollary, Theorem
1.2
. The proof of this result will be most challenging in the case
\sigma>0
. To prove this, we expand
W_{n}(z)
as

(19)

W_{n}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{n}G_{n}(z)}{\sigma_{n}}=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\sum_{k\geq 1}Q_{n}\left(R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\sigma_{n}Y_{n}\right)^{k}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}.

We will prove the tightness of this expansion and establish a functional central limit theorem using Stein’s method of exchangeable pairs for the process in
(k,z)\mapsto Q_{n}\left(R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\sigma_{n}Y_{n}\right)^{k}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}
. The proof will follow from a general Gaussian approximation result for processes of the type:

(B_{0},B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1})\mapsto n^{-(k-1)/2}\Tr(B_{0}X_{n}B_{1}X_{n}\cdots B_{k-1}X_{n}),

where
B_{t}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
B_{0}
has small rank. This study will be done in Section
6
and it contains the main new probabilistic ingredients of this paper. The proof of tightness for the series (
19
) will notably rely on a known deterministic upper bound on graphical sum-product expressions of the form

\sum_{\bm{i}}\prod_{e\in E}(M_{e})_{i_{e_{-}}i_{e_{+}}},

where
G=(V,E)
is a directed graph,
(M_{e})_{e\in E}
is a collection of matrices of size
n
indexed by the edges of
G
and the sum is over all
\bm{i}=(i_{v})_{v\in V}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{V}
, see Theorem
7.2
in Appendix (this is where the restricted assumption that either
(H_{\text{sym}})
holds or
U_{n}=F_{n}
is used).

Step 6: case
\delta\neq 0
, convergence of the outlier eigenvalues.

Combining (
18
) and the convergence of
W_{n}
toward a limiting matrix field
W
, we will deduce that if
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,\delta}
, we have the following weak convergence of random analytic functions on
\Gamma
:

\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\right)^{|\delta|}\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n})\to\varphi(z),

where
\varphi
is an in Theorem
1.3
:

\varphi(z)=\Tr\left(\adj_{|\delta|}\left(I_{r+s}+\mathcal{H}(z)\right)\bigwedge\nolimits^{|\delta|}\left(W(z)\right)\right).

It follows classically from Montel’s Theorem that, for analytic functions:
\varphi_{n}\to\varphi
and
\varphi\neq 0
imply the convergence of the set
\varphi_{n}^{-1}(\{0\})
toward the set
\varphi^{-1}(\{0\})
(see Proposition
7.3
for the precise statement we use). Consequently, the main remaining difficulty to prove Theorem
1.3
will be to prove that the above random analytic function
\varphi
does not vanish almost surely. This will be done by proving that the covariance kernel
\E[W(z)\circledast\bar{W}(z)]
is positive definite and using a Gaussian comparison trick to lower bound the variance of
\varphi
.

Comparison with other methods.

Our method of proof follows the strategy initiated in
[
BC16
]
. For outliers, it settled the case
\sigma>0
,
r=0
,
s=1
and
U_{n}=F_{n}
. Here we cope with the numerous new challenges outlined above. Other methods to study random Toeplitz matrices in the case
\sigma=0
were introduced in
[
SV21
]
and
[
BZ20
]
. Interestingly,
[
BZ20
]
also relies on the expansion of the determinant but, with our notation, the authors write directly:

\det(M_{n}-z)=\det(T_{n}(\bfa)-z+\sigma_{n}Y_{n})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sigma_{n}^{k}\Tr\left(\adj_{k}(T_{n}(\bfa)-z)\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(Y_{n})\right).

They prove the convergence of this expansion when
\sigma_{n}=n^{-\gamma}
,
\gamma>0
and
U_{n}=I_{n}
. In the case
\sigma>0
, we do not expect the tightness of this expansion anymore. A remarkable advantage of
[
BZ20
]
is that they only assume that
(H_{0})
holds and a density assumption of the law of
X
. A feature of our present method is that it disentangles clearly the algebraic deterministic part governed by
\adj_{|\delta|}\left(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}\right)
and the random matrix part governed by the matrix field
W_{n}(z)
in
M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
. We have not found a simple way to compare the limiting random analytical functions we have obtained (which have the same random set of zeros).

Some perspectives.

The method of proofs are rather general and apply for their main parts when
T_{n}(\bfa)
is replaced by
C_{n}-P_{n}Q_{n}
, a finite rank perturbation of a well-conditioned matrix
C_{n}
(up to the explicit asymptotic computations on the covariance of the matrix field
W_{n}
and the asymptotic properties of the restricted resolvent
Q_{n}(z-C_{n})^{-1}P_{n}
).

This work leaves many interesting directions to further researches such as the study of the left and right eigenvectors of the outlier eigenvalues. Also, the case
\sigma_{n}=\alpha^{n}
,
0<\alpha<1
, seems to be the critical range for the noise to start to interfere with the spectrum of
T_{n}(\bfa)
. In another direction, this work shows that the nature of the noise has an important influence on the outliers. It would be interesting to replace
Y_{n}
with a more structured noise matrix, such as an Hermitian random matrix. We note also that if the rank of the perturbation
P_{n}Q_{n}
, here
r+s
, grows with
n
many arguments of this work do not apply directly, we may mention general Toeplitz matrices as motivating examples fitting in this category. We finally mention the general study of the outlier eigenvalues of a polynomial in deterministic and random matrices.

Organization of the paper.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we recall some facts on Toeplitz matrices. In Section
3
, we study
\beta_{\sigma}
and prove Lemma
1.1
. In Section
4
, we study the stable region and prove Theorem
1.1
. Section
5
is devoted to the proofs of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
.
In Section
6
, we establish a functional central limit theorem for traces of polynomials of random matrices. Finally Section
7
in an appendix which gather known results.

Acknowledgments

We would like to thank Ofer Zeitouni for introducing us to the topic of Toeplitz matrices and for some stimulating discussions.

2
Notation and preliminaries

2.1
Notation and matrix identities

The spectral norm will be denoted
\|\cdot\|
, that is, for
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
,

\|A\|:=\sup_{\|x\|_{2}=1}\|Ax\|_{2},

where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
denotes the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

We will use the following perturbation inequality, which follows from Hadamard’s inequality: for
A,B\in M_{d}(\mathbb{C})
,

(20)

|\det A-\det B|\leq\|A-B\|d\max(\|A\|,\|B\|)^{d-1}.

2.2
Preliminaries on Toeplitz matrices

We first recall some well known facts on banded Toeplitz matrices, which can be found in the book
[
BG05
]
. We start with a useful remark: for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
T_{n}({\bf a})-zI=T_{n}({\bf a}-z)
, which is the Toeplitz matrix with symbol
\lambda\mapsto{\bf a}(\lambda)-z
.

2.2.1
Limiting empirical distribution of the singular values

By the Avram-Parter Theorem, see
[
BG05
, Chapter 9]
, the empirical distribution of the singular values of
T_{n}({\bf a}-z)
converges weakly to the push-forward by the map
t\mapsto|{\bf a}(t)-z|
of the uniform measure on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, that is, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

(21)

\mu_{(T_{n}({\bf a})-z)(T_{n}({\bf a})-z)^{*}}\overset{\text{weakly}}{\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\nu_{z},

where
\nu_{z}
is the push-forward by the map
t\mapsto|{\bf a}(t)-z|^{2}
of the uniform measure on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

2.2.2
Limiting empirical spectral distribution and limiting set of spectra

Let us now recall that the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
T_{n}(\bfa)
converges weakly (see
[
BG05
, Chapter 11]
).
We introduce the following notation for the zeroes of the symbol
{\bf a}-z
:

Definition 2.1
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

{\bf a}(\lambda)-z=\sum_{k=-r}^{s}a_{k}\lambda^{k}-z=\lambda^{-r}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{r+s}a_{k-r}\lambda^{k}-z\lambda^{r}\right)=\lambda^{-r}a_{s}\prod_{k=1}^{r+s}\left(\lambda-\lambda_{k}(z)\right),

where the
\lambda_{k}(z)
are the roots of the polynomial
Q_{z}(\lambda)=\sum_{k=0}^{r+s}a_{k-r}\lambda^{k}-z\lambda^{r}
, counting with multiplicities, and labelled such that:

|\lambda_{1}(z)|\leq\cdots\leq|\lambda_{r}(z)|\leq|\lambda_{r+1}(z)|\leq\cdots\leq|\lambda_{r+s}(z)|.

Remark 2.1
.

Since
{\bf a}-z
has only one pole of order
r
, the winding number
\wind({\bf a}-z)
is given by the number of roots of
Q_{z}
in
\mathbb{D}
minus
r
. In particular, we have
\wind({\bf a}-z)=0
if and only if

|\lambda_{r}(z)|<1<|\lambda_{r+1}(z)|.

Define the set

\Lambda({\bf a})=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\lambda_{r}(z)|=|\lambda_{r+1}(z)|\right\}.

By Schmidt and Spitzer, see
[
SS60
]
or
[
BG05
, Chapter 11]
, the set
\Lambda({\bf a})
is the limiting set of the spectra
\spectrum T_{n}({\bf a})
,
and for any compact set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\Lambda(\bfa)
, there are no eigenvalues of
T_{n}(\bfa)
in
\Gamma
provided that
n
is large enough.

The set
\Lambda({\bf a})
is easily seen to be a compact subset of
\spectrum T(\bfa)
and consists of a finite union of analytic arcs and a finite number of exceptional points. It is a connected set and has two-dimensional Lebesgue measure equal to zero.
If
z\not\in\Lambda(\bfa)
, there exists
\varrho
such that
|\lambda_{r}(z)|<\varrho<|\lambda_{r+1}(z)|
. Consider the perturbed symbol
\bfa_{\varrho}
defined by
\bfa_{\varrho}(t)=\sum_{k=-r}^{s}a_{k}\varrho^{k}t^{k}
and
denote by
g
the function on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Lambda({\bf a})
defined by

g(z)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log|{\bf a}_{\varrho}(e^{i\theta})-z|d\theta\right).

Then the empirical spectral measure of
T_{n}({\bf a})
converges weakly to

\frac{1}{2\pi}\nabla^{2}\log g,

in the distributional sense (where
\nabla^{2}
denotes the Laplacian). Moreover, Hirschman, see
[
HJ67
]
or
[
BG05
, Chapter 11]
), proved that the empirical spectral measure of
T_{n}({\bf a})
converges weakly to a measure supported on
\Lambda(\bfa)
, whose density is given explicitly by a formula involving the boundary behavior of the geometric mean of
\bfa-z
, as
z
approaches the different analytic arcs which constitute
\Lambda(\bfa)
.

Remark 2.2
.

It is interesting to point out that the set
\Lambda(\bfa)
will not play any role in the asymptotic spectral analysis of the matrix
M_{n}
(at least in the regime
\lim_{n}\frac{1}{n}\ln\sigma_{n}=0
that we consider here).

2.2.3
Szegő’s strong limit theorem

Recall now the Szegő’s strong limit theorem. Let
{\bf b}
be the Laurent polynomial
{\bf b}(t)=\sum_{k=-r}^{s}b_{k}t^{k}
and suppose that

{\bf b}(t)\not=0\ \text{for $t\in\mathbb{S}^{1}$ and }\wind{\bf b}=0.

Since
\wind{\bf b}=0
and
{\bf b}
has only one pole of order
r
, we have exactly
r
roots (counting with multiplicities)
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}
of
{\bf b}
with modulus strictly less than 1, and
s
roots
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s}
with modulus strictly greater than 1. Define:

\displaystyle G({\bf b})=b_{s}(-1)^{s}\prod_{j=1}^{s}\mu_{j}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad E({\bf b})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{j=1}^{s}\left(1-\frac{\delta_{i}}{\mu_{j}}\right)^{-1}.

The Szegő’s strong limit theorem
[
BG05
, Thm 2.11]
states that:

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
{\bf b}
as above with
\wind{\bf b}=0
.
Then, as
n\to\infty
,

\det T_{n}({\bf b})=G({\bf b})^{n}E({\bf b})\left(1+O(q^{n})\right),

with some constant
q\in\left(\frac{\max_{i}|\delta_{i}|}{\min_{j}|\mu_{j}|},1\right)
.

Note that
G({\bf b})
is exactly the exponential of the
0
-th Fourier coefficient of
\ln{\bf b}
, that is

G({\bf b})=\exp(\ln{\bf b})_{0}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\ln{\bf b}(e^{i\theta})d\theta\right).

Remark 2.3
.

In Subsection
5.2
and Subsection
5.3
, we will explore variations around Szegő’s strong limit theorem. We will notably give a new proof that
\det T_{n}({\bf b})/G({\bf b})^{n}
converges to a non-zero number if
\wind{\bf b}=0
.

2.2.4
Circulant matrices

According to
[
BG05
, (2.2) Chapter 2]
(note that with our notations we are considering the transpose instead of their matrix),
C_{n}(\bfa)
is diagonalized by a discrete Fourier transform, that is,

(22)

C_{n}(\bfa)=F_{n}\diag(\bfa(\omega_{n}^{l-1})),l=1,\ldots,n)F_{n}^{*},

where
\omega_{n}=e^{\frac{2\mathrm{i}\pi}{n}}
and
F_{n}
was defined in (
5
).

3
Limiting spectral distribution

3.1
Convergence of the empirical spectral distribution

As already pointed, for
\sigma=0
,
\sigma_{n}=n^{-\gamma}
for some
\gamma>0
,
[
BPZ20
]
proved that the empirical spectral measure of
M_{n}
converges weakly, in probability to the law of
\bfa(u)
, with support
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
.

For the rest of the subsection, we focus on the case
\sigma>0
and explain that the empirical spectral measure of
M_{n}
converges weakly, in probability. Denote by
N_{n}
the nilpotent matrix with
(N_{n})_{i,i+1}=1
and zero elsewhere. Then
T_{n}({\bf a})
is the following polynomial in
N_{n}
and
N_{n}^{*}
:

T_{n}({\bf a})=\sum_{k=0}^{s}a_{k}(N_{n})^{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{r}a_{-k}(N_{n}^{*})^{k}.

Since,
N_{n}
converges, as
n\to\infty
, in
∗
-moments to a Haar unitary
u

in some noncommutative probability space
(\mathcal{A},\phi)
, where
\mathcal{A}
is
C^{*}
-algebra and
\phi
a normal faithful tracial state on
\mathcal{A}
,
then
T_{n}({\bf a})
converges in
∗
-moments to

{\bf a}(u)=\sum_{k=0}^{s}a_{k}(u)^{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{r}a_{-k}(u^{*})^{k}.

By Theorem 6 in
[
Ś02
]
, if
G_{n}
is a complex Ginibre random matrix, i.e.
G_{n}(i,j)
are i.i.d. standard complex Gaussian variables, then one has that the empirical distribution of
\sigma_{n}\frac{G_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}+T_{n}({\bf a})
converges weakly almost surely to the Brown measure of
{\bf a}(u)+\sigma c
where
c
and
u
are free noncommutative random variables in
(\mathcal{A},\phi)
, where
c
is a standard circular noncommutative random variable and
u
is Haar distributed (see for instance
[
MS17
]
for a nice introduction to Brown measure in the context of free probability).

Moreover, by the universality principle Theorem 1.5 of Tao-Vu
[
TV10
]
, the same holds for
X_{n}
. Indeed, the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of
T_{n}({\bf a})
satisfies

\|T_{n}({\bf a})\|^{2}_{\text{HS}}:=\Tr(T_{n}({\bf a})T_{n}({\bf a})^{*})\leq n\|T_{n}({\bf a})\|^{2}\leq n\max_{\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}|{\bf a}(\omega)|^{2},

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the spectral norm. Hence,

\sup_{n}\frac{1}{n}\|T_{n}({\bf a})\|^{2}_{\text{HS}}<\infty,

so condition (1.3) of
[
TV10
, Thm 1.5]
is satisfied. Moreover,
since according to (
21
),
\mu_{(T_{n}({\bf a})-z)(T_{n}({\bf a})-z)^{*}}
converges weakly to
\nu_{z}
,
Theorem 1.5 in
[
TV10
]
implies that the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
M_{n}=U^{*}_{n}\sigma_{n}(\frac{X_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}+U_{n}T_{n}({\bf a})U^{*}_{n}/\sigma_{n})U_{n}
converges weakly towards the Brown measure
\beta
of
{\bf a}(u)+\sigma c
, almost surely.

The support and the density of the Brown measure for the free sum of a circular element and a normal operator can be computed using subordination techniques as in
[
BCC14
]
and this has been generalized to free sums of a circular element and an arbitrary operator in
[
Zho21
,
BZ22
]
. From their results, one extracts that in our case, the Brown measure of
\bfa(u)+\sigma c
is supported on the closure of the open set

A=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{|{\bf a}(e^{i\theta})-z|^{2}}>\sigma^{-2}\right\}

and is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
A
with density given by

\displaystyle\varrho(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\Bigg\{\omega(z)^{2}\E\left[\left(|\bfa(U)-z|^{2}+\omega(z)^{2}\right)^{-2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\frac{\left|\E\left[(\bfa(U)-z)\left(|\bfa(U)-z|^{2}+\omega(z)^{2}\right)^{-2}\right]\right|^{2}}{\E\left[\left(|\bfa(U)-z|^{2}+\omega(z)^{2}\right)^{-2}\right]}\Bigg\}

where
U
is uniformly distributed on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and
\omega\colon A\to(0,+\infty)
is the analytic function uniquely determined by

\E\left[\left(|\bfa(U)-z|^{2}+\omega(z)^{2}\right)^{-1}\right]=\sigma^{-2},\quad\text{for any $z\in A$.}

3.2
Proof of Lemma
1.1

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, put

I(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{|\bfa(e^{i\theta})-z|^{2}}.

We have that
I
is continuous and twice differentiable on the open set
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{c}
, and
I(z)=\infty
on
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
.

If
\sigma=0
, then
\beta_{0}
is the distribution of
\bfa(U)
with
U
uniformly distributed on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. In particular, we get

(23)

\supp(\beta_{0})=\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})={\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:I(z)=\infty\right\}}.

The conclusion of the lemma for
\sigma=0
follows.

For
\sigma>0
. as stated above, one has:

\supp(\beta_{\sigma})=\overline{\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:I(z)>\sigma^{-2}\right\}}.

Recall that complex differentiation gives
\partial_{z}z=1
and
\partial_{z}\overline{z}=0
. Hence,

\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}I(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{|\bfa(e^{i\theta})-z|^{4}}.

Therefore, since the Laplacian is
\Delta=4\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}
, we have that
\Delta I>0
on
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{c}
, so
I
is strictly subharmonic on
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{c}
and
I
has no local maximum. Indeed,
let
U
be some open ball in
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{c}
. Since
I
is continuous,
I
attains its maximum at some point
z_{0}\in\overline{U}
. Now suppose by contradiction that
z_{0}\in U
. Then, by Taylor formula, since
\nabla I(z_{0})=0
, one has

I(z_{0}+\xi)=I(z_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{T}H_{I}(z_{0})\xi+o(\|h\|^{2}),

where
H_{I}
denotes the Hessian matrix of
I
.
Hence, the Hessian
H_{I}
is negative semidefinite at
z_{0}
, so its trace is nonpositive. But since
\Delta I(z_{0})=\Tr(H_{I}(z_{0}))
, we arrive at a contradiction.

Let

A=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:I(z)>\sigma^{-2}\}.

Note that
A
is open by Fatou’s lemma. Note also that
A
contains
\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, since
I(z)=+\infty
for
z\in\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
.
Denote by
B
the set

B=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:I(z)\geq\sigma^{-2}\}.

The inclusion
\overline{A}\subset B
easily follows by continuity.
For the reverse inclusion, pick
z_{0}\in\text{Int}(A^{c})
, the interior of
A^{c}
(note that
\text{Int}(A^{c})
is non empty since
I(z)<\sigma^{-2}
for
z
large enough).
Hence, there is an open ball
B(z_{0},\delta)\subset A^{c}
, so for all
z\in B(z_{0},\delta)
,
I(z)\leq\sigma^{-2}
. Assume by contradiction that
z_{0}\not\in B^{c}
, i.e.
I(z_{0})\geq\sigma^{-2}
. Hence,
I(z_{0})=\sigma^{-2}
, so
I
admits a local maximum inside
B(z_{0},\delta)
which is not possible since
I
is strictly subharmonic. Hence,
z_{0}\in B^{c}
, and eventually
\overline{A}=B
proving the lemma for
\sigma>0
∎

We remark that the last identity in (
23
) implies that for all
\sigma\geq 0
.

(24)

\supp(\beta_{\sigma})=a(\mathbb{S}^{1})\cup\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{|\bfa(e^{i\theta})-z|^{2}}\geq\sigma^{-2}\right\}.

4
Stable spectrum

4.1
Resolvent bounds

Recall the definition of
S_{n}=C_{n}(\bfa)+\sigma_{n}Y_{n}
in (
8
). The goal of this subsection is to prove the following propositions about the resolvents of
S_{n}
and
C_{n}(\bfa)
denoted in (
9
) by
R_{n}(z)
and
R^{\prime}_{n}(z)
respectively.

Proposition 4.1
.

Assume that
(H_{4})
holds.
For any
\sigma\geq 0
and any compact set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
, a.s. for all
n
large enough, neither
C_{n}(\bfa)
nor
S_{n}
has eigenvalues in
\Gamma
. Moreover, there exists
C_{\Gamma}>0
such that

(25)

\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\|\leq C_{\Gamma}\,\mbox{\penalty\ and a.s. for all large $n$,\,}\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|R_{n}(z)\|\leq C_{\Gamma}.

As in the introduction, we write
B_{n}=-P_{n}Q_{n}
with
P_{n},Q_{n}
given by (
7
). Outside the spectra of
S_{n}
and
C_{n}(\bfa)
, recall that we have set
G_{n}(z)=Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n}-Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}
in (
15
).

Proposition 4.2
.

Assume that
(H_{4})
holds.
For any
\sigma\geq 0
and any compact set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
,
a.s.
\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|G_{n}(z)\|
converges towards zero as
n
goes to infinity.

Proofs of Proposition
4.1
and Proposition
4.2
.

We distinguish the cases
\sigma=0
and
\sigma>0
. We start with this last case.

Case
\sigma>0
.
We write

S_{n}=\frac{\sigma_{n}}{\sigma}U_{n}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma_{n}}U^{*}_{n}C_{n}(\bfa)U_{n}+\sigma\frac{X_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)U_{n}^{*}:=\frac{\sigma_{n}}{\sigma}U_{n}\tilde{S}_{n}U_{n}^{*}.

Since
\|U_{n}AU_{n}^{*}\|=\|A\|
for any matrix
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, it is enough to prove the statement for
\tilde{S}_{n}
. We may thus assume without loss of generality that
\sigma_{n}=\sigma
and
U_{n}=I_{n}
(in the sequel only unitary invariants properties of
C_{n}(\bfa)
are invoked). Next, we note that

-

the operator norm of
T_{n}({\bf a})
is bounded by
\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}|{\bf a}(\omega)|
so that assumption
(A1)
in
[
BC16
]
is satisfied.

-

For all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
\mu_{(T_{n}({\bf a})-zI_{n})(T_{n}({\bf a})-zI_{n})^{*}}
(and
\mu_{(C_{n}({\bf a})-zI_{n})(C_{n}({\bf a})-zI_{n})^{*}}
) converges weakly to the probability measure
\nu_{z}
defined in (
21
), so that assumption
(A2)
in
[
BC16
]
is satisfied.

-

Formula (
24
) corresponds to assumption
(A3)
in
[
BC16
]
for
\sigma>0
.

-

The matrices
B_{n},P_{n},Q_{n}
are uniformly bounded in
n
so that assumption
(A1^{\prime})
in
[
BC16
]
is satisfied (with
A^{\prime\prime}_{n}=B_{n}
).

-

B_{n}
has a fixed finite rank bounded by
r+s>0
and for any set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
,

C_{n}({\bf a})-zI_{n}
is well conditioned in
\Gamma
in the sense of (A4) of
[
BC16
]
. Indeed, the eigenvalues of the normal matrix
C_{n}({\bf a})
are
\bfa(e^{\mathrm{i}2\pi k/n})
,
k=0,\ldots,n-1
.
Thus, since
{\bf a}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\subset\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
, for any
z
in
\Gamma
, setting
\eta_{z}=\mathrm{d}(z,\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1}))>0
, for all
n
,
C_{n}({\bf a})-zI_{n}
has no singular value in
[0,\eta_{z}]
and
\|R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\|\leq\eta_{z}^{-1}
.

All the assumptions of
[
BC16
, Theorem 1.4]
are satisfied, the first statement of Proposition
4.1
follows from this theorem. The second statement follows from
[
BC16
, Proposition 3.1]
(since, with the notation of this paper
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:0\notin\supp\mu_{z}\}=\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
). As, for Proposition
4.2
, it is
[
BC16
, Proposition 4.2]
applied to our setting.

Case
\sigma=0
.

Using inequality (A.7.2) in
[
BS10
]
and the assumption that
U_{n}
is unitary, we have that

\max_{k}\left|s_{k}\left(S_{n}-zI_{n}\right)-s_{k}(C_{n}(\bfa)-zI_{n})\right|\leq\sigma_{n}\left\|Y_{n}\right\|=\sigma_{n}\left\|\frac{X_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\|,

where for any
n\times n
matrix
B
,
s_{1}(B),\ldots,s_{n}(B)
denote the singular values of
B
in decreasing order.

Now, for any
\varepsilon>0
, a.s. for all large
n
, we have that
\left\|\frac{X_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\|\leq 2+\varepsilon
(see
[
BS10
, Theorem 5.8]
).
Let
\delta_{\Gamma}=\mathrm{d}(\Gamma,\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1}))>0.

Then, a.s., for all large
n
, for any
z\in\Gamma
,
s_{n}\left(C_{n}(\bfa)-zI_{n}\right)\geq\delta_{\Gamma}>0.

We readily deduce that, setting
\eta_{\Gamma}=\delta_{\Gamma}/2>0
, we have
a.s. for all large
n
,

(26)

\inf_{z\in\Gamma}s_{n}\left(S_{n}-zI_{n}\right)\geq\eta_{\Gamma}.

Since
\|R_{n}(z)\|=s_{n}\left(S_{n}-zI_{n}\right)^{-1}
, Proposition
4.1
follows.

From the resolvent identity, we have

G_{n}(z)=Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n}-Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}=Q_{n}R_{n}(z)\sigma_{n}Y_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n},

thus, for any
z\in\Gamma
,

\displaystyle\|G_{n}(z)\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}\|Q_{n}\|\|R_{n}(z)\|\left\|\frac{X_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\|\|R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\|\|P_{n}\|.

From Proposition
4.1
, a.s.
\|R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\|
and
\|R_{n}(z)\|
are uniformly bounded by
C_{\Gamma}
for all
z\in\Gamma
. Recall also that the norms of the matrices
P_{n},Q_{n}
are uniformly bounded in
n
. It implies Proposition
4.2
in the case
\lim_{n}\sigma_{n}=\sigma=0
.
∎

4.2
No outliers outside
\spectrum T(\bfa)
: proof of Theorem
1.1

The proof follows the ideas of
[
BC16
]
and is based on Rouché’s theorem (see
[
Bur79
, Thm 8.18]
). We start by the following key lemma:

Lemma 4.1
.

For any compact
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\spectrum T({\bf a})
,

\liminf_{n}\min_{z\in\Gamma}\left|\frac{\det(T_{n}(\bfa)-z)}{\det(C_{n}(\bfa)-z)}\right|>\frac{1}{2^{rs}2^{r+s+1}}.

Proof.

Let
z\not\in\spectrum T({\bf a})
, that is
z\not\in{\bf a}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and
\wind({\bf a}-z)=0
. Let

G({\bf a}-z)=a_{s}(-1)^{s}\prod_{k=r+1}^{s+r}\lambda_{k}(z),

where the
\lambda_{k}
’s are defined in Definition
2.1
.
Write:

\frac{\det(T_{n}({\bf a})-z)}{\det(C_{n}({\bf a})-z)}=\frac{\det(T_{n}({\bf a})-z)}{G({\bf a}-z)^{n}}\frac{G({\bf a}-z)^{n}}{\det(C_{n}({\bf a})-z)}.

The determinant of the circulant matrix is given by (see
[
BG05
, Prop 2.2]
): for all
n\geq r+s+1
,

(27)

\det\left(C_{n}({\bf a})-z\right)=a_{s}^{n}(-1)^{s(n-1)}\prod_{k=1}^{s+r}\left(1-\lambda_{k}(z)^{n}\right).

Hence,
we have:

\displaystyle\frac{G({\bf a}-z)^{n}}{\det(C_{n}({\bf a})-z)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s}\frac{\prod_{k=r+1}^{s+r}\lambda_{k}(z)^{n}}{\prod_{k=1}^{s+r}\left(1-\lambda_{k}(z)^{n}\right)}=(-1)^{s}\prod_{k=1}^{r}\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{k}(z)^{n}}\prod_{k=r+1}^{s+r}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}(z)^{n}}-1}

According to remark
2.1
, for
k=1,\ldots,r
,
|\lambda_{k}(z)|<1
, hence
|1-\lambda_{k}(z)^{n}|<2
, and for
k=r+1,\ldots,s+r
,
|\lambda_{k}(z)|>1
, hence
\left|1-\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}(z)^{n}}\right|<2
. Thus, for all
n\geq r+s+1
, we have

\left|\frac{G({\bf a}-z)^{n}}{\det(C_{n}({\bf a})-z)}\right|>\frac{1}{2^{r+s}}.

Since
z\not\in a(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and
\wind({\bf a}-z)=0
, one can apply Szegő’s strong limit Theorem
2.1
, hence, as
n\to\infty
,

\frac{\det(T_{n}({\bf a})-z)}{G({\bf a}-z)^{n}}=E({\bf a}-z)\left(1+O(q^{n})\right)

where

E({\bf a}-z)=\prod_{k=1}^{r}\prod_{j=r+1}^{s+r}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{k}(z)}{\lambda_{j}(z)}\right)^{-1}.

Since
\frac{|\lambda_{r}(z)|}{|\lambda_{r+1}(z)|}<q<1
, and
q
depends continuously on
z
(see
[
BG05
, p.268]
), the convergence holds uniformly on compact sets.

Moreover, for all
k=1,\ldots,r
, and for all
j=r+1,\ldots,r+s
, one has

\left|1-\frac{\lambda_{k}(z)}{\lambda_{j}(z)}\right|\leq 1+\frac{|\lambda_{k}(z)|}{|\lambda_{j}(z)|}<2,

hence,

|E({\bf a}-z)|>\frac{1}{2^{rs}}.

Eventually, one gets
that for all compact
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\spectrum T({\bf a})
, and
n
large enough,

\min_{z\in\Gamma}\left|\frac{\det(T_{n}({\bf a})-z)}{\det(C_{n}({\bf a})-z)}\right|>\frac{1}{2^{rs}2^{r+s+1}}.\qed

We are ready to prove Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\backslash(\supp(\beta_{\sigma})\cup\spectrum T(\bfa))
be a compact set. From (
13
) and Proposition
4.1
, a.s. for all
n
large enough, the eigenvalues in
\Gamma
of
M_{n}
are zeroes of the holomorphic function
f_{n}(z)=\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n})
.

Let
g_{n}(z)=\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n})
. We want to apply Rouché’s theorem to the holomorphic functions
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
, see
[
Bur79
, Thm 8.18]
. On the one hand, we have, recalling that

G_{n}(z)=Q_{n}(R_{n}(z)-R^{\prime}_{n}(z))P_{n},

that

\displaystyle|f_{n}(z)-g_{n}(z)|

\displaystyle=|\det[I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n}]-\det[I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}]|

\displaystyle=|\det[I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}+G_{n}(z)]-\det[I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}]|

\displaystyle\leq\|G_{n}(z)\|(r+s)\max(\|I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}\|,\|I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}+G_{n}(z)\|)^{r+s-1},

using the inequality (
20
).
Since, the norms of
P_{n}
and
Q_{n}
are bounded uniformly in
n
, and using Prop.
4.1
,
we have,

\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}\|\leq C_{\Gamma},

for some constant
C_{\Gamma}
depending only on
\Gamma
. Since, by Proposition
4.2
, a.s.,
\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|G_{n}(z)\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, one has that a.s., for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, for all
z\in\Gamma
,

|f_{n}(z)-g_{n}(z)|<\varepsilon.

On the other hand, using again Sylvester’s identity,

(28)

g_{n}(z)=\det(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n})=\frac{\det(z-T_{n}(\bfa))}{\det(z-C_{n}(\bfa))}.

Hence, since
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\spectrum T(\bfa)
, we have that for
n
large enough,

\inf_{z\in\Gamma}\left|\frac{\det(z-T_{n}(\bfa))}{\det(z-C_{n}(\bfa))}\right|>\frac{1}{2^{r+s+rs+1}}

by Lemma
4.1
, so we get that, a.s. there exists
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, for all
z\in\Gamma
,

|f_{n}(z)-g_{n}(z)|<|g_{n}(z)|.

By Rouché’s theorem (we only need the above inequality on the boundary
\partial\Gamma
of
\Gamma
), for
n\geq n_{0}
,
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
have the same number of zeroes in
\Gamma
. Since
g_{n}
has no zeroes in
\Gamma
, we deduce that, a.s., there are no eigenvalues of
M_{n}
in
\Gamma
for
n
large enough.
∎

5
Unstable spectrum

5.1
Convergence of the master matrix field

In this subsection, we give an asymptotic Gaussian equivalent of

W_{n}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}Q_{n}\left(R_{n}(z)-R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\right)P_{n}

defined in Equation (
11
). This result involves a Gaussian process coming from the CLT proved in Section
6
below. We show that
W_{n}
is close to the matrix field
\tilde{W}_{n}
that we define now.

Let
\Gamma
be a connected compact set of
\mathbb{C}\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
with interior
\overset{\circ}{\Gamma}
. We consider
\tilde{W}_{n},\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}
and
\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}
as the random elements of
\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega)
defined as follows. We set for
z\in\Gamma
,

\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}(z)=Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)U_{n}X_{n}U_{n}^{*}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}.

The field
\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}
is Gaussian and independent of
\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}
. Recalling the fancy product
\circledast
introduced for Theorem
1.2
, the covariance kernel of
\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}
is, for
z,z^{\prime}\in\Gamma
,

\E[\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}(z)\circledast\overline{\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}(z^{\prime})}]=\frac{\theta_{n}(z,z^{\prime})}{1-\sigma^{2}_{n}\theta_{n}(z,z^{\prime})}\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})^{\intercal},

with

\theta_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{1}{n}\Tr R^{\prime}_{n}(z)R_{n}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})^{*},

\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)R_{n}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})^{*}Q_{n}^{*}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{B}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=P^{*}_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z^{\prime})^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)P_{n},

and

\E[\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}(z)\circledast\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}(z^{\prime})]=\frac{\varrho^{2}\theta^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})}{1-\varrho\sigma^{2}_{n}\theta^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})^{\intercal},

with

\theta^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{1}{n}\Tr U_{n}^{*}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)U_{n}U_{n}^{\intercal}R_{n}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})^{\intercal}\bar{U}_{n},

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)U_{n}U_{n}^{\intercal}R_{n}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})^{\intercal}Q_{n}^{\intercal}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=P^{\intercal}_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z^{\prime})^{\intercal}\bar{U}_{n}U_{n}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)P_{n}.

The existence of the Gaussian process
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(2)}
will be checked in the proof of Theorem
5.1
below. It is easy to check that
\E[\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}(z)\circledast\overline{\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}(z^{\prime})}]=\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})^{\intercal}
and
\E[\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}(z)\circledast\tilde{W}^{(1)}_{n}(z^{\prime})]=\varrho\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})^{\intercal}
.
Finally, we set

(29)

\tilde{W}_{n}=\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}+\sigma_{n}\tilde{W}_{n}^{(2)}.

From the independence of
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}
and
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(2)}
, we get for
z,z^{\prime}\in\Gamma
,

(30)

\E[\tilde{W}_{n}(z)\circledast\overline{\tilde{W}_{n}(z^{\prime})}]=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})^{\intercal}}{1-\sigma^{2}_{n}\theta_{n}(z,z^{\prime})}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E[\tilde{W}_{n}(z)\circledast\tilde{W}_{n}(z^{\prime})]=\varrho\frac{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})^{\intercal}}{1-\varrho\sigma^{2}_{n}\theta^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})}.

Note that in the case
\sigma=\lim_{n}\sigma_{n}=0
,
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}
and
\tilde{W}_{n}
are close in Lévy-Prokhorov distance. We say in the statement below that
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}
is
asymptotically Gaussian
if the Lévy-Prokhorov distance between the laws of
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}
and of the Gaussian process in
\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\overset{\circ}{\Gamma})
with the same covariance kernel goes to
0
. By independence if
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}
is asymptotically Gaussian then
\tilde{W}_{n}
is also asymptotically Gaussian (with respect to the Gaussian process with covariance kernel (
30
)). Our main result on the matrix field
W_{n}
is the following.

Theorem 5.1
.

Assume
(H_{0})
and
(H_{k})
for all
k>2
and either
(H_{\text{sym}})
or
U_{n}=F_{n}
defined in (
5
). Let
\Gamma
be a compact connected set in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
. The laws
W_{n}
and
\tilde{W}_{n}
are tight in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\overset{\circ}{\Gamma}))
and the Lévy-Prokhorov distance between the laws of
W_{n}
and
\tilde{W}_{n}
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\overset{\circ}{\Gamma}))
goes to zero as
n
goes to infinity. Moreover, if
U_{n}
is flat at the border, then
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}
is asymptotically Gaussian.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We start by proving the existence of
\tilde{W}^{(2)}_{n}
as the realization of a sum of Gaussian processes. Then, we prove the theorem for
\sigma>0
and
\sigma=0
separately.

Step 1: construction of
\tilde{W}_{n}
.

Let
n\geq r+s
. We write
Q^{*}_{n}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r+s})

and
P_{n}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r+s})
, the
u_{i}
’s and the
v_{j}
’s being vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. We define for any
1\leq p,q\leq r+s
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
the sequence of matrices in
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
:

(31)

B_{p,q,z}=\left(U_{n}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)v_{q}u_{p}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)U_{n},\sigma_{n}U_{n}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)U_{n},\sigma_{n}U_{n}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)U_{n},\cdots\right)

and set

{\bf B}=(B_{p,q,z},1\leq p,q\leq r+s,z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mbox{supp}(\beta_{\sigma})).

For integer
k\geq 1
, let
P_{k}
be the set of
\pi=(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})
with
i_{t},j_{t}
in
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Let
\{W_{\pi},\pi\in P_{k},k\geq 1\}
be an infinite set of independent and identically distributed centered Gaussian random variables defined on the same probability space with
\mathbb{E}|W_{\pi}|^{2}=1
and
\mathbb{E}W_{\pi}^{2}=\varrho^{k}
and independent of
X_{n}
.
We consider
W=W_{k}(B)
as the centered Gaussian process, indexed by
k\geq 1
and
B=(B_{0},B_{1},\ldots)\in{\bf B}
defined by

(32)

W_{k}(B)=n^{-(k-1)/2}\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}}W_{\pi}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}(B_{t})_{i_{t}j_{t+1}},

where
i_{0}=i_{k}
. The random variables
W_{k}(B),k\geq 1
are independent centered random analytic functions defined on the same probability space.
We have

\mathbb{E}\left(W_{k}(B_{p,q,z})\overline{W_{l}}(B_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime},z^{\prime}})\right)=\mathbb{E}\left(W_{k}(B_{p,q,z}){W_{l}}(B_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime},z^{\prime}})\right)=0

for
k\neq l
, and for
k=l
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(W_{k}(B_{p,q,z})\overline{W}_{k}(B_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime},z^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma_{n}^{2}\theta_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\right)^{k-1}\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})_{pp^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})_{q^{\prime}q},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(W_{k}(B_{p,q,z}){W_{k}}(B_{p^{\prime},p^{\prime},z^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle=\varrho^{k}\left(\sigma_{n}^{2}\theta^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\right)^{k-1}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})_{pp^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})_{q^{\prime}q}.

Now, for any
z
in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mbox{supp}(\beta_{\sigma})
, when
n
goes to infinity,

f_{n}(z)=\sigma^{2}_{n}\theta_{n}(z,z)=\sigma_{n}^{2}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{1}{|z-\bfa(e^{2i\pi l/n})|^{2}}

converges towards
\sigma^{2}\int_{0}^{1}|z-\bfa(e^{2i\pi\theta})|^{-2}d\theta<1
.
Since
(f_{n})_{n}
is a bounded sequence for the uniform convergence on compact sets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mbox{supp}(\beta_{\sigma})
,
by Montel’s theorem,

(f_{n})_{n}
uniformly converges on any compact set in a connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mbox{supp}(\beta_{\sigma})
.
Thus, for any connected compact set
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mbox{supp}(\beta_{\sigma})
, there exists
0<\varepsilon_{\Gamma}<1
such that for large
n
,

(33)

\sup_{z\in\Gamma}|\sigma^{2}_{n}\theta_{n}(z,z)|<\varepsilon_{\Gamma}.

Then, for any
1\leq p,q\leq r+s
,

(34)

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\mathbb{E}\left(\left|W_{k}(B_{p,q,z})\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\Gamma}v_{q}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)v_{q}u_{p}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)R_{n}^{\prime}(z)^{*}u_{p}\left(\sigma_{n}^{2}\theta_{n}(z,z)\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon_{\Gamma}^{k-1}.

We deduce from Proposition
7.1
that on
\Omega=\overset{\circ}{\Gamma}
,

\tilde{W}_{n}^{(2)}(z)_{pq}=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}W_{k}(B_{p,q,z})

defines a Gaussian process on
\mathcal{H}(\Omega)
. This concludes the proof of the existence of the Gaussian process
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(2)}(z)=(\tilde{W}_{n}^{(2)}(z)_{p,q})
on
\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\Omega)
. Also from Lemma
7.2
, this sequence in tight.

Step 2: Case
\sigma>0
.
We denote by
d_{LP}
the Lévy-Prokhorov distance between probability measures. Let
I
be a finite set and
(z_{i})_{i\in I}
be a collection of complex numbers. Our first goal is to prove the following

(35)

\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{LP}((W_{n}(z_{i}))_{i\in I},(\tilde{W}_{n}(z_{i}))_{i\in I})=0,

in the case
\sigma>0
.
For any
(p,q)\in\{1,\ldots,r+s\}
, any
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mbox{supp}(\beta_{\sigma})
, any
k\geq 1
, set

a_{k}^{(p,q)}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}[Q_{n}\left(R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\sigma_{n}Y_{n}\right)^{k}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}]_{p,q},

and for any
k\geq 2
,

c_{k}^{(p,q)}(z)=W_{k}(B_{p,q,z}),\quad\text{and}\quad c_{1}^{(p,q)}(z)=u_{p}^{*}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)Y_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)v_{q}=[Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\sigma_{n}Y_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}]_{p,q},

where
B_{p,q,z}
is defined in (
31
).

The proof of Proposition 4.3 in
[
BC16
]
(with
A^{\prime}_{n}=U^{*}_{n}C_{n}(\bfa)U_{n}
) implies that there exists
0<\delta<1
and
C>0
such that the sequence of events
(\Omega_{n})_{n}
defined by

(36)

\Omega_{n}:=\left\{\forall N\geq n,\forall k\geq 1,\ \sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|\left(R^{\prime}_{N}(z)\sigma_{N}Y_{N}\right)^{k}\|\leq C(1-\delta)^{k}\right\}

has a probability converging to one when
n
goes to infinity.

On
\Omega_{n}
, we have the series expansion (
19
) and for all
z\in\Gamma
, for any
k\geq 1
and any
(p,q)\in\{1,\ldots,r+s\}^{2}
,

(37)

|a_{k}^{(p,q)}(z)|\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}C(1-\delta)^{k},

using Proposition
4.1
.
We set

k_{n}=\lfloor n^{c}\rfloor,

where
c
is defined by Proposition
6.2
.
We consider a finite set
I
, complex numbers
\alpha_{i}
, complex numbers
z_{i}
in
\Gamma
and
(p_{i},q_{i})\in\{1,\ldots,r+s\}
, all indexed by
I
, and study the convergence of the Lévy distance between the law of

\sum_{i\in I}\Re(\alpha_{i}(W_{n}(z_{i}))_{p_{i}q_{i}})\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}=\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
, with distribution function
F_{n}
,
where
b_{k}=\sum_{i\in I}\Re(\alpha_{i}a_{k}^{(p_{i},q_{i})}(z_{i}))\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}
, and the law of
\sum_{i\in I}\Re(\alpha_{i}\tilde{W}_{n}(z_{i})_{p_{i}q_{i}})\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}=\sum_{k\geq 1}d_{k}
, with distribution function
\tilde{F}_{n}
,
where
d_{k}=\sum_{i\in I}\Re(\alpha_{i}c_{k}^{(p_{i},q_{i})}(z_{i}))\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}
. As
\Omega_{n}
has a probability converging to one, we would deduce that (
35
) holds.

Recall that if
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are two laws on
\mathbb{R}
with distribution functions
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
respectively, the Lévy distance between
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
is defined by:

d_{L}(P_{1},P_{2})=\inf\{\epsilon>0:F_{1}(x-\epsilon)-\epsilon\leq F_{2}(x)\leq F_{1}(x+\epsilon)+\epsilon\;\mbox{for all}\;x\}.

This distance metrizes the convergence in distribution on
\mathbb{R}
.

For any fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
K\geq 2
and
\epsilon>0
,
denoting by
F^{(n)}_{K}
the distribution function of
\sum_{k=1}^{K}b_{k}
, we have that

(38)

1-F^{(n)}_{K}(x+\epsilon)-\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}b_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}>x\right)\leq 1-F^{(n)}_{K}(x-\epsilon)+\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}b_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right)

Denote by
\tilde{F}^{(n)}_{K}
the distribution function of
\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{k}
,
we have similarly that

(39)

1-\tilde{F}^{(n)}_{K}(x+\epsilon)-\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}d_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}d_{k}>x\right)\leq 1-\tilde{F}^{(n)}_{K}(x-\epsilon)+\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}d_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right).

Now,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}b_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\left(|\sum_{k>K}b_{k}|\right)

with
|\sum_{k>K}b_{k}|\leq\sum_{i\in I}|\alpha_{i}|\sum_{k>K}|a_{k}^{(p_{i},q_{i})}(z_{i})|\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}
.
Using Proposition
6.2
and (
33
), for large
n
, if
k_{n}>K
,

(40)

\displaystyle\sum_{K<k\leq k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left(|a_{k}^{(p_{i},q_{i})}(z_{i})|\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{K<k\leq k_{n}}\left\{Ck^{12}\left\{\sigma_{n}^{2}\theta_{n}(z_{i},z_{i})\right\}^{k-1}\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{K<k\leq k_{n}}\left\{Ck^{12}\varepsilon_{\Gamma}^{k-1}\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k>K}Ck^{6}\varepsilon_{\Gamma}^{(k-1)/2}=:\zeta_{K}\rightarrow_{K\rightarrow+\infty}0.

Now, according to (
37
),

(41)

\sum_{k>k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left(|a_{k}^{(p_{i},q_{i})}(z_{i})|\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}\right)\leq C\sqrt{n}(1-\delta_{0})^{k_{n}}

Thus,

(42)

\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}b_{k}|>\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\sum_{i\in I}|\alpha_{i}|\right)\left(\zeta_{K}+C\sqrt{n}(1-\delta_{0})^{k_{n}}\right).

Now,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}d_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\left(|\sum_{k>K}d_{k}|\right)

with
|\sum_{k>K}d_{k}|\leq\sum_{i\in I}|\alpha_{i}|\sum_{k>K}|c_{k}^{(p_{i},q_{i})}(z_{i})|
.
Using
(
34
),
we obtain that for large
n
, for any
i\in I
,

\sum_{k>K}\mathbb{E}\left(|c_{k}^{(p_{i},q_{i})}(z_{i})|\right)\leq C\sum_{k>K}\varepsilon_{\Gamma}^{(k-1)/2}=:\psi_{K}\rightarrow_{K\rightarrow+\infty}0.

Thus for large
n
, for any
K
,

(43)

\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}d_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sum_{i\in I}|\alpha_{i}|\psi_{K}.

Therefore, using (
42
) and (
43
), for any
K
and
n
large enough (independently), we have

\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}b_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\epsilon,\;\mbox{and}\;\mathbb{P}\left(|\sum_{k>K}d_{k}|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\epsilon.

Then, we can deduce from (
38
) and (
39
) that
for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
n_{0}(\epsilon)
and
K_{0}(\epsilon)
such that
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\forall n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon)
,
\forall K\geq K_{0}(\epsilon)
,

F^{(n)}_{K}(x-\epsilon)-\epsilon\leq F_{n}(x)\leq F^{(n)}_{K}(x+\epsilon)+\epsilon,

\tilde{F}^{(n)}_{K}(x-\epsilon)-\epsilon\leq\tilde{F}_{n}(x)\leq\tilde{F}^{(n)}_{K}(x+\epsilon)+\epsilon.

Therefore
\forall n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon)
,
\forall K\geq K_{0}(\epsilon)
,

d_{L}(\sum_{k=1}^{K}b_{k},\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k})\leq\epsilon\quad\text{and}\quad d_{L}(\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{k},\sum_{k\geq 1}d_{k})\leq\epsilon,

and then

\displaystyle d_{L}(\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k},\sum_{k\geq 1}d_{k})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{L}(\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k},\sum_{k=1}^{K}b_{k})+d_{L}(\sum_{k=1}^{K}b_{k},\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{k})+d_{L}(\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{k},\sum_{k\geq 1}d_{k})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\epsilon+d_{L}(\sum_{k=1}^{K}b_{k},\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{k})

Now, fixing
K\geq K_{0}(\epsilon)
, we deduce from Theorem
6.1
that (
35
) holds (using that
d_{L}\leq d_{LP}
).

Moreover, following the lines of (
40
), we obtain that
for large
n
, for any
z\in\Gamma
,

(44)

\sum_{k\leq k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left(|a_{k}^{(i,j)}(z)|\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}\right)\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}Ck^{6}\varepsilon_{\Gamma}^{(k-1)/2}<+\infty

since
\varepsilon_{\Gamma}<1
.
It readily follows from (
19
), (
41
) and (
44
) that
there exists some
C>0
such that, for any
1\leq p,q\leq r+s
,

(45)

\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big|W_{n}(z)_{pq}\Big|\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}\right]\leq C.

From Lemma
7.2
, this implies that
W_{n}
is a tight sequence in
\mathcal{H}_{r+s}(\overset{\circ}{\Gamma})
. It concludes the proof of Theorem
5.1
in the case
\sigma>0
.

Step 3: Case
\sigma=0
.
We define

\tilde{\Omega}_{n}=\left\{\frac{\|X_{n}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\leq 3\right\}.

According to Theorem 5.8 in
[
BS10
]
, this event has a probability converging to one when
n
goes to infinity. If
C_{\Gamma}
is the constant defined in Proposition
4.1
, we consider the event

\Omega_{n}=\tilde{\Omega}_{n}\cap\left\{\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|R_{n}(z)\|\leq C_{\Gamma}\right\}.

This event has a probability converging to one. Our goal is to prove that

(46)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\E[\|W_{n}(z)-\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}(z)\|\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}]=0.

From Lemma
7.2
, this implies that the Lévy-Prokhorov distance between the laws of
W_{n}
and
\tilde{W}_{n}
goes to
0
as
n
goes to infinity.

From the resolvent identity, for any integer
k_{n}\geq 2
and
z\in\Gamma
,

Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n}-Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}-1}Q_{n}\left(R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\sigma_{n}Y_{n}\right)^{k}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}+Q_{n}\left(R^{\prime}_{n}(z)\sigma_{n}Y_{n}\right)^{k_{n}}R_{n}(z)P_{n}.

Therefore, for any
z\in\Gamma
, we have

(47)

W_{n}(z)-\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}(z)=\sum_{k=2}^{k_{n}}w_{k}(z)+\sqrt{n}\sigma_{n}^{k_{n}-1}Q_{n}\left(R^{\prime}_{n}(z)Y_{n}\right)^{k_{n}}R_{n}(z)P_{n},

where

w_{k}(z)=\sqrt{n}\sigma_{n}^{k-1}Q_{n}\left(R^{\prime}_{n}(z)Y_{n}\right)^{k}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}

We set

k_{n}=\lceil\log n\rceil+1.

We first check that the last term on the right-hand side of (
47
) goes to
0
on the event
\Omega_{n}
. Indeed, if
\Omega_{n}
holds, we have, for some constant
C=C(\Gamma)>0
,

\|\sqrt{n}\sigma_{n}^{k_{n}-1}Q_{n}\left(R^{\prime}_{n}(z)Y_{n}\right)^{k_{n}}R_{n}(z)P_{n}\|\leq C\sqrt{n}(C\sigma_{n})^{k_{n}-1}\leq C\sqrt{n}(C\sigma_{n})^{\log n}.

This goes to
0
as
n
goes to infinity uniformly in
z\in\Gamma
.

We next check that the first term on the right-hand side of (
47
) goes to
0
. Using Proposition
6.2
, for large
n
, for any
z\in\Gamma
and
1\leq p,q\leq r+s
, for some constant
C=C(\Gamma)
changing from line to line,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left(|w_{k}(z)_{pq}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{k_{n}}\sigma_{n}^{k-1}\left(Ck^{12}\theta_{n}(z,z)^{k-1}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{k_{n}}Ck^{6}\left(C\sigma_{n}\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(C\sigma_{n}\right)^{k},

where we have used that for all
k\geq 2
,
k^{6}\leq C^{k}
for some
C>0
. For
n
large enough,
\sigma_{n}C<1
, we get

(48)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left(|w_{k}(z)_{pq}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C\sigma_{n}}{1-C\sigma_{n}},

which goes to
0
as
n
goes to infinity. It concludes the proof of (
46
).

Step 4 :
U_{n}
flat at the border.
It remains to check that if
U_{n}
is flat at the corner then
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}(z)
is close to Gaussian for any
z\in\Gamma
. We fix
z\in\Gamma
and for
1\leq p\leq r+s
, we introduce the vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

x_{p}=U_{n}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)^{*}u_{p}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad y_{p}=U_{n}^{*}R_{n}^{\prime}(z)v_{p}.

From Theorem
6.1
, it suffices to check that for all
1\leq p\leq r+s
,
\|x_{p}\|_{\infty}\to 0
and
\|y_{p}\|_{\infty}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. For
1\leq i\leq n
, we write

(x_{p})_{i}=\sum_{j,k}\bar{U}_{ji}\bar{R}^{\prime}_{n}(z)_{kj}(u_{p})_{k}.

We have
(u_{p})_{k}=0
unless
k\in C_{n}=\{1,\ldots,s\}\cup\{n-r+1,\ldots,n\}
and
(u_{p})_{k}
is uniformly bounded. Therefore from Lemma
5.1
below, for some
0<\kappa<1
and
C>0
,

\|x_{p}\|_{\infty}\leq C\sum_{k\in C_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\kappa^{d_{n}(k,j)}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|U_{ji}|,

where
d_{n}(i,j)=\min_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|i-j-kn|
is the distance between
i
and
j
modulo
n
. For integer
h\geq 1
, let
\varepsilon_{h}=\max_{j:d_{n}(j,C_{n})\leq h}\max_{i}|U_{ji}|
. Since
|U_{ij}|\leq 1
and since, for any
l\geq 0
, there are at most two
j
such that
d_{n}(k,j)=l
, we find
