\|x_{p}\|_{\infty}\leq C\sum_{k\in C_{n}}\sum_{l>h}2\kappa^{l}+C\sum_{k\in C_{n}}\sum_{l=0}^{h}2\kappa^{l}\varepsilon_{h}\leq\frac{2C(r+s)}{1-\kappa}\left(\kappa^{h}+\varepsilon_{h}\right).

The assumption that
U_{n}
is flat at the border implies that
\varepsilon_{h_{n}}\to 0
for some sequence
h_{n}\to\infty
. We deduce that
\|x_{p}\|_{\infty}\to 0
. The proof of
\|y_{p}\|_{\infty}\to 0
is identical.
∎

We have used the following probably well-known lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. There exist positive reals
C>0
and
0<\kappa<1
such that for all integers
n\geq 1
,
1\leq i,j\leq n

|R^{\prime}_{n}(z)_{ij}|\leq C\kappa^{d_{n}(i,j)},

where
d_{n}(i,j)=\min_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|i-j-kn|
is the distance between
i
and
j
modulo
n
.

Proof.

We may reproduce a classical argument in
[
EP88
,
BG99
]
for symmetric band matrices. It is standard that there exist
C>0
and
0<\kappa<1
such that for all integer
l\geq 1
, there exists a polynomial
P_{l}
(depending on
\bfa,z
) of degree
l
such that for all
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,

(49)

|(z-\bfa(\omega))^{-1}-P_{l}(\omega)|\leq C\kappa^{l},

(see below). Then we deduce from (
49
) and the spectral theorem that

|R^{\prime}_{n}(z)_{ij}-P_{l}(C_{n}(\bfa))_{ij}|\leq C\kappa^{l},

where
C_{n}(\bfa)
is the circulant matrix. Observe that
P_{l}(C_{n}(\bfa))_{i,j}=0
if
d_{n}(i,j)>(r+s)l
. We take
l=\lceil d_{n}(i,j)/(r+s)\rceil+1
. Adjusting the values of
\kappa
to
\kappa^{1/(r+s)}
and
C
, we deduce the lemma.

It remains to check that (
49
) holds. Up to replace
\bfa
by
\bfa-z
, without loss of generality, assume
z=0
. For
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we write

\bfa(\omega)^{-1}=\frac{\omega^{r}}{\sum_{\ell=-r}^{s}a_{\ell}\omega^{\ell+r}}=\frac{\omega^{r}}{Q(\omega)}.

By assumption
Q(\omega)=\prod_{i}(\omega-\lambda_{i})
with
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
. We may decompose
1/Q
into simple elements. The proof is then contained in the proof of Proposition
7.4
in Appendix.
∎

5.2
Limit of
Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}

We now study the limit of
Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}
when
n
goes to infinity for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. Recall the definitions of
\mathcal{F}(\omega)
,
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{E}
in (
10
) and the definition of
\mathcal{H}(z)
in (
12
).

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a compact subset of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. There exist
C>0
and
0<\kappa<1
(depending on
\bfa
and
\Gamma
) such that for any
z\in\Gamma
, for all
n\geq 1
,

\|Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}-\mathcal{H}(z)\|\leq C\kappa^{n},

Proof.

Recall that the circulant matrix
C_{n}(\bfa)
is diagonalized as

C_{n}(\bfa)=F_{n}\diag(\bfa(\omega_{n}^{i-1})),i=1,\ldots,n)F_{n}^{*},

where
\omega_{n}=e^{\frac{i2\pi}{n}}
and
F_{n}
is the eigenvector matrix given by
F_{i,j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\omega_{n}^{(i-1)(j-1)}
, for
1\leq i,j\leq n
. We decompose the matrix
F_{n}
into block matrices as follows:

(50)

F_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\\
B_{3}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{B}_{1}\\
\tilde{B}_{2}\\
\tilde{B}_{3}\\
\end{pmatrix},

where
B_{1}
and
B_{3}
are respectively the
s\times n
matrix given by the
s
first rows of
F_{n}
and the
r\times n
matrix given by the last
r
rows of
F_{n}
, that is

B_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\omega_{n}^{(i-1)(j-1)}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq s\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}};\quad B_{3}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\omega_{n}^{(n-r+i-1)(j-1)}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}},

and similarly
\tilde{B}_{1}
is the
r
first rows of
F_{n}
and
\tilde{B}_{3}
the
s
last rows:

\tilde{B}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\omega_{n}^{(i-1)(j-1)}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}};\quad\tilde{B}_{3}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\omega_{n}^{(n-s+i-1)(j-1)}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq s\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}},

(
B_{2}
and
\tilde{B}_{2}
will be of no use).

Setting

\Delta_{n}(z)=\diag((z-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{i-1}))^{-1}),i=1,\ldots,n),

it follows from (
22
) that

\displaystyle Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}

\displaystyle=Q_{n}F_{n}\Delta_{n}(z)F_{n}^{*}P_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&D_{r}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{3}^{*}&B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{1}^{*}\\
B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{3}^{*}&B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{1}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}E_{s}&0\\
0&I_{r}\end{pmatrix}.

Hence, computing the limit of
Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}
consists of computing the limit of each
B_{i}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{j}^{*}
, for
i,j=1,3
. One has, for
1\leq p,q\leq s
,

\displaystyle(B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{3}^{*})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega_{n}^{(p-1)(k-1)}\frac{1}{z-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1})}\overline{\omega_{n}^{(n-s+q-1)(k-1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{i2\pi(p-q+s)(k-1)/n}\frac{1}{z-\bfa(e^{i2\pi(k-1)/n})}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q+s)\theta}}{z-\bfa(e^{i\theta})}d\theta,

where one recognizes the limit of a Riemann sum. Proposition
7.4
in Appendix gives an exponential rate of convergence for such Riemann sum. Similarly, one has

\displaystyle(B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{1}^{*})_{p,q}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q)\theta}}{z-\bfa(e^{i\theta})}d\theta,\quad\text{for $1\leq p\leq s,\ 1\leq q\leq r,$}

\displaystyle(B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{3}^{*})_{p,q}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q+s-r)\theta}}{z-\bfa(e^{i\theta})}d\theta,\quad\text{for $1\leq p\leq r,\ 1\leq q\leq s,$}

\displaystyle(B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)\tilde{B}_{1}^{*})_{p,q}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q-r)\theta}}{z-\bfa(e^{i\theta})}d\theta,\quad\text{for $1\leq p\leq r,\ 1\leq q\leq r$}.

The result follows.
∎

5.3
Rank of
I+\mathcal{H}(z)

Our next proposition connects the rank of
I+\mathcal{H}(z)
to the winding number of
\bfa-z
.

Proposition 5.2
.

If
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and
\delta(z)=\wind(\bfa-z)
then

\dim(\ker(I+\mathcal{H}(z)))=|\delta(z)|.

This subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
5.2
. Up to replace
\bfa
by
\bfa-z
, we may assume without loss of generality that
z=0
. For ease of notation, we remove the dependency in
z=0
of all considered quantities (we write
\mathcal{H}
,
\delta
in place of
\mathcal{H}(z)
,
\delta(z)
for example). We set

Q(\omega)=\omega^{r}\bfa(\omega)=\sum_{\ell=-r}^{s}a_{\ell}\omega^{\ell+r}.

We denote by
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r+s}
the roots of
Q
(with multiplicities) and order them by non-decreasing modulus,
|\lambda_{1}|\leq|\lambda_{2}|\leq\cdots\leq|\lambda_{r+s}|
. The number of roots in
\mathbb{D}
is denoted by
J
:
|\lambda_{J}|<1<|\lambda_{J+1}|
. By definition, we have

\delta=J-r.

We start the proof by a two technical lemmas when all roots of
Q
are simple (i.e. all
\lambda_{i}
’s are distinct). We introduce the following vectors and matrices. For
j\geq 1
, we set

f_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{j}\\
D_{r}\psi_{j}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{r+s},

where
(\phi_{j})_{p}=\lambda_{j}^{r+p-1}
,
1\leq p\leq s
,
(\psi_{j})_{p}=\lambda_{j}^{p-1}
,
1\leq p\leq r
. We also define the vector in
\mathbb{C}^{r+s}
,
(g_{j})_{p}=\lambda_{j}^{s-p}
. Finally for
r\geq 1
, the matrix
\mathcal{T}\in M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
is the upper-triangular matrix

\mathcal{T}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&D_{r}T\end{pmatrix}

with
T_{pq}=0
for
1\leq q<p\leq r
and
T_{pq}=1/(q-p)!\cdot(1/Q)^{(q-p)}(0)
for
1\leq p\leq q\leq r
where
(1/Q)^{(0)}=1/Q
and for
i\geq 1
,
(1/Q)^{(i)}
is the
i
-th derivative of
1/Q
. Note that since
a_{-r}\neq 0
,
0
is not a pole of
1/Q
. If
r=0
, then we simply set
\mathcal{T}=0
.

Lemma 5.2
.

If all roots of
Q
are simple then

\mathcal{H}=-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{Q^{\prime}(\lambda_{j})}f_{j}g_{j}^{\intercal}\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{T}.

Proof.

We have

\frac{1}{\omega\bfa(\omega)}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{s}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{-r}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}(\omega)=\frac{1}{Q(\omega)}{\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{s+r-1}&\cdots&1\\
\omega^{s+r}&\cdots&\omega\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\omega^{2s+r-2}&\cdots&\omega^{s-1}\\
\omega^{s-1}&\ldots&\omega^{-r}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\omega^{s+r-2}&\ldots&\omega^{-1}\end{pmatrix}}.

Observe that the only coefficients of the above matrix with elements of the form
\omega^{p}
with
p<0
are in the upper triangular part of the bottom left
r\times r
corner.
On the other end, since all roots of
Q
are simple, from Cauchy’s Residue Theorem, we have for integer
p\geq 0
,

\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{\omega^{p}}{Q(\omega)}d\omega=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{p}}{Q^{\prime}(\lambda_{j})}

while for
p<0
,

\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{\omega^{p}}{Q(\omega)}d\omega=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{p}}{Q^{\prime}(\lambda_{j})}+\frac{1}{(-p-1)!}\left(\frac{1}{Q}\right)^{(-p-1)}(0).

It follows that

\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{1}{\omega\bfa(\omega)}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{s}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{-r}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}(\omega)=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{r-1}}{Q^{\prime}(\lambda_{j})}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{j}^{s}I_{s}&0\\
0&\lambda_{j}^{-r}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}(\lambda_{j})+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&T\end{pmatrix}.

Consequently,

\mathcal{H}=-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{r-1}}{Q^{\prime}(\lambda_{j})}\mathcal{D}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{j}^{s}I_{s}&0\\
0&\lambda_{j}^{-r}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}(\lambda_{j})\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{T}.

We next use that
\mathcal{F}(\omega)
is a rank one matrix:
\mathcal{F}(\omega)=a(\omega)b^{\intercal}(\omega)
with
a(\omega)_{p}=\omega^{p}
and
b(\omega)_{p}=\omega^{-p}
. It is then easy to check that

\lambda_{j}^{r-1}\mathcal{D}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{j}^{s}I_{s}&0\\
0&\lambda_{j}^{-r}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}(\lambda_{j})=f_{j}g_{j}^{\intercal}.

The conclusion follows.
∎

We next define for
1\leq j\leq r+s
,

\tilde{f}_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{j}\\
-D_{r}\psi_{j}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{r+s},

Since all
\lambda_{j}
’s are distinct, note that the vectors
\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{j}\\
\psi_{j}\end{pmatrix}
,
1\leq j\leq r+s
, classically form a basis of
\mathbb{C}^{r+s}
. Hence since
\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&\pm D_{r}\end{pmatrix}
is invertible,
(f_{j})_{j}
and
(\tilde{f}_{j})_{j}
are two other basis of
\mathbb{C}^{r+s}
.

The next lemma asserts that the family of vectors
(g_{j})
and
\mathcal{E}\tilde{f}_{j}
are bi-orthogonal.

Lemma 5.3
.

If all roots of
Q
are simple then for all
1\leq i,j\leq r+s
,

g_{j}^{\intercal}\mathcal{E}\tilde{f}_{i}=\delta_{ij}Q^{\prime}(\lambda_{j}).

Proof.

For
1\leq q\leq r
, we have

(D_{r}\psi_{i})_{q}=\sum_{p\geq q}a_{-r+p-q}(\lambda_{i})^{p-1}=\lambda_{i}^{r}\sum_{\ell=-r}^{-q}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell+q-1}.

Similarly, for
1\leq q\leq s
,

(E_{s}\phi_{i})_{q}=\lambda_{i}^{r}\sum_{\ell=s-q+1}^{s}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-s+q-1}.

We get

\displaystyle g_{j}^{\intercal}\mathcal{E}\tilde{f}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{q=1}^{s}\lambda_{j}^{-q+s}(E_{s}\phi_{i})_{q}-\sum_{q=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}^{-q}(D_{r}\psi_{i})_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{r}\left(\sum_{q=1}^{s}\lambda_{j}^{s-q}\sum_{\ell=s-q+1}^{s}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-s+q-1}-\sum_{q=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}^{-q}\sum_{\ell=-r}^{-q}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell+q-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{r}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{s}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-1}\sum_{q=s-\ell+1}^{s}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{i}}\right)^{s-q}-\sum_{\ell=-r}^{-1}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-1}\sum_{q=1}^{-\ell}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}}\right)^{q}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{r}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{s}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{i}}\right)^{k}-\sum_{\ell=-r}^{-1}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-1}\sum_{k=1}^{-\ell}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}}\right)^{k}\right).

If
i\neq j
, we thus find

\displaystyle g_{j}^{\intercal}\mathcal{E}\tilde{f}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{r}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{s}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-1}\frac{1-\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{i}}\right)^{\ell}}{1-\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{i}}}-\sum_{\ell=-r}^{-1}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-1}\frac{\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}}-\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}}\right)^{-\ell+1}}{1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{i}^{r}}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{s}a_{\ell}(\lambda_{i}^{\ell}-\lambda_{j}^{\ell})+\sum_{\ell=-r}^{-1}a_{\ell}(\lambda_{i}^{\ell}-\lambda_{j}^{\ell})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{r}\frac{\bfa(\lambda_{i})-\bfa(\lambda_{j})}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}.

Since
Q(\lambda_{i})=Q(\lambda_{j})=0
, the claim follows. Similarly, for
i=j
, we get from what precedes

\displaystyle g_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{E}\tilde{f}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{r}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{s}\ell a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-1}-\sum_{\ell=-r}^{-1}(-\ell)a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=-r}^{s}\ell a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell+r-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}(\lambda_{i}),

where at the third line, we have used that
Q(\lambda_{i})=\sum_{\ell}a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell+r}=0
so that
Q^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})=\sum_{\ell}(\ell+r)a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell+r-1}=\sum_{\ell}\ell a_{\ell}\lambda_{i}^{\ell+r-1}
. It concludes the proof.
∎

We are ready to prove Proposition
5.2
.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

We shall first prove Proposition
5.2
when
\delta\geq 0
and all roots simple. Then extend to the case of roots with arbitrary multiplicities and finally to
\delta<0
.

Case 1: simple roots, non-negative winding number.
We assume here that all roots of
Q
are simple and
J=\delta+r\geq 0
.

Let
(e_{p})_{1\leq p\leq r+s}
be the canonical basis of
\mathbb{C}^{r+s}
,
\Pi_{+}
be the orthogonal projection onto the vector space
(e_{1}\ldots,e_{s})
and
\Pi_{-}=I-\Pi_{+}
the orthogonal projection onto the vector space
(e_{r+1}\ldots,e_{r+s})
(if
r>0
).

For
x\in\mathbb{C}^{r+s}
, we write

x=\sum_{j=1}^{r+s}x_{j}\tilde{f}_{j}.

If
x\in\ker(I+\mathcal{H})
, we get from Lemma
5.2
and Lemma
5.3
, that

(51)

0=(I+\mathcal{H})x=\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{j}(\tilde{f}_{j}-f_{j})+\sum_{j=J+1}^{r+s}x_{j}\tilde{f}_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{r+s}x_{j}\mathcal{T}\tilde{f}_{j}.

We first compose (
51
) by
\Pi_{+}
. Since the image of
\Pi_{+}\mathcal{T}=0
and
\Pi_{+}(\tilde{f}_{j}-f_{j})=0
, we find in
\mathbb{C}^{s}
that,

0=\sum_{j=J+1}^{r+s}x_{j}\Pi_{+}\tilde{f}_{j}=\sum_{j=J+1}^{r+s}x_{j}\phi_{j}.

Remark that
(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{s})
forms a basis of
\mathbb{C}^{s}
and that, since
J\geq r
, we have
r+s-J\leq s
. It follows that if
x\in\ker(I+\mathcal{H})
then
x_{j}=0
for all
j>J
.

If
r>0
, we next compose (
51
) by
\Pi_{-}
. Since
\Pi_{-}(\tilde{f}_{j}-f_{j})=-2D_{r}\psi_{j}
, we find in
\mathbb{C}^{r}
,

0=-2\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{j}D_{r}\psi_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{j}D_{r}TD_{r}\psi_{j}.

We use that
D_{r}
is invertible (
D_{r}
is upper triangular and
D_{pp}=a_{-r}\neq 0
), we deduce that the above equation is equivalent to

0=\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{j}(2I-TD_{r})\psi_{j}.

We observe that
TD_{r}
is upper triangular with
(TD_{r})_{pp}=1
. In particular
2I-TD_{r}
is invertible and the above equation is equivalent to

0=\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{j}\psi_{j}.

Finally, since any subset of the
\psi_{j}
’s of cardinality
r
forms a basis of
\mathbb{C}^{r}
, we deduce that
\dim(\ker(I+\mathcal{H}))=J-r
as requested. If
r=0
, we also find
\dim(\ker(I+\mathcal{H}))=J
since
f_{j}=\tilde{f}_{j}=\phi_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq s
forms a basis of eigenvectors of
I+\mathcal{H}
.

Case 2: general multiplicities, non-negative winding number.
We still assume that
\delta=J-r\geq 0
. If
Q
has roots with arbitrary multiplicities, it should be possible to adapt the above Lemma
5.2
and Lemma
5.3
to a general polynomial
Q
but the computations with Cauchy’s residue formula seems daunting. Alternatively, the strategy of the proof in the general case consists in using that the set of polynomials with simple roots is dense among all polynomials. It however requires some care since the dimension of the kernel is not a continuous function of the entries of a matrix.

Before entering in the technical details, we describe the strategy. Assume that we are given polynomials
Q_{\varepsilon}=a_{s}\prod_{i=1}^{r+s}(\omega-\lambda_{i}(\varepsilon))
with simple roots such that
(\lambda_{1}(\varepsilon),\ldots,\lambda_{r+s}(\varepsilon))
converges to
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r+s})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then the associated matrices
E(\varepsilon)
,
D(\varepsilon)
,
\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon)
, converges to
E
,
D
,
\mathcal{H}
. Also, if
r>0
,
\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)
converges toward the matrix
\mathcal{T}
, since
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
in this case. Assume further that there are vectors,
1\leq i\leq r+s
,

f^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)=\begin{pmatrix}\phi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)\\
D(\varepsilon)\psi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)\end{pmatrix},\quad f^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi^{\prime}_{i}\\
D\psi^{\prime}_{i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)=\begin{pmatrix}\phi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)\\
-D(\varepsilon)\psi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)\end{pmatrix},\quad f^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi^{\prime}_{i}\\
-D\psi^{\prime}_{i}\end{pmatrix},

such that
(\tilde{f}_{i})_{i}
is a basis of
\mathbb{C}^{r+s}
,
\phi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)
converges to
\phi^{\prime}_{i}
,
\psi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)
converges to
\psi^{\prime}_{i}
,
(\phi^{\prime}_{i})_{i>J}
free in
\mathbb{C}^{s}
, any subset of the
\psi^{\prime}_{i}
’s of cardinality
r
forms a basis of
\mathbb{C}^{r}
and finally

(52)

\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon)\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)=\mathbbm{1}_{i\leq J}f^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)-\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon).

Then we claim that all these assumptions imply that

\dim(\ker(I+\mathcal{H}))=J-r.

Indeed, since all matrices and vectors converge, our assumptions imply that

\mathcal{H}\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{i\leq J}f^{\prime}_{i}-\mathcal{T}\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{i},

We may then reproduce the argument given in case 1.

Our task is thus to construct this polynomial
Q_{\varepsilon}
and these converging vectors
\phi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)
and
\psi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)
. For
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varepsilon>0
, we define
\phi_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{C}^{s}
by
(\phi_{\lambda}(\varepsilon))_{p}=(\lambda+\varepsilon)^{r+p-1}
. Similarly
\psi_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
is defined by
\psi_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)_{p}=(\lambda+\varepsilon)^{p-1}
. By construction, we have
\psi_{\lambda}=\psi_{\lambda}(0)
and
\phi_{\lambda}=\phi_{\lambda}(0)
.
For integer
m\geq 1
, we set
\phi^{1,m}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)=\phi_{\lambda}((m-1)\varepsilon)
and
\psi^{1,m}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)=\psi_{\lambda}((m-1)\varepsilon)
. By recursion on
\ell\geq 2
, we define

\psi^{\ell,m}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)=\frac{\psi^{\ell-1,m+1}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)-\psi^{\ell-1,m}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\phi^{\ell,m}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)=\frac{\phi^{\ell-1,m+1}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)-\phi^{\ell-1,m}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}

We denote by
\Phi_{\lambda}^{m,1}(\varepsilon)
,
\Phi_{\lambda}^{1,m}(\varepsilon)
,
\Psi_{\lambda}^{m,1}(\varepsilon)
and
\Psi_{\lambda}^{1,m}(\varepsilon)
the matrices whose columns are the vectors, for
1\leq k\leq m
,
\phi_{\lambda}^{k,1}(\varepsilon),\phi_{\lambda}^{1,k}(\varepsilon)
,
\psi_{\lambda}^{k,1}(\varepsilon)
and
\psi_{\lambda}^{1,k}(\varepsilon)
respectively. By construction,

\Phi_{\lambda}^{m,1}(\varepsilon)=\Phi_{\lambda}^{1,m}T_{m}(\varepsilon)\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\Psi_{\lambda}^{m,1}(\varepsilon)=\Psi_{\lambda}^{1,m}T_{m}(\varepsilon),

where
T_{m}\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
is an upper triangular matrix with coefficients of the diagonal
(1,\varepsilon^{-1},\ldots,\varepsilon^{-m+1})
. In particular
T_{m}(\varepsilon)
is invertible and the vector space spanned by
(\phi_{\lambda}^{1,1}(\varepsilon),\ldots,\phi_{\lambda}^{m,1}(\varepsilon))
is equal to the vector space spanned by
(\phi_{\lambda}^{1,1}(\varepsilon),\ldots,\phi_{\lambda}^{1,m}(\varepsilon))
. The same comment holds for the
\psi_{\lambda}
’s.

We also have, by recursion on
m
, that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\phi_{\lambda}^{m,1}(\varepsilon)=\phi_{\lambda}^{m}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\psi_{\lambda}^{m,1}(\varepsilon)=\psi_{\lambda}^{m},

where for
1\leq p\leq s
,
(\phi_{\lambda}^{m})_{p}=(r+p-1)_{m-1}\lambda^{r+p-m}
, for
1\leq p\leq r
,
(\psi_{\lambda}^{m})_{p}=(p-1)_{m-1}\lambda^{p-m}
and we have used the Pochhammer symbol:
(k)_{0}=1
,
(k)_{l}=k(k-1)\ldots(k-l+1)
for integer
l\geq 1
. The matrix
\Psi_{\lambda}^{m}
whose column are
(\psi_{\lambda}^{1},\ldots,\psi_{\lambda}^{m})
is lower triangular. Hence, we get the key property, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\dim(\SPAN(\psi^{1}_{\lambda},\ldots,\psi^{m}_{\lambda}))=\min(m,s)=\dim(\SPAN(\psi^{1,m}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon),\ldots,\psi^{1,m}_{\lambda}(\varepsilon))),

and similarly for the
\phi_{\lambda}^{\prime}
s.

We are ready to define the vectors
\phi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)
and
\psi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)
. Let
d\geq 1
be the number of distinct roots of
Q
,
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})
be those roots and denote by
m_{j}
the multiplicity of
\lambda_{j}
. For
\varepsilon>0
, we consider the polynomial
Q_{\varepsilon}
of degree
r+s
with leading coefficient
a_{s}
and whose roots are
(\lambda_{j}+(k-1)\varepsilon)
,
1\leq j\leq d,1\leq k\leq m_{j}
. We reorder these roots as
(\lambda_{i}(\varepsilon))_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq r+s
by non-decreasing magnitude. For
\varepsilon
large enough, all roots
\lambda_{i}(\varepsilon)
are distinct and there are precisely
J
roots in the unit disc
\mathbb{D}
. We consider the vectors
\phi^{\prime}_{j,k}(\varepsilon)=\phi_{\lambda_{j}}^{k}(\varepsilon)
,
\psi^{\prime}_{j,k}(\varepsilon)=\psi_{\lambda_{j}}^{k}(\varepsilon)
,
1\leq k\leq m_{j}
. We re-order them to get vectors
\phi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)
and
\psi^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon)
,
1\leq i\leq r+s
. We can check that all required properties are satisfied. Indeed, (
52
) follows from Lemma
5.2
and Lemma
5.3
, and all other properties have been discussed above.

Case 3: negative winding number.
We now assume that
J<r
. In this case, we may reverse the sense of integration: more precisely, we write

\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{\omega^{p}}{Q(\omega)}d\omega=\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{\omega^{-p}}{Q(\omega^{-1})}d\omega=\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{\omega^{r+s-p}}{\widehat{Q}(\omega)}d\omega,

where
\widehat{Q}(\omega)=\omega^{r+s}Q(\omega^{-1})
is the reciprocal polynomial of
Q
whose roots are
(\lambda_{j}^{-1})_{j}
. We may thus perform the same analysis of
I+\mathcal{H}
with
Q
replaced by
\widehat{Q}
,
J
replaced by
\widehat{J}=r+s-J
,
(r,s)
replaced by
(\widehat{r},\widehat{s})=(s,r)
. In particular, since
J<r
, we have
\hat{J}>\widehat{r}
. It thus suffices to reproduces the same argument to conclude. (At the level of matrices it amounts to replace
\mathcal{H}
by its transpose
\mathcal{H}^{\intercal}
).
∎

5.4
Convergence of the covariance kernel

In this subsection, we perform some computations on the kernel of
\tilde{W}_{n}
which appears in Theorem
5.1
. Recall the definitions of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\theta_{n}
and their analogs with a prime defined in Subsection
5.1
. Recall finally, the definitions of
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\theta
in Theorem
1.2
. We gather these computations in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\Gamma
be a compact subset of
\mathbb{C}\backslash\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. Uniformly over all
z,z^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, we have the following convergence:

\lim_{n}\theta_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=\theta(z,z^{\prime})\;,\quad\lim_{n}\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}(z,z^{\prime})\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\lim_{n}\mathcal{B}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathcal{B}(z,z).

If
U_{n}=F_{n}
with
\varepsilon=1
or if
U_{n}
is in the orthogonal group with
\varepsilon=-1
, then

\lim_{n}\theta^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=\theta_{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})\;,\quad\lim_{n}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\lim_{n}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(z,z).

Proof.

The proofs are entirely similar to the proof of Proposition
5.1
. We only give the proofs of the convergence to
\mathcal{A}(z,z^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{A}_{-1}(z,z^{\prime})
. We start with the convergence of
\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})
. Using the notation of Proposition
5.1
, we write
R^{\prime}_{n}(z)=F_{n}\Delta_{n}(z)F_{n}^{*}
, we find

\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=Q_{n}F_{n}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})F_{n}^{*}Q_{n}^{*}=\mathcal{D}\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{1}^{*}&B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{3}^{*}\\
B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{1}^{*}&B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{3}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}^{*},

where
B_{1},B_{3}
are as in (
50
). We may then argue as in Proposition
5.1
. For
1\leq p,q\leq s
,

\displaystyle(B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{1}^{*})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega_{n}^{(p-1)(k-1)}\frac{1}{(z-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1}))}}\omega_{n}^{-(q-1)(k-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{i2\pi(p-q)(k-1)/n}\frac{1}{(z-\bfa(e^{i2\pi(k-1)/n}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(e^{i2\pi(k-1)/n}))}}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q)\theta}}{(z-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))}}d\theta.

Similarly, for
1\leq p\leq s
,
1\leq q\leq r
,

\displaystyle(B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{3}^{*})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega_{n}^{(p-1)(k-1)}\frac{1}{(z-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1}))}}\omega_{n}^{-(n-r+q-1)(k-1)}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q+r)\theta}}{(z-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))}}d\theta.

For
1\leq p\leq r
,
1\leq q\leq s
,

\displaystyle(B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{1}^{*})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega_{n}^{(n-r+p-1)(k-1)}\frac{1}{(z-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1}))}}\omega_{n}^{-(q-1)(k-1)}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q-r)\theta}}{(z-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))}}d\theta.

Finally, for
1\leq p,q\leq r
,

\displaystyle(B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{3}^{*})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega_{n}^{(n-r+p-1)(k-1)}\frac{1}{(z-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1}))}}\omega_{n}^{-(n-r+q-1)(k-1)}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q)\theta}}{(z-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))\overline{(z^{\prime}-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))}}d\theta.

We deduce easily that
\mathcal{A}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})\to\mathcal{A}(z,z^{\prime})
.

We next suppose that
U_{n}
is orthogonal, that is
U_{n}U_{n}^{\intercal}=I_{n}
. We prove the convergence of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z)
in this case. We observe that

F_{n}F_{n}^{\intercal}=F_{n}^{\intercal}F_{n}=K_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&\\
&&&1\\
&&\iddots&\\
&1&&\end{pmatrix}.

That is,
(F_{n}F_{n}^{\intercal})_{pq}=\mathbbm{1}(p+q-2=0\;[n])
. Since
U_{n}U_{n}^{\intercal}=I_{n}
and
F_{n}F_{n}^{\intercal}
is a real matrix, we get

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(z,z^{\prime})=Q_{n}F_{n}\Delta_{n}(z)K_{n}\Delta_{n}(z^{\prime})F^{\intercal}_{n}Q_{n}^{\intercal}=\mathcal{D}\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)K_{n}\Delta_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{1}^{\intercal}&B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)K_{n}\Delta_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{3}^{\intercal}\\
B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)K_{n}\Delta_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{1}^{\intercal}&B_{3}\Delta_{n}(z)K_{n}\Delta_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{3}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}^{\intercal}.

We note that

\displaystyle(B_{1}\Delta_{n}(z)K_{n}\Delta_{n}(z^{\prime})B_{1}^{\intercal})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega_{n}^{(p-1)(k-1)}\frac{1}{(z-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1})){(z^{\prime}-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{n-k+2-1}))}}\omega_{n}^{(q-1)(n-k+2-1)}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i(p-q)\theta}}{(z-\bfa(e^{i\theta}))(z^{\prime}-\bfa(e^{-i\theta}))}d\theta.

The rest of the proof repeats similar computations.
∎

We conclude this subsection with a lemma on the rank of the covariance kernel.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
, the matrix
\mathcal{A}(z,z)\otimes\mathcal{B}(z,z)^{\intercal}/(1-\sigma^{2}\theta(z,z))
is positive-definite.

Proof.

Since
z\notin\supp(\beta_{\sigma})
, we have
\sigma^{2}\theta(z,z)<1
. We need to prove that
\mathcal{A}(z,z)\otimes\mathcal{B}(z,z)^{\intercal}
is invertible. Since the rank is multiplicative under tensor product, we need to prove that
\mathcal{A}(z,z)
and
\mathcal{B}(z,z)
are invertible. We prove the claim for
\mathcal{A}(z,z)
. Since
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{E}
are invertible is suffices to prove that

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(z)=\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{-(r+s)}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}(\omega)\begin{pmatrix}I_{s}&0\\
0&\omega^{r+s}I_{r}\end{pmatrix}\frac{d\omega}{\omega|z-\bfa(\omega)|^{2}}

is invertible.
Let
f\in\mathbb{C}^{r+s}
, we write

f=\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{pmatrix}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad f(\omega)=\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
\omega^{r+s}f_{2}\end{pmatrix}

with
f_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{s}
and
f_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
. Observe that
\mathcal{F}(\omega)=\varphi(\omega)\varphi(\omega)^{*}
with
\varphi(\omega)_{p}=\omega^{p}
. Let
C=1/\mathrm{dist}(z,\bfa(\mathbb{S}^{1}))^{2}
, we get

\displaystyle\langle f,\mathcal{K}f\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{|\langle\varphi(e^{i\theta}),f(e^{i\theta})\rangle|^{2}}{|z-\bfa(e^{i\theta})|^{2}}d\theta

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\langle\varphi(e^{i\theta}),f(e^{i\theta})\rangle|^{2}d\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left|\sum_{p=1}^{s}f_{1}(p)e^{-i\theta p}+\sum_{q=1}^{r}f_{2}(q)e^{-i\theta(q-r)}\right|^{2}d\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sum_{1\leq p,p^{\prime}\leq s}\bar{f}_{1}(p)f_{1}(p^{\prime})e^{i\theta(p-p^{\prime})}+\sum_{1\leq q,q^{\prime}\leq r}\bar{f}_{2}(q)f_{2}(q^{\prime})e^{i\theta(q-q^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+2\Re\left(\sum_{1\leq p\leq s,1\leq q\leq r}\bar{f}_{1}(p)f_{2}(q)e^{i\theta(p-q+r)}\right)d\theta

\displaystyle=C\left(\|f_{1}\|^{2}+\|f_{2}\|^{2}\right)=C\|f\|^{2},

where at the last line, we have used the linearity of the trace and the orthonormality of the functions
e_{p}:\theta\to e^{ip\theta}
in
L^{2}([0,2\pi],d\theta/(2\pi))
.
We deduce that
\mathcal{A}(z,z)
is positive definite. The proof for
\mathcal{B}(z,z)
is identical (it is even simpler). The conclusion follows.
∎

5.5
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We first claim that
\max_{p,q}\E|W^{(1)}_{n}(z)_{p,q}|^{4}
is bounded uniformly in
n
and
z\in\Gamma=\bar{\Omega}
. Indeed, since the operator norms of
P
,
Q
,
U_{n}
and
R^{\prime}_{n}(z)
are bounded uniformly, it suffices to check that there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any deterministic vectors
\phi,\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
of unit norms,

(53)

\E|\langle\phi,X\psi\rangle|^{4}\leq C.

To this end, we write

\displaystyle\E|\langle\phi,X\psi\rangle|^{4}

\displaystyle=\E\langle\phi,X\psi\rangle^{2}\overline{\langle\phi,X\psi\rangle}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\bar{\phi}_{i_{1}}\bar{\phi}_{i_{2}}\phi_{i_{3}}\phi_{i_{4}}\E[X_{i_{1}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{2}}\bar{X}_{i_{3}j_{3}}\bar{X}_{i_{4}j_{4}}]\psi_{j_{1}}\psi_{j_{2}}\bar{\psi}_{j_{3}}\bar{\psi}_{j_{4}},

where the sum is over all
1\leq i_{l},j_{l}\leq N
,
1\leq l\leq 4
. Using assumptions
(H_{0})
and
(H_{4})
, we deduce that, with
m=\E|X_{ij}|^{4}
, we have

\E|\langle\phi,X\psi\rangle|^{4}\leq m\sum_{p}\sum_{i,j}\delta_{p}(i,j)|\phi_{i_{1}}\phi_{i_{2}}\phi_{i_{3}}\phi_{i_{4}}\psi_{j_{1}}\psi_{j_{2}}\psi_{j_{3}}\psi_{j_{4}}|,

where the sum is over all pairings
p
of
\{1,2,3,4\}
(that is
p
is one of the three pair partitions of
\{1,\ldots,4\}
) and
\delta_{p}(i,j)=1
if and only if for all pairs
(a,b)
of
p
,
(i_{a},j_{a})=(i_{b},j_{b})
and
\delta_{p}(i,j)=0
otherwise. In particular, we find

\E|\langle\phi,X\psi\rangle|^{4}\leq 3m\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}}|\phi_{i_{1}}\phi_{i_{2}}\psi_{j_{1}}\psi_{j_{2}}|^{2}=3m.

This proves (
53
).

Theorem
1.2
is then an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.1
, Proposition
5.3
and the uniform integrability of
W^{(1)}_{n}(z)_{pq}W^{(1)}_{n}(z^{\prime})_{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}}
. The only remaining statement that we need to check is that when
U_{n}=I_{n}
then
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}=Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)XR^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}
converges weakly. Write

R^{\prime}_{n}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}G_{1}\\
G_{2}\\
G_{3}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{G}_{1}^{\intercal}&\tilde{G}_{2}^{\intercal}&\tilde{G}_{3}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix},

where
G_{1}
,
\tilde{G}_{3}
have size
s\times n
and
G_{3},\tilde{G}_{1}
have size
r\times n
,
We have

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}=\mathcal{D}\begin{pmatrix}G_{1}X\tilde{G}_{3}^{\intercal}&G_{1}X\tilde{G}_{1}^{\intercal}\\
G_{3}X\tilde{G}_{3}^{\intercal}&G_{3}X\tilde{G}_{1}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{E}.

We should therefore check that the above matrix in the middle converges. To this end, we observe
(R^{\prime}_{n}(z))_{pq}=\gamma_{n}(p-q)
for some
n
-periodic function
\gamma_{n}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{C}
. If
p-q=\ell\;\mathrm{mod}(n)
we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}(\ell)=(R^{\prime}_{n}(z))_{pq}

\displaystyle=(F_{n}\Delta_{n}(z)F_{n}^{*})_{pq}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega_{n}^{(p-1)(k-1)}\frac{1}{z-\bfa(\omega_{n}^{k-1})}\omega_{n}^{-(q-1)(k-1)}

(54)

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\gamma(\ell)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i\theta\ell}}{z-\bfa(e^{i\theta})}d\theta.

Next, we consider the infinite array
(\tilde{X}^{(n)}_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined by
\tilde{X}^{(n)}_{ij}=X_{i_{n},j_{n}}
where
i_{n}=i
,
j_{n}=j

\mathrm{mod}(n)
and
1\leq i_{n},j_{n}\leq n
. The infinite array
\tilde{X}^{(n)}
converges in distribution (for the product topology on infinite arrays) toward
\tilde{X}=(X_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}
an infinite array of iid copies of
X_{11}
.

We may now check that
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}

converges weakly to an explicit matrix expressed in terms of
\gamma
and
\tilde{X}
. Consider for example, the entry
G_{1}X\tilde{G}_{3}^{\intercal}
. For
1\leq p,q\leq s
, we have

\displaystyle(G_{1}X\tilde{G}_{3}^{\intercal})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a=1}^{n}\sum_{b=1}^{n}(R^{\prime}_{n}(z))_{pa}X_{a,b}(R^{\prime}_{n}(z))_{b,n-s+q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b\in D_{n}}\gamma_{n}(p-a)\tilde{X}^{(n)}_{a,b}\gamma_{n}(b+s-q),

where
D_{n}=\{-\lceil n/2\rceil+1,\ldots\lfloor n/2\rfloor\}
and we have performed the change of variable from
\{1,\ldots,n\}\to D_{n}
,
a\to a\mathbbm{1}(a\leq n/2)+(a-n)\mathbbm{1}(a>n/2)
and used the
n
-periodicity of
\tilde{X}^{(n)}
and
\gamma_{n}
.
Similarly, for
1\leq p\leq s
,
1\leq q\leq r
,

\displaystyle(G_{1}X\tilde{G}_{1}^{\intercal})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b\in D_{n}}\gamma_{n}(p-a)\tilde{X}^{(n)}_{a,b}\gamma_{n}(b-q).

For
1\leq p\leq r
,
1\leq q\leq s
,

\displaystyle(G_{3}X\tilde{G}_{3}^{\intercal})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b\in D_{n}}\gamma_{n}(p-r-a)\tilde{X}^{(n)}_{a,b}\gamma_{n}(b+s-q).

For
1\leq p\leq r
,
1\leq q\leq r
,

\displaystyle(G_{3}X\tilde{G}_{1}^{\intercal})_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b\in D_{n}}\gamma_{n}(p-r-a)\tilde{X}^{(n)}_{a,b}\gamma_{n}(b-q).

Note also that assumption
(H_{0})
and Markov inequality imply that the event {
for all

a,b
,
|\tilde{X}^{(n)}_{a,b}|\leq C({|a|+|b|})^{2}\}
has probability tending to one as
C\to\infty
. Using Lemma
5.1
, we deduce by dominated convergence that
\tilde{W}_{n}^{(1)}
converges weakly toward

(55)

W^{(1)}=\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{E},

where
\mathcal{A}
is obtained by taking the limit
n\to\infty
in the above formulas. More precisely, for
1\leq p,q\leq r+s
, we have

\mathcal{A}_{pq}=\sum_{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\gamma(p-a+\varepsilon_{p})X_{a,b}\gamma(b+s-q),

with
\varepsilon_{p}=-(r+s)\mathbbm{1}(p>s)

(recall that
\gamma
depends implicitly on
z
). It concludes the proof of the convergence.∎

5.6
Convergence of the outlier eigenvalues : proof of Theorem
1.3

Proof of the first claim: convergence of the outlier eigenvalues if
\varphi
is a.s. not the zero function.
Let
\Omega
be a bounded open connected subset of
\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,\delta}
,
\delta\neq 0
, as in the statement of Theorem
1.3
. Set
\Gamma=\bar{\Omega}\subset\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,\delta}
be the closure of
\Omega
. On the event
\{\spectrum(S_{n})\cap\Gamma=\emptyset\}
, we set

\varphi_{n}\colon z\mapsto\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\right)^{|\delta|}\det\left(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R_{n}(z)P_{n}\right),

and
\varphi_{n}=0
otherwise. The function
\varphi_{n}
is a random analytic function on
\Omega
. Next, the event
\{\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|G_{n}(z)\|\leq 1\}
has probability converging to
1
by Proposition
4.2
.
Multiplying (
17
) by
\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\right)^{|\delta|}
, we have, on the event
\{\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|G_{n}(z)\|\leq 1\}
, that for any
z\in\Gamma
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\right)^{|\delta|}\sum_{k=0}^{r+s}\Tr\left(\adj_{k}(I_{r+s}+Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n})\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(G_{n}(z))\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{|\delta|}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\right)^{|\delta|-k}\Tr\left(\adj_{k}(I_{r+s}+\mathcal{H}(z))\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\left(W_{n}(z)\right)\right)+\varepsilon_{n}(z),

where, for some constant
C=C_{\Gamma}
,

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon_{n}\|_{\Gamma}=\sup_{z\in\Gamma}|\varepsilon_{n}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\right)^{|\delta|}\{\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}-\mathcal{H}(z)\|\}\vee\{\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|Q_{n}R^{\prime}_{n}(z)P_{n}-\mathcal{H}(z)\|\}^{|\delta|}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+C\left\{\left(\frac{\sigma_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|W_{n}(z)\|\right\}\vee\left\{\left(\frac{\sigma_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\|W_{n}(z)\|\right\}^{r+s-|\delta|}.

Since
\frac{1}{n}\ln\sigma_{n}\to 0
, by Proposition
5.1
and Theorem
5.1
, we have that, in probability,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\varepsilon_{n}\|_{\Gamma}=0.

Now, from Proposition
5.2
,
\adj_{k}(I_{r+s}+\mathcal{H}(z))=0
for
k<|\delta|
. We find

(56)

\varphi_{n}(z)=\Tr\left(\adj_{|\delta|}(I_{r+s}+\mathcal{H}(z))\bigwedge\nolimits^{|\delta|}\left(W_{n}(z)\right)\right)+\varepsilon_{n}(z).

From the continuous mapping theorem, along any accumulation point
W
of
W_{n}
,
\varphi_{n}
converges weakly in
\mathcal{H}(\Omega)
toward

\varphi(z)=\Tr\left(\adj_{|\delta|}(I_{r+s}+\mathcal{H}(z))\bigwedge\nolimits^{|\delta|}\left(W(z)\right)\right)

defined in the statement of Theorem
1.3
. If
\varphi
is a.s. not the constant function, the first claim of the theorem is then a consequence of Proposition
7.3
in Appendix.

Proof of the second claim: if
W
is Gaussian
\varphi
is a.s. not the zero function.
It remains to prove that if
W
is Gaussian then
\varphi
is a.s. not the constant function. We start with a general lemma on Gaussian vectors. Recall that we say that a random vector
X
on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
is Gaussian if
(\Re(X),\Im(X))
is a real Gaussian vector on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
d\geq 1
and
X
be a centered Gaussian vector on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
such that

\E XX^{*}
is positive definite. Then there exists
Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{d})
centered Gaussian on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
with
\E ZZ^{*}=I_{d}
such that the laws of
X
and
Z
are equivalent measures.

Proof.

Recall that the support of the law of a real Gaussian vector on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
is a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then, the proof is based on the observation that two real Gaussian vectors
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
have equivalent laws if their supports are equal. This is immediate since Gaussian distributions have positive densities on their supports (with respect to the Lebesgue measure on their support). Now, we identify
\mathbb{C}^{d}
with the real vector space, say
C_{d}
, of dimension
2d
, spanned by
(e_{k},ie_{k}),1\leq k\leq d,
where
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{d})
is the canonical basis of
\mathbb{C}^{d}
. We see
X=\Re(X)+i\Im(X)
as a real Gaussian vector on
C_{d}
and denote by
V
its support. Let
E_{k}
,
1\leq k\leq d
, be the vector subspace of
C_{d}
spanned by
(e_{k},ie_{k})
, we may identify
E_{k}
with a copy of
\mathbb{C}
. We finally set
V_{k}=E_{k}\cap V
. Our assumption implies that
\dim(V_{k})\in\{1,2\}
and

V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\ldots\oplus V_{d}.

It remains to define
Z
as the Gaussian vector whose coordinates
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{d})
in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
are centered independent,
Z_{k}
has support
V_{k}
and
\E|Z_{k}|^{2}=1
(if
\dim(V_{k})=2
, we further assume that
\E Z_{k}^{2}=0
to fully characterize the law of
Z_{k}
). ∎

We may prove the second claim of the theorem. We fix
z\in\Gamma
and set
A=\adj_{|\delta|}(I_{r+s}+\mathcal{H}(z))
. It suffices to prove that
\varphi(z)\neq 0
with probability one. By Lemma
5.5
and Lemma
5.4
it suffices to prove that
T\neq 0
with probability one, where

T=\Tr\left(A\bigwedge\nolimits^{|\delta|}\left(Z\right)\right)

and
Z\in M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
is a centered Gaussian matrix with independent indices of unit variance. We recall that, indexing the entries of the matrix
\bigwedge\nolimits^{|\delta|}\left(Z\right)
by pairs of subsets
(I,J)
of
\{1,\ldots,r+s\}
of cardinal
|\delta|
, the entry
(I,J)
of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{|\delta|}\left(Z\right)
is
\det Z(I|J)
. Next, we write

\det Z(I|J)=\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\prod_{i\in I}Z_{i,\sigma(i)},

where the sum is over all bijections
\sigma
from
I
to
J
and
(-1)^{\sigma}
is the corresponding signature. Since
Z_{i,j}
are independent, centered of unit variance, we have the classical orthogonality relations: if
(I,J)\neq(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})

\E[\det Z(I|J)\overline{\det Z(I^{\prime}|J^{\prime})}]=0

and

\E[|\det Z(I|J)|^{2}]=\sum_{\sigma}\prod_{i\in J}\E|Z_{i,\sigma(j)}|^{2}=|\delta|!,

(these relations are central in
[
BZ20
]
).
Thus, we get

\E[|T|^{2}]=\E\left|\sum_{I,J}A_{J,I}\det(Z(I|J))\right|^{2}=\sum_{I,J}|A_{J,I}|^{2}|\delta|!.

Proposition
5.2
implies that
A\neq 0
and thus

\E[|T|^{2}]>0.

Finally, since
T
is a polynomial in the variables
(Z_{ij})
, the Carbery-Wright inequality
[
CW01
]
implies that
T\neq 0
with probability one.

Proof of the third claim: if
U_{n}=I_{n}
and
(H_{\mathrm{ac}})
holds then
\varphi
is a.s. not the zero function.

We finally prove that in the case
U_{n}=I_{n}
then
\varphi
is a.s. non-zero provided that the law of
X_{11}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on a real vector subspace, say
K
, of
\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(V)\in\{1,2\}
. Without loss of generality, up to taking a rotation, we can assume that
K=\mathbb{R}
or
K=\mathbb{C}
. We fix some
z\in\Gamma
.

From what precedes, it is enough to prove that the law of
W(z)
is absolutely continuous with respect to a Gaussian law. Since
W(z)=W^{(1)}(z)+\sigma W^{(2)}(z)
and
(W^{(1)},W^{(2)})
independent, it suffices to check that the law of
W^{(1)}(z)
is absolutely continuous with respect to a Gaussian law. This amounts to check that the law of
W^{(1)}(z)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on a vector subspace of
M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2(r+s)^{2}}
.

Recall the definition of
W^{(1)}(z)
in (
55
). Since
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{E}
are invertible, it suffices to check that the marginal laws of
\mathcal{A}(z)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on real vector subspaces of
M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2(r+s)^{2}}
. To this end, recall that, for
1\leq p,q\leq r+s
, we have

\mathcal{A}_{p,q}=\sum_{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\gamma(p-a+\varepsilon_{p})X_{a,b}\gamma(b+s-q),

where
\tilde{X}=(X_{a,b})_{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is an infinite array of iid copies of
X_{11}
,
\varepsilon_{p}=-(r+s)\mathbbm{1}(p>s)
and
\gamma(\ell)
is given in (
54
). By Lemma
5.1
, for some
0<\kappa<1
and
C>0
,

\gamma(\ell)\leq C\kappa^{|\ell|}.

The proof is ultimately a consequence of the fact that the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is invariant by orthogonal transformations and that
\mathcal{A}
can be regarded as the image of the infinite array
\tilde{X}=(X_{a,b})
by a linear map of finite rank. The first part of the proof clarifies this last point. We fix some
\beta
such that
\kappa^{2}<\beta<1
, we consider the Hilbert space
H=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{2},w)
of infinite arrays
(x_{a,b})_{a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
,
x_{a,b}\in K
, equipped with the scalar product

\langle x,y\rangle_{H}=\sum_{a,b}\bar{x}_{ab}y_{ab}\beta^{|a|+|b|}.

We may then consider the linear map
T
from
H
to
M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2(r+s)^{2}}
defined by, for
1\leq p,q\leq r+s
,

T(x)_{pq}=\sum_{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\gamma(p-a+\varepsilon_{p})x_{a,b}\gamma(b+s-q).

It is indeed a bounded linear map since, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle|T(x)_{pq}|

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\kappa^{2(r+s)}\sum_{a,b}\kappa^{|a|+|b|}|x_{ab}|

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\kappa^{2(r+s)}\sum_{a,b}\left(\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{\beta}}\right)^{|a|+|b|}|x_{ab}|\beta^{(|a|+|b|)/2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\kappa^{2(r+s)}\sqrt{\sum_{a,b}\left(\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\beta}\right)^{|a|+|b|}}\|x\|_{H}.

We equip
M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2(r+s)^{2}}
with the Euclidean scalar product. Let
T^{*}
be the adjoint of
T
. Let
H_{0}\subset H
be the image of
T^{*}
and
H^{*}_{0}\subset M_{r+s}(\mathbb{C})
the image of
T
. We have
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(H_{0})=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(H^{*}_{0})=d\in\{1,\ldots,2(r+s)^{2}\}
(
d\neq 0
follows from Lemma
5.4
). From the spectral theorem applied to
TT^{*}
and
T^{*}T
, we obtain the singular value decomposition of
T
: for any
x\in H
,

T(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sigma_{i}u_{i}\langle f_{i},x\rangle_{H},

where
\sigma_{i}>0
,
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
and
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{d})
are orthonormal basis of
H_{0}
and
H^{*}_{0}
respectively (for their respective scalar products).

Now, let
(E_{a,b}),(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, be the canonical basis of
H
. For integer
n\geq 1
, let
\pi_{n}
be the orthogonal projection from
H
onto
H^{n}
the subspace spanned by
\{x_{a,b}E_{a,b}:\max(|a|,|b|)\leq n,x_{a,b}\in K\}
. For integer
n\geq 1
large enough, we have
\pi_{n}(f_{i})\neq 0
, for all
1\leq i\leq d
. In particular, for all
n
large enough, the rank of
T_{n}=T\circ\pi_{n}
is also
d
.

We fix such integer
n\geq 1
. Let
T_{n}^{*}
be the adjoint of
T_{n}
now for the usual Euclidean scalar product on
H^{n}
,

\langle x,y\rangle=\sum_{a,b}\bar{x}_{ab}y_{ab}.

Let
H_{0}^{n}\subset H^{n}
be the image of
T_{n}^{*}
. The singular value decomposition of
T_{n}
reads: for all
x\in H^{n}
:

(57)

T_{n}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}u^{\prime}_{i}\langle f^{\prime}_{i},x\rangle,

where
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}>0
,
(f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{d})
and
(u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{d})
are orthonormal basis of
H_{0}^{n}
and
H^{*}_{0}
.

All ingredients are gathered to conclude. Recall that
\tilde{X}=(X_{a,b})
. From assumption
(H_{0})
and
\beta<1
,

\E\|\tilde{X}\|_{H}^{2}=\sum_{(a,b)}\E|X_{ab}|^{2}\beta^{|a|+|b|}=\sum_{(a,b)}\beta^{|a|+|b|}<\infty.

Hence, almost surely
\tilde{X}\in H
. It follows that

\mathcal{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}T(\tilde{X})=T_{n}(\tilde{X})+T\circ(1-\pi_{n})(\tilde{X}).

By construction
T_{n}(\tilde{X})
and
T\circ(1-\pi_{n})(\tilde{X})
are independent variables whose supports are both included in
H_{0}^{*}
. Therefore the claim that the law of
\mathcal{A}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
H_{0}^{*}
is implied by the claim that the law of
T_{n}(X)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
H_{0}^{*}
.

This is now easy to check. The real vector space
H^{n}
has finite dimension
N=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(K)(2n+1)^{2}
. By assumption
(H_{\mathrm{ac}})

X_{n}=\pi_{n}(\tilde{X})
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
H^{n}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We may complete
(f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{d})
in an orthonormal basis
(f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{N})
of
H^{n}
. Let
U
be the orthogonal matrix which maps the canonical basis of
H^{n}
to
(f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{N})
. By the change of variable formula, the law of the vector
UX_{n}=(\langle f^{\prime}_{1},X_{n}\rangle,\ldots,\langle f^{\prime}_{N},X_{n}\rangle)
is also absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
H^{n}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{N}
. In particular the law of
Y=(\langle f^{\prime}_{1},X_{n}\rangle,\ldots,\langle f^{\prime}_{d},X_{n}\rangle)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
H_{0}^{n}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Finally, from the singular value decomposition (
57
), the law of
T_{n}(\tilde{X})=\sum_{i}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}Y_{i}
is a absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
H_{0}^{*}
(since
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}>0
and
(u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{d})
orthonormal basis). ∎

6
Central limit theorem for random matrix products

In this section, for ease of notation, we drop the explicit dependency in
n
and write
X
in place of
X_{n}
.

6.1
A central limit theorem for random matrix products

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and let
B=(B_{0},B_{1},\ldots)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of deterministic matrices in
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
such that, for some vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

(58)

B_{0}=u\otimes\bar{v},

so that
B_{0}
is a rank one projector. Writing
X
in place of
X_{n}
, for integer
k\geq 1
, we are interested in the complex random variable

(59)

Z=Z_{k}(B)=n^{-(k-1)/2}\Tr(B_{0}XB_{1}X\cdots B_{k-1}X)=n^{-(k-1)/2}\langle v,XB_{1}X\cdots B_{k-1}X,u\rangle.

The variable
Z
is a generalized entry of the matrix product
XB_{1}X\cdots B_{k-1}X
. The goal of this subsection is prove that under mild assumptions, the process
(Z_{k}(B))
, indexed by
k\geq 1
and
B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
, is close in distribution to Gaussian process that we now define.

We introduce the following quantities, for
B,B^{\prime}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
,

(60)

\Sigma_{k}(B,B^{\prime})=n^{-k+1}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\Tr(B_{t}(B^{\prime}_{t})^{*})\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\Sigma^{\prime}_{k}(B,B^{\prime})=\varrho^{k}n^{-k+1}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\Tr(B_{t}(B^{\prime}_{t})^{\intercal}).

We note that for
B_{0}=u\otimes\bar{v}
and
B^{\prime}_{0}=u^{\prime}\otimes\bar{v}^{\prime}
as in (
58
), we have

\Tr(B_{0}(B^{\prime}_{0})^{*})=\langle v,u^{\prime}\rangle\langle v^{\prime},u\rangle\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\Tr(B_{0}(B^{\prime}_{0})^{\intercal})=\langle\bar{v},u^{\prime}\rangle\langle\bar{v}^{\prime},u\rangle.

We consider
W=W_{k}(B)
as the centered Gaussian process, indexed by
k\geq 1
and
B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\E W_{k}(B)\bar{W}_{l}(B^{\prime})=\E W_{k}W_{l}=0
for
k\neq l
and for
k=l
,

\E W_{k}(B)\bar{W}_{k}(B^{\prime})=\Sigma_{k}(B,B^{\prime})\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E W_{k}(B)W_{k}(B^{\prime})=\Sigma^{\prime}_{k}(B,B^{\prime}).

This process
W
can be realized as follows:

(61)

W_{k}(B)=n^{-(k-1)/2}\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}}W_{\pi}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}(B_{t})_{i_{t}j_{t+1}},

where
P_{k}
is the set of
\pi=(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})
with
i_{t},j_{t}
in
\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
i_{0}=i_{k}
and
(W_{\pi})_{\pi\in P_{k}}
are independent and identically distributed centered Gaussian random variables with
\E|W_{\pi}|^{2}=1
and
\E W_{\pi}^{2}=\varrho^{k}
.

We define a new process
\tilde{W}=\tilde{W}_{k}(B)
by setting

\tilde{W}_{1}(B)=Z_{1}(B)\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\tilde{W}_{k}(B)=W_{k}(B)\hbox{ for }k\geq 2,

where
W
and
X
are chosen to be independent. Note that
Z_{1}(B)=\langle v,Xu\rangle
by definition, in particular, it is linear in
X
.

We denote by
\|\cdot\|
the operator norm. We consider the following assumption on the sequence of matrices
B=(B_{0},B_{2},\ldots)
, for any integer
k\geq 0
,

(62)

\|B_{k}\|\lesssim 1,

where here and below the notation
\lesssim
coincides with the
O(\cdot)
notation and the constants hidden may depend on
k
and the upper bound
C_{k}
on the moments
\E|X_{ij}|^{k}
in Assumption
(H_{k})
. Note that for
k=0
, we have
\|B_{0}\|=\|u\|_{2}\|v\|_{2}
where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is the Euclidean norm.

Since
\Tr(M)\leq\mathrm{rank}(M)\|M\|
for
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, under assumption (
62
), we have

|\Sigma_{k}(B,B)|\lesssim 1\quad\hbox{ and }\quad|\Sigma^{\prime}_{k}(B,B)|\lesssim 1.

This explains the choice of the scaling for
Z
and
W
.

The main result in this subsection is the following multivariate central limit theorem theorem.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume that
(H_{k})
holds for all
k\geq 2
. Let
T
be a finite set and for
t\in T
, let
k_{t}\geq 1
be an integer and
B_{t}=(B_{t,0},B_{t,1},\ldots)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
with
B_{t,0}=u_{t}\otimes\bar{v}_{t}
as in (
58
) and such that, for all
t\in T
, (
62
) holds for
B_{t}
, uniformly in
n
.

Then, the Lévy-Prokhorov distance between the laws of
(Z_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T}
and
(\tilde{W}_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T}
goes to
0
as the
n
goes to infinity.

Moreover, if for all
t\in T
,
\|u_{t}\|_{\infty}
and
\|v_{t}\|_{\infty}
go to
0
as
n
goes to infinity, then the Lévy-Prokhorov distance between the laws of
(Z_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T}
and
(W_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T}
goes to
0
as the
n
goes to infinity.

The special role played by
k=1
is easy to notice. For example, if
u=v=e_{1}
is a vector the canonical basis then
Z_{1}=X_{11}
and we might not expect a central limit theorem (unless the law of
X_{ij}
is Gaussian). However, as soon as
k\geq 2
, there is a central limit theorem. This central limit phenomenon can be related to the second-order freenees, see
[
MS06
]
.

The proof of Theorem
6.1
will rely on Stein’s method of exchangeable pairs. In particular, a quantitative version of Theorem
6.1
can be extracted from the proof (even if we made no effort to optimize the bounds on the various asymptotically negligible terms).

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
6.1
. Expanding the matrix product, in (
59
) we find

(63)

Z=n^{-(k-1)/2}\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}(B_{t})_{i_{t}j_{t+1}}\prod_{t=1}^{k}X_{j_{t}i_{t}}=\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}}M_{\pi}X_{\pi},

where, as above,
P_{k}
is the set of
\pi=(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})
with
i_{t},j_{t}
in
\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
i_{0}=i_{k}
and we have set

X_{\pi}=\prod_{t=1}^{k}X_{j_{t}i_{t}}\;\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\;M_{\pi}=n^{-(k-1)/2}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}(B_{t})_{i_{t}j_{t+1}}.

The vector
\pi
can be thought as a path on the set of indices. The complex number
M_{\pi}
is the deterministic weight associated to the path
\pi
while the complex number
X_{\pi}
is the random weight associated to
\pi
. When we want to make the dependence in the variables explicit in
k,B
we will write

Z
as
Z=Z_{k}(B)
and
M_{\pi}
as
M_{\pi}=M_{\pi}(B)
for example.

We set

(64)

L=L_{k}(B)=\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k}}M_{\pi}(B)X_{\pi},

where
Q_{k}\subset P_{k}
is the subset of paths
\pi=(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})
such that for all
s\neq t

(j_{t},i_{t})\neq(j_{s},i_{s})
. Our first lemma asserts that
Z
and
T
are close in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
. The main interest of
L
is that it is a linear function of the matrix entry
X_{ij}
for any
i,j
. Obviously
L_{1}=Z_{1}
. For
k\geq 2
, the next lemma asserts that
Z_{k}
and
L_{k}
remain close.

Lemma 6.1
.

For any
k\geq 1
and
B=(B_{0},B_{1},B_{2},\ldots)
satisfying (
58
) and (
62
), we have

\E|Z-L|^{2}\lesssim n^{-1}.

Proof.

Set

\hat{Z}=Z-L=\sum_{\pi\in R_{k}}M_{\pi}X_{\pi},

with
R_{k}=P_{k}\backslash Q_{k}
. The statement of the lemma follows from the two claims

(65)

|\E\hat{Z}|\lesssim n^{-1/2}

and

(66)

\E|\hat{Z}-\E\hat{Z}|^{2}\lesssim n^{-1}.

We start with (
65
). Note that by assumption
(H_{k})
, we have
\E|X_{\pi}|\lesssim 1
. We write

\E\hat{Z}=\sum_{\pi\in R_{k}}M_{\pi}\E X_{\pi}.

To each
\pi=(j_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
in
P_{k}
, we associate a bipartite directed (multi)-graph
G(\pi)
with bipartite vertex sets
I=\{i_{t}:1\leq t\leq k\}
and
J=\{j_{t}:1\leq t\leq k\}
and edge sets
E_{JI}=\{(j_{t},i_{t}):1\leq t\leq k\}
and
E_{IJ}=\{(i_{t-1},j_{t}):1\leq t\leq k\}
with the convention
i_{0}=i_{k}
. The multiplicity of an edge
(j,i)\in E_{JI}
is the number of
t
such that
(j,i)=(j_{t},i_{t})
. Since
\E X_{ji}=0
,
\E X_{\pi}=0
unless all edges in
E_{JI}
have multiplicity at least two. We denote by
\bar{P}_{k}
the subset of such paths.

We say that two paths
\pi=(j_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
,
\pi^{\prime}=(j^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,j^{\prime}_{k})
are isomorphic and write
\pi\sim\pi^{\prime}
if there exist two bijections
\phi,\psi
on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
\phi(j_{t})=j^{\prime}_{t}
and
\psi(i_{t})=i^{\prime}_{t}
. This is an equivalence relation. By construction
G(\pi)
and
G(\pi^{\prime})
are isomorphic if
\pi\sim\pi^{\prime}
. Also, by assumption
\E X_{\pi}=\E X_{\pi^{\prime}}
if
\pi\sim\pi^{\prime}
. Note that there is a finite number of equivalence classes (
k^{2k}
is a very rough upper bound). To prove (
65
), it follows that it is enough to prove that for any
\pi\in\bar{P}_{k}
,

(67)

m_{1}(\pi)={{\left|\sum_{\pi^{\prime}:\pi^{\prime}\sim\pi}M_{\pi^{\prime}}\right|}}\lesssim n^{-1/2}.

We fix such
\pi=(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})\in\bar{P}_{k}
and denote by
I,J,E_{IJ},E_{JI}
the vertex and edge sets of
G(\pi)
. Writing
\pi^{\prime}=(j^{\prime}_{1},i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,j^{\prime}_{k},i^{\prime}_{k})
, we have

(68)

m_{1}(\pi)=n^{-(k-1)/2}{{\left|\sum_{\pi^{\prime}:\pi^{\prime}\sim\pi}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}(B_{t})_{i^{\prime}_{t}j^{\prime}_{t+1}}\right|}}.

Since each edge, say
(j,i)
, in
E_{JI}
has multiplicity at least
2
,
i
and
j
are visited at least twice (that is
i_{t}=i
for at least two distinct
1\leq t\leq k
and similarly for
j
). In particular,
|I|\leq k/2
and
|J|\leq k/2
. We define the in-degree of
j\in J
as the number of distinct
0\leq t\leq k-1
such that
j_{t+1}=j
(that is
(i_{t},j_{t+1})\in E_{IJ}
is an incoming edge). From what precedes, all
j\in J
have in-degree at least two.

We start with a rough upper bound. We claim that

(69)

m_{1}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-(k-1)/2}n^{|I|\wedge|J|-1/2}.

Let us prove this claim. We start with the following observation. The involution map on
P_{k}
,
\theta:(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})\to(i_{k},j_{k},\ldots,i_{1},j_{1})
inverses the role played by
I
and
J
. Moreover, we have
m_{1,B}(\theta(\pi))=m_{1,\check{B}}(\pi)
where we have written the dependence of
m_{1}
on
B
explicitly and set for
B=(B_{0},B_{1},B_{2}\ldots)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
,
\check{B}=(B_{0}^{\intercal},B_{k-1}^{\intercal},\ldots,B_{1}^{\intercal},B_{k},\ldots)
. Since that
M
and
M^{\intercal}
have the same norm, it is sufficient to prove that (
69
) holds with only
|I|
in the exponent.

We use the convention that a product over an emptyset is equal to
1
. For any
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{p}
in
\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
p\geq 2
and
M_{1},\ldots,M_{p}
in
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, from Cauchy-Scwharz inequality, we have the upper bound,

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\prod_{q=1}^{p}{{\left|(M_{q})_{\iota_{q}j^{\prime}}\right|}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{q=3}^{p}\|M_{q}\|\sqrt{\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}|(M_{1})_{\iota_{1}j^{\prime}}|^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}|(M_{2})_{\iota_{2}j^{\prime}}|^{2}}

(70)

\displaystyle=\prod_{q=3}^{p}\|M_{q}\|\sqrt{(M_{1}M_{1})^{*}_{\iota_{1}\iota_{1}}}\sqrt{(M_{2}M_{2})^{*}_{\iota_{2}\iota_{2}}}\leq\prod_{q=1}^{p}\|M_{q}\|.

To estimate
m_{1}(\pi)
in (
68
), we sum over all possible bijections, that is over all possible values of
(j^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{k})
. As already pointed, all
J
-vertices have in-degree at least
2
. Then, we put the absolute value in (
68
) inside the sum. We use (
70
) for each
j\in J
when summing over all possible images say
j^{\prime}
. Here, in (
70
),
p\geq 2
is the in-degree of
j\in J
in
G(\pi)
, the matrices
M_{q}
are equal to
B_{t_{q}}
for some
0\leq t_{q}\leq k-1
and
\iota_{q}=i^{\prime}_{t_{q}}
(recall
i^{\prime}_{0}=i^{\prime}_{k}
). There is at most
n
possible images, say
i^{\prime}
, of any
i\in I
, we deduce that

m_{1}(\pi)\leq{{\left(\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{t}\|\right)}}n^{-(k-1)/2}n^{|I|}.

It does not quite prove (
69
) due to a missing factor
n^{-1/2}
. To obtain this improvement, we use that
B_{0}
is a rank one projector. More precisely, we use the first expression in (
70
) when
j=j_{1}\in J
and pick
\iota_{1}=i^{\prime}_{0}=i^{\prime}_{k}
,
M_{1}=B_{0}
. Then, since
\Tr(B_{0}B_{0}^{*})=\|u\|_{2}\|v\|_{2}=\|B_{0}\|^{2}
, we deduce from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sqrt{(B_{0}B^{*}_{0})_{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}}\leq\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\Tr(B_{0}B^{*}_{0})}\leq\|B_{0}\|\sqrt{n}.

Hence, when summing over the possible images of
I
-vertices, we have improved the bound from
n^{|I|}
to
n^{|I|-1/2}
. This proves (
69
).

The bound (
69
) proves (
67
) as soon as
|I|\wedge|J|\leq k/2-1
. Since
|I|\vee|J|\leq k/2
, the remaining case to consider is:
k
even and
|I|=|J|=k/2

We first remark that when summing over all
\pi^{\prime}\sim\pi
that is over all possible values of
i^{\prime}_{t}
,
j^{\prime}_{t}
, we may remove the constraints that
i^{\prime}_{t}\neq i^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
if
i_{t}\neq i_{t^{\prime}}
. Indeed, the case where this constraint is not satisfied corresponds to
i^{\prime}_{t}=i^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
and can thus be written in absolute value as
m_{1}(\tilde{\pi})
where
\tilde{\pi}
is obtained from
\pi
by identifying
i_{t}
and
i_{t^{\prime}}
. In particular, there are at most
|I|-1
vertices in
I(\tilde{\pi})
and we can apply (
69
) to neglect this contribution. The same argument works for
J
-vertices. We denote by
\hat{m}_{1}(\pi)
, the expression of
m_{1}(\pi)
when the constraints
i_{t}\neq i_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
if
i_{t}\neq i_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
have been removed, and similarly for
J
-vertices. From what precedes, to prove (
67
), it suffices to check
\hat{m}_{1}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-1/2}
. We have the following expression for
\hat{m}_{1}(\pi)
,

\hat{m}_{1}(\pi)=n^{-(k-1)/2}{{\left|\sum_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{k/2},j^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,j^{\prime}_{k/2}}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}(B_{t})_{i^{\prime}_{i_{t-1}},j^{\prime}_{j_{t}}}\right|}},

where the sum is over all
1\leq i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}\leq n
, for all
1\leq s\leq k/2
.

Consider
G_{IJ}
as the bipartite (multi)-graph on the disjoint union
I\sqcup J
with edge (multi)-set
E_{IJ}
(this is a slight abuse of notation
E_{IJ}
is treated here as a multiset with
k
elements). The assumption
2|I|=2|J|=k
implies that all vertices are of degree
2
in
G_{IJ}
. In particular,
G_{IJ}
is a disjoint union of alternating cycles (where a cycle a length
2
is composed of two distinct edges
(i,j)
from
i
to
j
).

Pick an element
i\in I
. It appears in exactly two factors in the above expression for
\hat{m}_{1}(\pi)
, say
t=t_{1}
and
t=t_{2}
. We then use the elementary identity

\sum_{i^{\prime}}(M_{1})_{i^{\prime}k_{1}}(M_{2})_{i^{\prime}k_{2}}=(M^{\intercal}_{1}M_{2})_{k_{1}k_{2}}.

with
M_{\varepsilon}=B_{t_{\varepsilon}}
and
k_{\varepsilon}=j^{\prime}_{j_{t_{\varepsilon}}}
,
\varepsilon=1,2
. Repeating this for all
I
and
J
-vertices, we deduce that

\hat{m}_{1}(\pi)=n^{-(k-1)/2}\prod_{l=1}^{c}{{\left|\Tr(A_{l})\right|}},

where
c
is the number of connected components of
G_{IJ}
and
A_{l}
is a product of the matrices
(B^{\sigma_{t}}_{t})
,
t\in p_{l}
, where
p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{c})
is a partition of
\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
into
c
blocks and
B_{t}^{\sigma_{t}}\in\{B_{t},B^{\intercal}_{t}\}
. Since
\|B_{t}\|\lesssim 1
, we have
\|A_{l}\|\lesssim 1
and thus
|\Tr(A_{l})|\lesssim n
. However, if
0\in p_{l}
, then, since
B_{0}=u\otimes\bar{v}
,
\Tr(M_{1}B_{0}M_{2})=\langle v,M_{2}M_{1}u\rangle
and we find

\Tr(A_{l})=\langle v,A^{\prime}_{l}u\rangle

where
A^{\prime}_{l}
is a product of the matrices
(B^{\varepsilon_{t}}_{t})
,
t\in p_{l}\backslash\{0\}
. Hence,
|\Tr(A_{l})|\lesssim 1
if
0\in p_{l}
. We have thus checked that

\hat{m}_{1}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-(k-1)/2}n^{c-1}\lesssim n^{-(k-1)/2}n^{|I|-1}.

This concludes the proof of (
67
) and (
65
).

We now prove (
66
). The proof follows exactly the same lines. It ultimately relies on (
70
) and some topological facts. We write

(71)

\displaystyle\E|\hat{Z}-\E\hat{Z}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in R_{k}}M_{\pi_{1}}\bar{M}_{\pi_{2}}\E[(X_{\pi_{1}}-\E X_{\pi_{1}})\overline{(X_{\pi_{2}}-\E X_{\pi_{2}})}].

With the above notation, to each
\pi_{1}=(j_{1,1},\ldots,i_{1,k})
,
\pi_{2}=(j_{2,1},\ldots,i_{2,k})
in
P_{k}
, we associate the bipartite directed graph
G(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=G(\pi_{1})\cup G(\pi_{2})
with vertex sets
I=I(\pi_{1})\cup I(\pi_{2})
,
J=J(\pi_{1})\cup J(\pi_{2})
and edge sets
E_{IJ}=E_{IJ}(\pi_{1})\cup E_{IJ}(\pi_{2})
,
E_{JI}=E_{JI}(\pi_{1})\cup E_{JI}(\pi_{2})
. Due to the independence of the entries of
X
, the expectation in (
71
) is zero unless
E_{JI}(\pi_{1})
and
E_{JI}(\pi_{2})
have a non-empty intersection and each edge in
E_{JI}
has multiplicity at least two. We denote by
\bar{P}_{k,k}\subset P_{k}\times P_{k}
the collection of pairs of paths
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
such that each edge in
E_{JI}
is visited twice. We set
\bar{R}_{k,k}=(R_{k}\times R_{k})\cap\bar{P}_{k,k}
.

As above, we introduce an equivalence relation on
P_{k}\times P_{k}
:
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\sim\pi^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2})
if there exist two bijections
\phi,\psi
on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that for
\varepsilon=1,2
,
t=1,\ldots,k
,
\phi(j_{\varepsilon,t})=j^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,t}
,
\psi(i_{\varepsilon,t})=i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,t}
with
\pi^{\delta}_{\varepsilon}=(j^{\delta}_{\varepsilon,1},\ldots,i^{\delta}_{\varepsilon,k})
. There is a finite number of equivalence classes. To prove (
66
), it is thus sufficient to prove that for any fixed
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in\bar{R}_{k,k}
,

(72)

m_{2}(\pi)={{\left|\sum_{(\pi^{\prime}_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2})\sim(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}M_{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}\right|}}\lesssim n^{-1},

where we have used that the expectation in (
71
) is uniformly upper bounded by some constant depending on
k
and depends only the equivalence class. Writing, for
\varepsilon=1,2
,
\pi_{\varepsilon}=(j_{\varepsilon,1},\ldots,i_{\varepsilon,k})
, we get

m_{2}(\pi)=n^{-k+1}{{\left|\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\sim\pi}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}(B_{t})_{i^{\prime}_{1,t}j^{\prime}_{1,t+1}}(\bar{B}_{t})_{i^{\prime}_{2,t}j^{\prime}_{2,t+1}}\right|}}.

We first claim that

(73)

m_{2}(\pi)\leq{{\left(\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{t}\|^{2}\right)}}n^{-k+1}n^{|I|\wedge|J|-1}\lesssim n^{-k+1}n^{|I|\wedge|J|-1}.

Indeed, we argue as in the proof of (
69
). Thanks to the involution map
\theta
, it suffices to prove that
m_{2}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-k+1}n^{|I|-1}
. Then we use (
70
) for each
j\in J
. If
j_{1,1}\neq j_{2,1}
, when summing over
j_{\varepsilon,1}
, we set in the first expression in (
70
),
M_{1}=B_{0}
,
\iota_{1}=i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,k}
(
i_{1,k}
and
i_{2,k}
could be equal or not). Similarly, if
j_{1,1}=j_{2,1}
, we set in the first expression in (
70
),
M_{1}=M_{2}=B_{0}
,
\iota_{\varepsilon}=i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,k}
for
\varepsilon=1,2
. Hence in both cases, there is factor
((B_{0}B^{*}_{0})_{i^{\prime}_{1,k}i^{\prime}_{1,k}}(B_{0}B^{*}_{0})_{i^{\prime}_{2,k}i^{\prime}_{2,k}})^{1/2}
. As in the proof of (
69
), we sum over all possible images of
I
-vertices and use that
B_{0}
is a rank one projector, it gives (
73
) (in both cases
i_{1,k}=i_{2,k}
and
i_{1,k}\neq i_{2,k}
).

We consider two cases in the proof of (
72
).

Case 1 :
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
.
Since
\pi_{1}\in R_{k}
, there exists at least one edge
JI
of multiplicity at least
2
in
E_{JI}(\pi_{1})
and thus of multiplicity at least
4
in
E_{JI}
. All other edges in
E_{JI}
have multiplicity at least
2
. The sum of all edge multiplicities in
E_{JI}
is
2k
. It follows that the total number of distinct edges in
E_{JI}
is at most
k-1
. In particular
|I|\vee|J|\leq k-1
. Thanks to (
73
), this proves that (
72
) when
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
.

Case 2 :
\pi_{1}\neq\pi_{2}
.
Vertices in
J
have out-degree at least
2
since each edge in
E_{JI}
has multiplicity at least two. The sum of out-degrees of
J
-vertices is
2k
. It follows that
|J|\leq k
. Similarly
|I|\leq k
. If
|I|\wedge|J|\leq k-1
, then thanks to (
73
), (
72
) holds. The last case to consider is thus
|I|=|J|=k
.

As in the proof of (
69
), we remark also that when summing over all
\pi^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2})
in the expression of
m_{2}(\pi)
, we may remove the constraints that
i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,t}\neq i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
if
i_{\varepsilon,t}\neq i_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
. Indeed, the case where this constraint is not satisfied can be reduced to cases of
\tilde{\pi}\in\bar{R}_{k,k}
with
|I(\tilde{\pi})|<|I(\pi)|
or
|J(\tilde{\pi})|<|J(\pi)|
which can be directly bounded thanks to (
73
). We denote by
\hat{m}_{2}(\pi)
, the expression of
m_{2}(\pi)
when the constraints
i_{\varepsilon,t}\neq i_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
if
i_{\varepsilon,t}\neq i_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
have been removed. From what precedes, it suffices to check
\hat{m}_{2}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-1}
.

As above, we consider the graph
G_{IJ}
on
I\sqcup J
spanned by the edges
E_{IJ}
. Since
|I|=|J|=k
, all vertices have degree exactly two in
G_{IJ}
. As above, by construction,
\hat{m}_{2}(\pi)
is equal to a product of contributions on each connected component. Arguing as in the proof of (
69
), we get that

\hat{m}_{2}(\pi)=n^{-k+1}\prod_{l=1}^{c}{{\left|\Tr(A_{l})\right|}},

where
c
is the number of connected components of
G_{IJ}
and
A_{l}
is a product of the matrices
(C_{\varepsilon,t})
,
(\varepsilon,t)\in p_{l}
, where
p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{c})
is a partition of
\{1,2\}\times\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
into
c
blocks and
C_{\varepsilon,t}\in\{B_{t},B^{\intercal}_{t}\}
. Using that
B_{0}
is a rank one projector, we deduce that

(74)

\hat{m}_{2}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-k+1}n^{c^{\prime}},

where
c^{\prime}\leq c-1
is the number of connected components of
G_{JI}
not containing a boundary vertex
j_{\varepsilon,1},i_{\varepsilon_{k}}
. The proof of (
72
) will thus be complete if we check that
c\leq k-1
. Since
|I|=|J|=k
, it is thus sufficient to check that there exists a connected component with two distinct
IJ
-edges (since each connected component contains at least one
J
-vertex and
I
-vertex).

Now, we use the assumption that
\pi_{1}\neq\pi_{2}
but
E_{JI}(\pi_{1})\cap E_{JI}(\pi_{2})
non-empty. It implies that there exists
v_{*}\in I\cup J
which is in
\pi_{1}\cap\pi_{2}
but the neighboring edges in
G(\pi_{1})
and
G(\pi_{2})
are not all equal. Then, since in-degrees and out-degrees are exactly two, it leaves only two possibilities. If
v_{*}=i_{*}\in I
then for some
t_{1},t_{2}
:
j_{1,t_{1}}=j_{2,t_{2}}
,
i_{*}=i_{1,t_{1}}=i_{2,t_{2}}
and
j_{1,t_{1}+1}\neq j_{2,t_{2}+1}
. If
v_{*}=j_{*}\in J
,
i_{1,t_{1}-1}\neq i_{2,t_{2}-1}
,
j_{*}=j_{1,t_{1}}=j_{2,t_{2}}
and
i_{1,t_{1}}=i_{2,t_{2}}
. Indeed, only the edge
IJ
may differ: if two neighboring
JI
-edges of
v_{\star}
differ, then the out and in-degree of
v_{*}
would be at least
4
(since each
JI
-edge has multiplicity at least
2
). We have thus found a vertex with two distinct adjacent
IJ
-edges. It concludes the proof of (
66
).
∎

Our next lemma computes the asymptotic covariance of
L=L_{k}(B)
. For integer
k\geq 1
. For
B,B^{\prime}
in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
, we set

(75)

\hat{\Sigma}_{k}(B,B^{\prime})=\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k}}M_{\pi}(B)\bar{M}_{\pi}(B^{\prime})\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\hat{\Sigma}_{k}^{\prime}(B,B^{\prime})=\varrho^{k}\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k}}M_{\pi}(B)M_{\pi}(B^{\prime}),

where we have written explicitly the dependence of
M_{\pi}
in
B
.
The following lemma asserts that
\hat{\Sigma}
and
\hat{\Sigma}^{\prime}
is the asymptotic covariance of
L
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
k,l\geq 1
be integers and
B,B^{\prime}
in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying assumptions (
58
)-(
62
). Set
L=L_{k}(B)
and
L^{\prime}=L_{l}(B^{\prime})
. We have
\E L=0
. Moreover, if
k\neq l
,
\E L\bar{L}^{\prime}=\E LL^{\prime}=0
while if
k=l
,

\E L\bar{L}^{\prime}=\hat{\Sigma}_{k}(B,B^{\prime})+o(1)\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E LL^{\prime}=\hat{\Sigma}^{\prime}_{k}(B,B^{\prime})+o(1),

where
o(1)
denotes a vanishing sequence as
n\to\infty
depending on
k,l
and the upper bound on the operator norms of
B_{t}
and
B^{\prime}_{t}
,
0\leq t\leq k-1
.

Proof.

The claim
\E L=0
follows from
\E X_{\pi}=0
for all
\pi\in Q_{k}
which is an immediate consequence of
\E X_{ij}=0
and the definition of
Q_{k}
. For the second claim, we write

\E L\bar{L}^{\prime}=\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k},\pi^{\prime}\in Q_{l}}M_{\pi}(B)\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}}(B^{\prime})\E[X_{\pi}\bar{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}].

Assume without loss of generality
k>l
. Recall
\E X_{ij}=0
and each
(j_{t},i_{t})
appears once in
\pi=(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})\in Q_{k}
. It follows
\E[X_{\pi}\bar{X}_{\pi^{\prime}}]
will be zero unless the
k
distinct
(j_{t},i_{t})
’s appear also
\pi^{\prime}
. This is impossible since
k>l
. The same argument applies to
\E LL^{\prime}
. We skip the proof of the claim for
k=l
: it will be a straightforward consequence of the forthcoming Lemma
6.4
, Lemma
6.5
and (
76
).
∎

Our next lemma checks that
\hat{\Sigma}_{k}
and
\Sigma_{k}
defined respectively by (
75
) and (
60
) are close.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
k\geq 1
be and integer and
B,B^{\prime}
in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying assumptions (
58
)-(
62
). Then

{{\left|\Sigma_{k}(B,B^{\prime})-\hat{\Sigma}_{k}(B,B^{\prime})\right|}}\lesssim n^{-1}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad{{\left|\Sigma^{\prime}_{k}(B,B^{\prime})-\hat{\Sigma}^{\prime}_{k}(B,B^{\prime})\right|}}\lesssim n^{-1}.

Proof.

By polarization, it suffices to check the statement for
B=B^{\prime}
. We have

\Sigma_{k}=\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}}M_{\pi}\bar{M}_{\pi}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\Sigma_{k}=\varrho^{k}\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}}M_{\pi}M_{\pi}.

Therefore,

{{\left|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\right|}}={{\left|\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}\backslash Q_{k}}M_{\pi}\bar{M}_{\pi}\right|}}.

Using the notation of Lemma
6.1
, we decompose
R_{k}=P_{k}\backslash Q_{k}
over equivalence classes. Then (
73
) applied to
m_{2}(\pi,\pi)
with
\pi\in R_{k}
implies
{{\left|\Sigma_{k}-\hat{\Sigma}_{k}\right|}}\lesssim n^{-1}
.
A similar bounds holds for
\Sigma^{\prime}_{k}-\hat{\Sigma}^{\prime}_{k}
.
∎

We now use Stein’s method of exchangeable pairs. Let
\tilde{X}=(\tilde{X}_{ij})
be an independent copy of
X
. For integers
1\leq p,q\leq n
, we define
X^{pq}
by setting for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
,

X^{pq}_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}X_{ij}&\hbox{ if $(i,j)\neq(p,q)$}\\
\tilde{X}_{pq}&\hbox{ if $(i,j)=(p,q)$}\end{array}\right..

Finally, we set
\hat{X}=X^{pq}
where
(p,q)
is uniformly distributed on
\{1,\ldots,n\}^{2}
. By construction
(X,\hat{X})
and
(\hat{X},X)
have the same distribution: they form an exchangeable pair. We define
L^{pq}=L^{pq}_{k}(B)
and
\hat{L}=\hat{L}_{k}(B)
as the random variables defined as
L
in (
64
) with
X
replaced by
X^{pq}
and
\hat{X}
respectively.

Lemma 6.4
.

For
L=L_{k}(B)
and
\hat{L}=\hat{L}_{k}(B)
as above, we have

\E[\hat{L}-L|X]=-\frac{k}{n^{2}}L.

Proof.

By construction

\E[\hat{L}-L|X]=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{p,q}\E[L^{pq}-L|X]=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k}}M_{\pi}\sum_{p,q}\E[X^{pq}_{\pi}-X_{\pi}|X].

Fix
\pi=(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})\in Q_{k}
. Observe that
X^{pq}_{\pi}=X_{\pi}
if
(p,q)\notin\{(j_{t},i_{t}):1\leq t\leq k\}
. Otherwise, we have
X^{pq}_{\pi}=\tilde{X}_{pq}X_{\pi\backslash pq}
where we have set
X_{\pi\backslash pq}=\prod_{t:j_{t}i_{t}\neq pq}X_{j_{t}i_{t}}
. Hence
\E[X^{pq}_{\pi}|X]=0
since, by the definition of
Q_{k}
,
(p,q)
is then equal to exactly one
(j_{t},i_{t})
. We deduce that

\sum_{p,q}\E[X^{pq}_{\pi}-X_{\pi}|X]=-\sum_{t=1}^{k}X_{\pi}=-kX_{\pi}.

The conclusion follows.
∎

For the next lemma, we introduce a new quantity which can be interpreted as the asymptotic quadratic variation of
\hat{L}-L
. More precisely, for
B,B^{\prime}
in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
, we set

\displaystyle S_{k}(B,B^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k}}M_{\pi}(B)\bar{M}_{\pi}(B^{\prime})\sum_{l=1}^{k}(|X_{\pi\backslash(j_{l}i_{l})}|^{2}+|X_{\pi}|^{2})

\displaystyle S_{k}^{\prime}(B,B^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k}}M_{\pi}(B)M_{\pi}(B^{\prime})\sum_{l=1}^{k}(\varrho X^{2}_{\pi\backslash(j_{l}i_{l})}+X^{2}_{\pi}),

where we have set
X_{\pi\backslash j_{l}i_{l}}=\prod_{t:j_{t}i_{t}\neq j_{l}i_{l}}X_{j_{t}i_{t}}=\prod_{t\neq l}X_{j_{t}i_{t}}
. We observe that
S_{k}
and
S_{k}^{\prime}
are measurable with respect to
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X
and that, by assumption
(H_{0})
,

(76)

\E S_{k}=2\hat{\Sigma}_{k}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E S^{\prime}_{k}=2\hat{\Sigma}^{\prime}_{k}.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
k,l\geq 1
be integers, and
B,B^{\prime}
in
M^{\mathbb{N}}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
satisfying assumptions (
58
)-(
62
). Set
L=L_{k}(B
,
\hat{L}=\hat{L}_{k}(B)
,
L^{\prime}=L_{l}(B^{\prime})
,
\hat{L}^{\prime}=\hat{L}_{l}(B^{\prime})
,
S_{k}=S_{k}(B,B^{\prime})
,
S^{\prime}_{k}=S_{k}(B,B^{\prime})
. We have

\E[(\hat{L}-L)\overline{(\hat{L}^{\prime}-L^{\prime})}|X]=\delta_{kl}\frac{S_{k}}{n^{2}}+\frac{E}{n^{2}}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E[(\hat{L}-L){(\hat{L}^{\prime}-L^{\prime})}|X]=\delta_{kl}\frac{S^{\prime}_{k}}{n^{2}}+\frac{E^{\prime}}{n^{2}},

where the random variables
E,E^{\prime}
satisfy
\E|E|^{2}\lesssim n^{-1}
and
\E|E^{\prime}|^{2}\lesssim n^{-1}
.

Proof.

We give the proof for
(\hat{L}-L){(\hat{L}^{\prime}-L^{\prime})}
, the case of
(\hat{L}-L)\overline{(\hat{L}^{\prime}-L^{\prime})}
being identical.
For
\pi\in\sqcup_{k}Q_{k}
, we set
M_{\pi}=M_{\pi}(B)
and
M^{\prime}_{\pi}=M_{\pi}(B^{\prime})
. We write

\displaystyle\E[(\hat{L}-L){(\hat{L}^{\prime}-L^{\prime})}|X]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{p,q}\E[(\hat{L}^{pq}-L){(\hat{L^{\prime}}^{pq}-L^{\prime})}|X]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k},\pi^{\prime}\in Q_{l}}M_{\pi}M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\prime}}\sum_{p,q}\E[(X^{pq}_{\pi}-X_{\pi})(X^{pq}_{\pi^{\prime}}-X_{\pi^{\prime}})|X].

Observe that
X^{pq}_{\pi}=X_{\pi}
if
(p,q)\notin\{(j_{t},i_{t}):1\leq t\leq k\}
, say
(p,q)\notin\pi
for short. Otherwise,
(p,q)\in\pi
and we have
X^{pq}_{\pi}=\tilde{X}_{pq}X_{\pi\backslash pq}
where we have set
X_{\pi\backslash pq}=\prod_{t:j_{t}i_{t}\neq pq}X_{j_{t}i_{t}}
. By the definition of
Q_{k}
,
Q_{l}
and Assumption
(H_{k})
we get

\E[(X^{pq}_{\pi}-X_{\pi})(X^{pq}_{\pi^{\prime}}-X_{\pi^{\prime}})|X]=\varrho X_{\pi\backslash pq}X_{\pi^{\prime}\backslash pq}+X_{\pi}X_{\pi^{\prime}}
if
(p,q)\in\pi\cap\pi^{\prime}
and otherwise
\E[(X^{pq}_{\pi}-X_{\pi})(X^{pq}_{\pi^{\prime}}-X_{\pi^{\prime}})|X]=0
.

We deduce that

\displaystyle\E[(\hat{L}-L){(\hat{L}^{\prime}-L^{\prime})}|X]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{\pi_{1}\in Q_{k},\pi_{2}\in Q_{l}}M_{\pi_{1}}M^{\prime}_{\pi_{2}}\sum_{(p,q)\in\pi_{1}\cap\pi_{2}}\left(\varrho X_{\pi_{1}\backslash pq}X_{\pi_{2}\backslash pq}+X_{\pi_{1}}X_{\pi_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{kl}\frac{S^{\prime}_{k}}{n^{2}}+\frac{E^{\prime}_{1}+E^{\prime}_{2}}{n^{2}},

with

\displaystyle E^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi_{1}\in Q_{k},\pi_{2}\in Q_{l},\pi_{1}\neq\pi_{2}}M_{\pi_{1}}M^{\prime}_{\pi_{2}}|\pi_{1}\cap\pi_{2}|X_{\pi_{1}}X_{\pi_{2}},

\displaystyle E^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varrho\sum_{\pi_{1}\in Q_{k},\pi_{2}\in Q_{l},\pi_{1}\neq\pi_{2}}M_{\pi_{1}}M^{\prime}_{\pi_{2}}\sum_{(p,q)\in\pi_{1}\cap\pi_{2}}X_{\pi_{1}\backslash pq}X_{\pi_{2}\backslash pq}.

We should prove that for
i=1,2
,
\E|E^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}\lesssim n^{-1}
. We prove only the case
i=1
, the case
i=2
being identical. We may assume
k\geq 2
(if
k=1
,
E^{\prime}_{i}=0
). To that end, we start by expanding
|E^{\prime}_{1}|^{2}
:
