\E|E^{\prime}_{1}|^{2}=\sum M_{\pi_{1}}M^{\prime}_{\pi_{2}}\bar{M}_{\pi_{3}}\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi_{4}}|\pi_{1}\cap\pi_{2}||\pi_{3}\cap\pi_{4}|\E\left[X_{\pi_{1}}X_{\pi_{2}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{3}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{4}}\right],

where the sum is over
4
-tuple
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4})\in Q_{k}\times Q_{l}\times Q_{k}\times Q_{k}
,
\pi_{1}\neq\pi_{2}
,
\pi_{3}\neq\pi_{4}
.
To
4
-tuple
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4})\in Q_{k}\times Q_{l}\times Q_{k}\times Q_{k}
, we may associate a bipartite directed graph
G(\pi)=(I,J,E_{IJ},E_{JI})
which is the union of the graphs
G(\pi_{\varepsilon}),\varepsilon=1,\ldots,4
defined in Lemma
6.1
. We consider the subset
\bar{Q}_{k,l,k,l}
of
Q_{k}\times Q_{l}\times Q_{k}\times Q_{k}
such that
\pi_{1}\neq\pi_{2}
,
\pi_{3}\neq\pi_{4}
,
E_{JI}(\pi_{1})\cap E_{JI}(\pi_{2})\neq\emptyset
,
E_{JI}(\pi_{3})\cap E_{JI}(\pi_{4})\neq\emptyset
and such that each edge in
E_{JI}
is visited at least twice. By assumption, in the above expansion for
\E|E^{\prime}_{1}|^{2}
, only elements
\pi\in\bar{Q}_{k,l,k,l}
might give a non-zero term in the summand.

We consider the usual equivalence class by the action of pairs of bijections on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Arguing as in Lemma
6.1
, it is sufficient to prove for any fixed
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4})\in\bar{Q}_{k,l,k,l}
, we have

(77)

m_{4}(\pi)={{\left|\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\sim\pi}M_{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}M_{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}_{3}}\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}_{4}}\right|}}\lesssim n^{-1},

where the sum is over all
\pi^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2},\pi^{\prime}_{3},\pi^{\prime}_{4})\sim\pi
. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we have

(78)

m_{4}(\pi)\leq n^{-2k+2}n^{(|I|+\delta_{I})\wedge(|J|+\delta_{J})-2},

where
\delta_{I},\delta_{J}\in\{0,1/2,1\}
. We have
\delta_{I}=1
if
j_{\varepsilon,1}=j_{\varepsilon^{\prime},1}
for all
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
in
\{1,\ldots,4\}
. Indeed, arguing as in the proof of (
73
), in this case the factor
\prod_{\varepsilon=1}^{2}((B_{0}B_{0}^{*})_{i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,k}i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,k}})^{1/2}
will appear when using (
70
) for
j=j_{\varepsilon,1}
. Similarly, we have
\delta_{I}=1/2
, if
j_{\varepsilon,1}=j_{\varepsilon^{\prime},1}
for exactly
3
different indices, say
\varepsilon=2,3,4
for example: in this case, the factor
\prod_{\varepsilon=1}^{3}((B_{0}B_{0}^{*})_{i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,k}i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,k}})^{1/2}
will appear when using (
70
) for
j=j_{1,1}
and
j=j_{2,1}
, hence the exponent
|I|-3/2
. In all other cases, when using (
70
), there will be
4
terms
\prod_{\varepsilon=1}^{4}((B_{0}B_{0}^{*})_{i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,k}i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,k}})^{1/2}
and
\delta_{I}=0
. Thanks to the involution
\theta
the same argument holds with
J
replaced by
I
and
\delta_{J}
defined accordingly.

Hence if
(|I|+\delta_{I})\wedge(|J|+\delta_{J})\leq 2k-1
, (
78
) implies (
77
). Moreover, by counting degrees of
G
, we find that
|I|\leq 2k
and
|J|\leq 2k
. We assume from now on
2k-1\leq|I|\leq|J|\leq 2k
.

We start with the case
|I|=2k-1
and
\delta_{I}\in\{1/2,1\}
. Then there is one
JI
-edge of multiplicity
p\in\{3,4\}
(this is
(j_{1},i_{1})
) and all other
JI
-edges are of multiplicity
2
. It follows
|J|=2k-1
as well.

We may argue as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
: to prove (
77
), it suffices to prove that
\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-1}
where
\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)
is obtained from
m_{4}(\pi)
by removing, when summing over
\pi^{\prime}
, the constraints that
i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,t}\neq i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
if
i_{\varepsilon,t}\neq i_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
and similarly for the
J
-vertices.

Also, as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we consider the graph
G_{IJ}
on
I\sqcup J
spanned by the edges in
E_{IJ}
. All vertices of
G_{IJ}
have degree
2
, except one
J
-vertex with degree
p
(this is a
j_{\varepsilon,1}
) and one
I
-vertex with degree
p
(this is a
i_{\varepsilon,1}
).

Case 1.

|I|=2k-1
and
\delta_{I}=1/2
.
We have
p=3
in this case. Then, by parity, there is one connected component of
G_{IJ}
which the two vertices of degree
3
and the other connected components of
G_{IJ}
contain vertices of degree
2
only. The argument leading to (
74
) implies that

\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)=n^{-2k+2}|\sum_{i,j}\prod_{q=1}^{3}(A_{1,q})_{ij}|\prod_{l=2}^{c}|\Tr(A_{l})|,

where
c
is the number of connected components of
G_{IJ}
,
A_{l}
,
A_{1,q}
are product of matrices
B_{t}
or
B^{\intercal}_{t}
,
0\leq t\leq k-1
, each
B_{t}
appears exactly 4 times. The assumption
\delta_{I}=1/2
, implies that each
A_{1,q}
contains as a factor
B_{0}
and one
A_{l}
,
l\geq 2
, contains a factor
B_{0}
. Since
B_{0}
is a rank one projector, we deduce
|\prod_{l=2}^{c}|\Tr(A_{l})|\lesssim n^{c-2}
and from (
70
), that

|\sum_{i,j}\prod_{q=1}^{3}(A_{1,q})_{ij}|\lesssim 1.

So finally,

\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-2k+2}n^{c-2}\leq n^{-1},

where the inequality
c\leq 2k-1
follows the fact that each connected component contains at least one
J
-vertex and the connected component of the
j_{\varepsilon,1}
of degree
3
contains at least
2
. It concludes the proof of (
77
) in this case.

Case 2.

|I|=|J|=2k-1
and
\delta_{I}=\delta_{J}=1/2
. In this case, there are two subcases to consider. Set
j_{1}=j_{\varepsilon,1}
and
i_{1}=i_{\varepsilon,1}
. Either the two vertices of degree
4
in
G_{IJ}
(that is
j_{1}
and
i_{1}
) are in the same connected component or not. If they are in the same connected component, then arguing as in case 1, we get

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)=n^{-2k+2}|\sum_{i,j}\prod_{q=1}^{4}(A_{1,q})_{ij}|\prod_{l=2}^{c}|\Tr(A_{l})|

or

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)=n^{-2k+2}|\sum_{i,j}(A_{1,q})_{ii}(A_{2,q})_{jj}\prod_{q=3}^{4}(A_{1,q})_{ij}|\prod_{l=2}^{c}|\Tr(A_{l})|,

with
A_{1,q},A_{l}
as above (the two cases corresponding to the two
4
-regular unlabeled connected multigraphs with
2
vertices). Moreover, each
A_{1,q}
contain
B_{0}
in its product since
\delta_{I}=1
. Hence from (
70
), we find

\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-2k+2}n^{c-1}\leq n^{-1}.

where the inequality
c\leq 2k-2
follows from the fact that each connected component contains one
I
-vertex and the connected component of
j_{1}
contains at least
3
(consider a path from
i_{1}
to
j_{1}
and recall that
(i_{\varepsilon,k},j_{1})
is a
JI
-edge,
k\geq 2
). It concludes the proof of (
77
) in this subcase.

In the other subcase,
j_{1}
and
i_{1}
are not in the same connected component. Then these connected components are composed of two alternating cycles attached to
j_{1}
or
i_{1}
. We obtain the expression:

\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)=n^{-2k+2}|\sum_{j}\prod_{q=1}^{2}(A_{1,q})_{jj}||\sum_{i}\prod_{q=1}^{2}(A_{2,q})_{ii}|\prod_{l=3}^{c}|\Tr(A_{l})|.

Since
\delta_{I}=1
,
A_{1,q}
contains two factor
B_{0}
. From (
77
), we get

\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-2k+2}n^{c-1}\leq n^{-1}.

where the inequality
c\leq 2k-2
follows the fact that each connected component contains at least two
IJ
-edges and two connected components contain at least
4
. It concludes the proof of (
77
) in this case.

Case 3.
The final case to consider is
|I|=|J|=2k
,
\delta_{I}=\delta_{J}=1
, all
JI
-edges have multiplicity
2
. As in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, it suffices to prove that
\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-1}
where
\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)
is obtained from
m_{4}(\pi)
by removing, when summing over
\pi^{\prime}
, the constraints that
i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,t}\neq i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
if
i_{\varepsilon,t}\neq i_{\varepsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
and similarly for the
J
-vertices.
In the graph
G_{IJ}
on
I\sqcup J
spanned by the edges in
E_{IJ}
, all vertices have now degree
2
. The argument leading to (
74
) implies that

\hat{m}_{4}(\pi)\lesssim n^{-2k+2}n^{c^{\prime}},

where
c^{\prime}
is the number of connected components of
G_{IJ}
not containing a boundary vertex
j_{\varepsilon,1},i_{\varepsilon,k}
. To prove (
77
), it is thus sufficient to check that
c^{\prime}\leq 2k-3
. Let
c
be the number of connected components of
G_{IJ}
.

Let us first assume that all vertices
i_{\varepsilon,k}
are in the same connected component. Then
c^{\prime}=c-1
and this connected component has at least
4
,
IJ
-edges. Moreover, as in the proof of case 2 in Lemma
6.1
, the assumptions:
\pi_{1}\neq\pi_{2}
,
\pi_{3}\neq\pi_{4}
imply that there exist two distinct vertices and each adjacent to two distinct
IJ
-edges all distinct. Combining all possibilities and using
k\geq 2
, these
4

IJ
-edges can be chosen to be different from the
4

IJ
-edges attached to
i_{\varepsilon,k}
mentioned above. Since each connected component contains at least
2

IJ
-edges, it implies that
c\leq 2k-2
and thus
c^{\prime}\leq 2k-3
as claimed.

Similarly, if not all vertices
i_{\varepsilon,k}
are in the same connected component then
c^{\prime}\leq c-2
. Then, as in the proof of case 2 in Lemma
6.1
, the bound
c\leq 2k-1
comes from any of the two assumptions
\pi_{1}\neq\pi_{2}
or
\pi_{3}\neq\pi_{4}
.
∎

Our next lemma studies the variance of the variables
S_{k}(B,B)
and
S^{\prime}_{k}(B,B)
defined above Lemma
6.5
. Recall that the expectation of these variables were computed in (
76
).

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer, and
B,B^{\prime}
in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying assumptions (
58
)-(
62
). Set
S_{k}=S_{k}(B,B^{\prime})
and
S^{\prime}_{k}=S^{\prime}_{k}(B,B^{\prime})
. We have

\E[|S_{k}|^{2}]\lesssim 1\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E[|S^{\prime}_{k}|^{2}]\lesssim 1.

Moreover if
k\geq 2
, then

\E[|S_{k}-\E S_{k}|^{2}]\lesssim n^{-1}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E[|S^{\prime}_{k}-\E S^{\prime}_{k}|^{2}]\lesssim n^{-1}.

For
k=1
, we have, with
B_{0}=u\otimes\bar{v}
and
B^{\prime}_{0}=u^{\prime}\otimes\bar{v}^{\prime}
,

\E[|S_{1}-\E S_{1}|^{2}]\lesssim\|u\|_{4}^{2}\|u^{\prime}\|^{2}_{4}\|v\|_{4}^{2}\|v^{\prime}\|_{4}^{2}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E[|S^{\prime}_{1}-\E S^{\prime}_{1}|^{2}]\lesssim\|u\|_{4}^{2}\|u^{\prime}\|^{2}_{4}\|v\|_{4}^{2}\|v^{\prime}\|_{4}^{2}.

Proof.

We start with the first claim. Thanks to Assumption
(H_{4})
, we have

\E|S_{k}|^{2}\vee\E|S^{\prime}_{k}|^{2}\lesssim\sum_{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in Q^{\times 2}_{k}}m_{4}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2}),

where
m_{4}
was defined in (
77
) on
Q^{\times 4}_{k}
. We next remark that

m_{4}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\leq m_{2}(\pi_{1},\pi_{1})m_{2}(\pi_{2},\pi_{2})

where for
m_{2}
was defined in (
72
) on
Q^{\times 2}_{k}
. From (
73
) applied to
(\pi,\pi)
,
\pi\in Q_{k}
, we have
m_{2}(\pi,\pi)\lesssim n^{-k+1}n^{|I|-1}
where
I
is the number of
I
-vertices in
G(\pi)
. Since
|I|\leq k
, we deduce that
m_{2}(\pi,\pi)\lesssim 1
for any
\pi\in Q_{k}
and that

m_{4}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\lesssim 1.

It concludes the proof of the first claim of the lemma.

We now prove the second claim for
k\geq 2
. We treat the case of
S_{k}
, the case of
S^{\prime}_{k}
being identical. For ease of notation, for
\pi\in Q_{k}
, we set

Y_{\pi}=\sum_{l=1}^{k}{{\left(|X_{\pi\backslash(j_{l}i_{l})}|^{2}+|X_{\pi}|^{2}\right)}}.

We have

\E|S_{k}-\E S_{k}|^{2}=\sum_{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in Q^{\times 2}_{k}}|M_{\pi_{1}}|^{2}|M_{\pi_{2}}|^{2}\E\left[(Y_{\pi_{1}}-\E Y_{\pi_{1}})(Y_{\pi_{2}}-\E Y_{\pi_{2}})\right].

In particular, if
E_{JI}(\pi_{1})\cap E_{JI}(\pi_{2})=\emptyset
, then the summand is
0
, where
E_{JI}(\pi_{\varepsilon})
is defined in Lemma
6.1
. We denote by
Q_{k,k}
the subset of
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
in
Q_{k}\times Q_{k}
such that

E_{JI}(\pi_{1})\cap E_{JI}(\pi_{2})\neq\emptyset
. Thanks to Assumption
(H_{4})
, it suffices to prove that for any
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in Q_{k,k}
, we have

m_{4}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\lesssim n^{-1}.

where
m_{4}
was defined in (
77
). Set
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
. The assumption
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in Q_{k,k}
implies that
\pi
has at least one
JI
-edge of multiplicity at least
4
. Hence, counting the sum of multiplicities of
JI
-edges, we get
2(|I|-1)+4\leq 4k
. Hence
|I|\leq 2k-1
the bound
m_{4}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\lesssim n^{-1}
is a consequence of (
78
) and case 2 in the proof of Lemma
6.5
.

We finally consider the case
k=1
. By definition, we simply have

S_{1}=\sum_{i,j}u_{i}\bar{u}^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{j}\bar{v}_{j}(1+|X_{ij}|^{2}).

Hence,

\E|S_{1}-\E S_{1}|^{2}=\E{{\left|\sum_{i,j}u_{i}\bar{u}^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{j}\bar{v}_{j}(|X_{ij}|^{2}-1)\right|}}^{2}=\sum_{i,j}|u_{i}|^{2}|u^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}|v_{j}|^{2}|v^{\prime}_{j}|^{2}\E[(|X_{ij}|^{2}-1)^{2}]=\kappa\|u\cdot u^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}\|v\cdot v^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2},

with
\kappa=\E[(|X_{ij}|^{2}-1))^{2}]
and, for vectors
w,w^{\prime}
,
w\cdot w^{\prime}
is the vector with coordinates for
(w\cdot w^{\prime})_{i}=w_{i}w^{\prime}_{i}
. From Cauchy-Scwharz inequality, the conclusion follows for
S_{1}
. For
S^{\prime}_{1}
, the computation is identical.
∎

Our final technical lemma is a deviation bound of
\hat{L}-L
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer, and
B
in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying assumptions (
58
)-(
62
). Then, for
L=L_{k}(B)
and
\hat{L}=\hat{L}_{k}(B)
, we have for
k\geq 2
,

\E|L-\hat{L}|^{4}\lesssim n^{-3}.

For
k=1
,

\E|L-\hat{L}|^{4}\lesssim n^{-2}\|u\|_{4}^{4}\|v\|_{4}^{4}.

Proof.

We first assume that
k\geq 2
. The proof is again a variant of Lemma
6.1
. Recall our notation: we have
\hat{X}=X^{pq}
and
\hat{L}=L^{pq}
with
(p,q)
uniformly distributed on
\{1,\ldots,n\}^{2}
. As already observed, for any
p,q
, we have

L^{pq}-L=\sum_{\pi\in Q_{k}}M_{\pi}(X^{pq}_{\pi}-X_{\pi})=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{\pi\in Q^{l,pq}_{k}}M_{\pi}(X^{pq}_{\pi}-X_{\pi})=\sum_{l=1}^{k}D^{pq}_{l},

where
Q^{l,pq}_{k}\subset Q_{k}
is the subset of paths
\pi=(j_{1},i_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i_{k})
such that
(p,q)=(j_{l},i_{l})
. From Hölder inequality, we deduce that

|L^{pq}-L|^{4}\leq k^{3}\sum_{l=1}^{k}|D^{pq}_{l}|^{4}.

Hence, to prove the lemma when
k\geq 2
, it is sufficient to prove that for any
1\leq l\leq k
,

(79)

\E[|D^{pq}_{l}|^{4}]\lesssim n^{-3},

where
(p,q)
is uniform on
\{1,\ldots,n\}^{2}
. To this end, we write,

(80)

\E[|D^{pq}_{l}|^{4}]=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{p,q}\sum_{\pi\in(Q^{l,pq}_{k})^{\times 4}}M_{\pi_{1}}M_{\pi_{2}}\bar{M}_{\pi_{3}}\bar{M}_{\pi_{4}}\E\left[(X^{pq}_{\pi_{1}}-X_{\pi_{1}})(X^{pq}_{\pi_{2}}-X_{\pi_{2}})\overline{(X^{pq}_{\pi_{3}}-X_{\pi_{3}})}\overline{(X^{pq}_{\pi_{4}}-X_{\pi_{4}})}\right],

where
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4})
. Next, as already observed, for
\pi\in Q^{l,pq}_{k}
, we have

X^{pq}_{\pi}-X_{\pi}=X_{\pi\backslash pq}(X^{\prime}_{pq}-X_{pq}).

We use the notation of the proof of Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.5
. For
\pi\in Q_{k}^{\times 4}
, we consider the bipartite oriented graph
G(\pi)
with vertex set
I\cup J
and edge sets
E_{IJ}=\cup_{\varepsilon}E_{IJ}(\pi_{\varepsilon})
,
E_{JI}=\cup_{\varepsilon}E_{JI}(\pi_{\varepsilon})
. Due to the independence of the entries of
X
, the expectation in (
80
) is zero unless each edge in
E_{JI}
is visited at least twice. We denote by
\bar{Q}^{l}_{k,k,k,k}\subset Q_{k}^{\times 4}
the collection of elements
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4})
with
\pi_{\varepsilon}=(j_{\varepsilon,1},\ldots,i_{\varepsilon,k})
such that
(j_{\varepsilon,l},i_{\varepsilon,l})=(j_{\varepsilon^{\prime},l},i_{\varepsilon^{\prime},l})
for all
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
and such that each edge in
E_{JI}
is visited at least twice.

We consider the same equivalence class on
Q_{k}^{\times k}
than in the proof of Lemma
6.5
. Since there is a finite number of equivalence classes and the expectation on right-hand side of (
80
) is constant over each equivalence class, to prove (
79
), it is thus sufficient to prove that for any fixed
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4})\in\bar{Q}^{l}_{k,k,k,k}
, we have

(81)

m_{4}(\pi)={{\left|\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\sim\pi}M_{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}M_{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}_{3}}\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}_{4}}\right|}}\lesssim n^{-1}.

where
\pi^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2},\pi^{\prime}_{3},\pi^{\prime}_{4})
. Note that
m_{4}
was already defined in (
77
).

By construction, each edge, say
(j,i)
, in
E_{JI}
has multiplicity at least
2
and
(j_{\varepsilon,l},i_{\varepsilon,l})\in E_{JI}
has multiplicity at least
4
. In particular,
2(|I|-1)+4\leq 4k
and
2(|J|-1)+4\leq 4k
. The bound (
81
) is then a consequence of (
78
) and case 2 in the proof of Lemma
6.5
.

It remains to check the lemma for
k=1
. This is straightforward. We have

L^{pq}-L=\bar{v}_{p}(X^{\prime}_{pq}-X_{pq})u_{q}.

Hence
\E|\hat{L}-L|^{4}=n^{-2}\|u\|_{4}^{4}\|v\|_{4}^{4}\E|X^{\prime}_{11}-X_{11}|^{4}
. It concludes the proof of the lemma. ∎

All technical lemmas are now gathered to prove our main statement.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

By Lemma
6.1
, it suffices to prove the statement with
L=(L_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T}
in place of
Z=(Z_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T}
. We set
W=(W_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T}
,
\tilde{W}=(\tilde{W}_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T}
and
\Delta=\max_{t}\|u_{t}\|_{\infty}\vee\|v_{t}\|_{\infty}
. Note that since
\|u_{t}\|_{2},\|v_{t}\|_{2}\lesssim 1
by assumption (
62
), we have
\|u_{t}\|_{4}\leq(\|u_{t}\|_{\infty}\|u_{t}\|_{2})^{1/2}\lesssim\Delta^{1/2}
.

We start with the case
\Delta=o(1)
. We apply Theorem
7.1
in Appendix with
d=|T|
,
X=L
,
X^{\prime}=\hat{L}
,
\mathcal{F}=\sigma(X)
,
\Lambda=k/n^{2}
and
E_{0}=0
. Then Lemmas
6.3
-
6.7
imply that if
f
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{C}^{T}
,
{{\left|\E f(L)-\E f(W)\right|}}\lesssim n^{2}\times(n^{-5/2}+n^{-9/4}+n^{-2}\Delta^{3})\lesssim n^{-1/4}+\Delta^{3}
, where the constant hidden in
\lesssim
depend on the first three derivatives of
f
,
|T|
and
\E[|X_{ij}|^{4}]
. It concludes the proof of the last claim of the theorem.

We now prove the first claim of the theorem. Let
T_{2}=\{t\in T:k_{t}\geq 2\}
and
T_{1}=T\backslash T_{2}
. The idea to use Theorem
7.1
for elements in
T_{2}
after having resampled a few entries of
X
so that to have removed most of the dependency from
L_{k_{t}}(B_{t})
,
t\in T_{1}
. More precisely, let
X^{\prime\prime}
be an independent copy of
X
and
X^{\prime}
. Let
S\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}^{2}
and define
L^{S}
as the random variable
L=L(X^{S})
where
X^{S}_{ij}=X_{ij}
if
(i,j)\notin S
and
X^{S}_{ij}=X^{\prime\prime}_{ij}
if
(i,j)\in S
.

We write
S=\{(p_{1}q_{1}),\ldots,(p_{m}q_{m})\}
and, set
S_{0}=S
and for
1\leq l\leq|S|
,
S_{l}=\{(p_{1}q_{1}),\ldots,(p_{l}q_{l})\}
. From the triangle inequality, we have, for
L=L_{k_{t}}(B_{t})
,
t\in T_{2}
,

(82)

\E[|L^{S}-L|]\leq\sum_{l=1}^{|S|}\E[|L^{S_{l}}-L^{S_{l-1}}|]\lesssim|S|n^{-1/4},

where the last inequality is a consequence of Lemma
6.7
:
\max_{p,q}\E|L^{pq}-L|^{4}\leq\sum_{p,q}\E|L^{pq}-L|^{4}\lesssim n^{-1}
if
L=L_{k}(B)
with
k\geq 2
.

Now, we set
m=n^{1/5}
and for each
t\in T
, let
I_{t}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
be the
m
-largest coordinate entries of
u_{t}
,
(u_{1,t})_{i}=(u_{t})_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{i\in I_{t}}
and
u_{2,t}=u_{t}-u_{t}
. Since
\|u_{t}\|_{2}\lesssim 1
, we have

\|u_{2,t}\|_{\infty}\leq\|u_{t}\|_{2}/\sqrt{m}\lesssim n^{-1/10}.

We define
J_{t}
and
v_{\varepsilon,t}
similarly with the
m
-largest entries of
v_{t}
. We set
S=\cup_{t}I_{t}\times J_{t}
. From what precedes,
\E[|L^{S}_{k_{t}}(B_{t})-L_{k_{t}}(B_{t})|]\lesssim n^{-1/20}
for all
t\in T_{2}
.

We note also that if
t\in T_{1}
, then
k_{t}=1
by definition For ease of notation, we set
L_{1}(B)=L_{1}(u,v)
. From the bilinearity of the scalar product and the definition of
S
,

L_{1}(B_{t})=L_{1}(u_{t},v_{t})=L_{1}(u_{1,t},v_{1,t})+L^{S}_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{2,t})+L^{S}_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{1,t})+L^{S}_{1}(u_{1,t},v_{2,t}).

We consider the vector

((L^{S}_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T_{2}},(L^{S}_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{2,t}),L^{S}_{1}(u_{1,t},v_{2,t}),L^{S}_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{1,t}))_{t\in T_{1}}).

This vector is independent of
(L_{1}(u_{1,t},v_{1,t}))_{t\in T_{1}}
by construction. Also, as above, we may apply Theorem
7.1
: we deduce from Lemmas
6.3
-
6.7
that this vector is close in distribution to the corresponding Gaussian vector:

(W_{k_{t}}(B_{t}))_{t\in T_{2}},(W_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{2,t}),W_{1}(u_{1,t},v_{2,t}),W_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{1,t}))_{t\in T_{1}}).

It remains to use the bilinearity of
L_{1}
again:
L_{1}(u_{t},v_{t})
is close in distribution to
L_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{2,t})+W_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{2,t})+W_{1}(u_{1,t},v_{2,t})+W_{1}(u_{2,t},v_{1,t})
. It concludes the proof. ∎

6.2
Tightness of long random matrix products

The goal of this subsection is to prove that the random variable
Z=Z_{k}(B)
is tight in
(n,k,B)
under natural assumptions on
B
and as long as
k
is not too large. The main difference with the preceding subsection being that we will now follow more closely the dependence in
k
.

Our next proposition will be quantitative. We make the following assumption on the growth rate of
C_{k}=\E|X_{ij}|^{k}
.

Finally, we will have two type technical conditions which are restrictions due the limitations of our proofs. We will assume either that Assumption
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
holds
or that the matrices
B_{k}
are diagonal:

(83)

\displaystyle\hbox{for all $k\geq 1$, $B_{k}$ is diagonal}.

Our main result in this subsection is the following quantitative estimate.

Proposition 6.1
.

Assume that assumption
(H_{k})
holds and
C_{k}=(\E|X_{ij}|^{k})^{1/k}\leq n^{0.02}
and let
B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that (
58
) holds. We assume also that either
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
or (
83
) holds. Then, there exists a numerical constant
c>0
such that for all
k\leq n^{c}
, we have

\E|Z_{k}(B)|^{2}\leq ck^{12}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{k}\|^{2}.

The proof of Proposition
6.1
starts as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
. The technical challenge is that we now track carefully the dependency
k
, this is where the extra assumptions
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
or (
83
) come into play. When assumption (
83
) holds, Proposition
6.1
is closely related to
[
BC16
, Proposition 6.1]
where the assumptions on
B_{0}
are however stronger. When assumption
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
holds, our proof is significantly more difficult and requires more ideas.

For ease of notation, we set
Z=Z_{k}(B)
. We have

\E[|Z|^{2}]=n^{-k+1}\sum_{\pi\in\bar{P}_{k,k}}M_{\pi_{1}}\bar{M}_{\pi_{2}}\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}],

where
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
with
\pi_{\varepsilon}=(j_{\varepsilon,1},\ldots,i_{\varepsilon,k})
,
\bar{P}_{k,k}\subset P_{k}\times P_{k}
was defined in the proof of Lemma
6.1
below (
71
) and
M_{\pi_{\varepsilon}}
,
X_{\pi_{\varepsilon}}
was defined below (
63
). We recall that
\bar{P}_{k,k}
is the set of
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
such that each
JI
-edge has multiplicity at least two.

We start with the proof of Proposition
6.1
when (
83
) holds.
Recall the definition of equivalence classes on
P_{k}\times P_{k}
introduced in the proof of Lemma
6.1
. Since
\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]
is constant over each equivalent class, we get

(84)

\E[|Z|^{2}]=\sum_{\pi\in\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}}\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]{{\left(\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\sim\pi}M_{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}\right)}},

where
\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}
is the set of equivalence classes in
\bar{P}_{k,k}
. Thanks to assumption (
83
), we may further restrict our attention to
\widetilde{P}_{k,k}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}
defined as the subsets of
\bar{P}_{k,k}
and
\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}
such that
j_{\varepsilon,t+1}=i_{\varepsilon,t}
for all
t\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
,
\varepsilon=1,2
.

For integer
1\leq\ell\leq k
, we denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell)
, the set of
\pi
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}
such its associated bipartite directed graph
G(\pi)
satisfies
|I|\wedge|J|=\ell
. Recall that, by degree counting,
|I|\vee|J|\leq k
for all
\pi\in\bar{P}_{k,k}
. We deduce from (
73
) that, if (
83
) holds,

(85)

\E[|Z|^{2}]\leq\beta^{2k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}n^{\ell-k}\sum_{\pi\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell)}{{\left|\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]\right|}}.

Our first lemma gives a bound
\E[|X_{\pi_{1}}X_{\pi_{2}}|]
, which is classical in random matrix theory, see for example
[
AGZ10
, p24]
. Recall that
C_{k}^{k}=\E|X_{ij}|^{k}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

If
\pi\in\bar{P}_{k,k}
is such that
|E_{JI}|=\ell
,

{{\left|\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]\right|}}\leq C_{k}^{6(k-\ell)}.

Moreover if
\E X_{ij}^{3}=\E X_{ij}\bar{X}_{ij}^{2}=0
then
{{\left|\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]\right|}}\leq C_{k}^{4(k-\ell)}
.

Proof.

If
e=(j,i)\in E_{JI}
, we denote by
m_{e},m_{1,e},m_{2,e}
the multiplicities of
e
in
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
,
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
. We have
m_{e}=m_{1,e}+m_{2,e}\geq 2
. Using the independence of the entries
X_{ij}
and
\E|X_{ij}|^{2}=1
, we get

(86)

{{\left|\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]\right|}}={{\left|\prod_{e\in E_{JI}}\E[X_{e}^{m_{1,e}}\bar{X}_{e}^{m_{2,e}}]\right|}}\leq\prod_{e\in E_{JI}:m_{e}\geq 3}\E[|X_{e}|^{m_{e}}].

Since
m_{e}\leq k
, we deduce from Hölder inequality that

\E[|X_{\pi_{1}}X_{\pi_{2}}|]\leq\prod_{e:m_{e}\geq 3}C_{k}^{m_{e}}.

Finally, we observe that
\sum_{e}m_{e}\mathbbm{1}_{m_{e}\geq 3}=\sum_{e}m_{e}-2\sum_{e}\mathbbm{1}_{m_{e}=2}=2(k-a_{2})
, where
a_{2}
is the number of
JI
-edges of multiplicity
2
. Observe that

2a_{2}+3(a-a_{2})\leq 2k.

In other words,
2(k-a_{2})\leq 6(k-\ell)
. The conclusion follows. If
\E X_{ij}^{3}=\E X_{ij}\bar{X}_{ij}^{2}=0
, we repeat the above argument and use that
m_{e}>2
implies
m_{e}\geq 4
.
∎

Our final lemma for Proposition
6.1
when assumption (
83
) holds is an upper bound on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell)
. A slightly weaker statement is proved in
[
AGZ10
, Lemma 6.1]
.

Lemma 6.9
.

For any integers
1\leq\ell\leq k
, we have

|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell)|\leq 2(2k)^{6(k-\ell)+11}.

Proof.

Up to multiplying by a factor
2
, we give an upper bound on the number of equivalence classes of
\widetilde{P}_{k,k}
with
|J|=\ell
. We equip the set
T=\{1,2\}\times\{1,\ldots,k\}
with the lexicographic order,
(1,1)<(1,2)<\cdots<(1,k)<(2,1)<\cdots<(2,k)
. We think of as an element of
T
as a time, if
t=(\varepsilon,l)
,
l\geq 2
, we write
t-1\in T
for the immediate predecessor of
t
. Recall that
j_{\varepsilon,l+1}=i_{\varepsilon,l}
for
\pi\in\widetilde{P}_{k,k}
, hence
\pi
is uniquely determined by
(j_{t})_{t\in T}
and
i_{\varepsilon,k}
,
\varepsilon=1,2
.

In each equivalence class, we define a canonical element by saying that
\pi
is
canonical
if it is minimal
for the lexicographic order in its equivalence class. For example, if
\pi\in\bar{P}_{k,k}
is canonical then

j_{1,1}=1
,
J=\{1,\ldots,|J|\}
and the
J
vertices appear for the first time in the sequence
(\pi_{t})_{t\in T}
in order (
2
before
3
and so on).
Our goal is then to give an upper bound on the number of canonical elements in
\tilde{P}_{k,k}
with
|J|=\ell
.

Fix such canonical element
\pi=(\pi_{t})_{t\in T}
in
\tilde{P}_{k,k}
. We build a non-decreasing sequence of trees
(\mathcal{T}_{t})
on
J
as follows. Initially, for
t=(1,1)
,
\mathcal{T}_{(1,1)}
has no edge and vertex set
j_{1,1}=\{1\}
. At time
t>(1,1)\in T
, if the addition to
\mathcal{T}_{t-1}
of the oriented edge

(j_{t-1},j_{t})
does not create a cycle, then
\mathcal{T}_{t}
is the tree spanned by
\mathcal{T}_{t}
and
(j_{t-1},j_{t})
, we then say that

(j_{t-1},j_{t})
is a
tree edge
. Otherwise
\mathcal{T}_{t}=\mathcal{T}_{t-1}
. By construction, the vertex set of
\mathcal{T}_{t}
is
J_{t}=\{j_{s}:s\leq t\}=\{1,\ldots,j\}
where
j
is the number of visited
J
-vertices so far (since
\pi
is canonical). In particular,
\mathcal{T}_{(2,k)}
is a spanning tree of
J
, and the number of tree edges is
e=\ell-1
.

Next, we divide the elements of
T
into types. We say that
t\in T
is a
first time
if
\mathcal{T}_{t}=\mathcal{T}_{t-1}\cup(j_{t-1},j_{t})
.
We say that

t\in T
is a
tree time
if
(j_{t-1},j_{t})
is a tree edge of
\mathcal{T}_{t-1}
. Finally, the other times
t\in T
are
called
important times
. We have thus partitioned
T
into first times, tree times, and important times. By construction,
j_{t}\in J_{t-1}
for important and tree times. We also add another special time, the
merging time

m_{\star}
defined as follows. If
J(\pi_{2})\cap J(\pi_{1})=\emptyset
, then
m_{\star}=0
. Otherwise
m_{\star}=(2,l)
where
l
is minimal with the property that
j_{2,l}\in J_{1,k}
. In this last situation, the merging time is a first time.

We also note that all edges are visited at least twice. We deduce that the number of important times is at most

2k-2e=2(k-\ell)+2.

Observe that if
t=(\varepsilon,l)
,
l<k
is a first time and
t\neq m_{\star}
then
t+1
cannot be a tree time. The sequence
(j_{\varepsilon,l}),1\leq l\leq k
, can thus be decomposed as
the successive repetitions of:
(i)
a sequence of first times (possibly empty),
(ii)
an important time or the merging time,

(iii)
a sequence of tree times (possibly empty).

We mark each important time
t=(\varepsilon,l)
by the vector
(j_{t},j_{\varepsilon,\tau-1})
where
\tau>l
is the next time which is not a tree time if this time exists. Otherwise
t=(\varepsilon,l)
is the last important time of
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and we put the mark
(j_{t},j_{\varepsilon,k})
. Similarly, if the merging time
m_{\star}=(2,l)
,
1\leq l\leq k
, we mark it by the vector
(j_{m*},j_{2,\tau-1})
where
\tau>l
is is the next time which is not a tree time if this time exists or otherwise we mark it by
(j_{m*},j_{2,k})
.

We claim that the canonical sequence
\pi
is
uniquely determined by
i_{1,k},i_{2,k}
, the positions of the important times, their marks, and the value of the merging time
m_{\star}
and its mark. Indeed, if
t=(\varepsilon,l)
is an important time or the merging time, the sequence
(j_{\varepsilon,l},\ldots,j_{\varepsilon,\tau-1})
is the unique path in
\mathcal{T}_{t}
from
j_{\varepsilon,l}
to
j_{\varepsilon,\tau-1}
(there is a unique path between two vertices of a tree). Moreover, if
\sigma\geq\tau
is the next important time, merging time or
\sigma=k+1
if
t
is the last important time of
\pi_{\varepsilon}
,
(\varepsilon,\tau),\ldots,(\varepsilon,\sigma-1)
is a sequence of first times (if
\tau=\sigma
this sequence is empty). It follows that if
j=|J_{t}|
, we have
j_{\varepsilon,\tau+s-1}=j+s
, for
s=1,\cdots,\sigma-\tau
, by the minimality of canonical paths.

There are at most
2k
possibilities for the position of an important time and the value of the merging time and
\ell^{2}
possibilities for its mark. In particular, the number of distinct canonical paths in
\widetilde{P}_{k,k}

with
|J|=\ell
is bounded by

k^{2}(2\ell^{3})^{2(k-\ell)+3},

(the factor
k^{2}
is front accounts for the possibilities for
(i_{1,k},i_{2,k})
). It gives the requested bound. ∎

We are ready for the proof of Proposition
6.1
when assumption (
83
) holds.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
when assumption (
83
) holds.

By Lemma
6.8
, if
\pi\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell)
, we have

{{\left|\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]\right|}}\leq C_{k}^{6(k-\ell)},

(indeed, since each
I
vertex is preceded by an edge in
E_{JI}
, we have
|E_{JI}|\geq\ell
and similarly for
J
). It follows from (
85
) and Lemma
6.9
that

\E[|Z|^{2}]\leq 2(2k)^{11}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{k}\|^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\varrho^{k-\ell},

where

\varrho=\frac{C_{k}^{6}(2k)^{6}}{n}.

Thanks to the assumption
C_{k}\leq n^{0.02}
, we have
\varrho\leq 1/2
for all
n
large enough and
k\leq n^{c_{1}}
for
c_{1}>0
small enough,. The conclusion follows for all
n
large enough. For smaller
n
, the conclusion of the proposition trivially holds if the constant
c
is large enough. ∎

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
6.1
when
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
holds. The upper bound (
73
) is not sufficient when the matrices
B_{k}
are not diagonal, essentially because the number of equivalence classes is much larger than the equivalence classes considered in Lemma
6.9
.

Let
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in P_{k}\times P_{k}
with associated graph
G=G(\pi)
. Observe from (
86
) that
\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]
depends only on the multiplicities
(m_{1,e},m_{2,e})
of each
JI
-edge
e
of
G
. We introduce a new equivalence relation on
P_{k}\times P_{k}
, we write
\pi\hat{\sim}\pi^{\prime}
if there exists a bijection
\psi
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}^{2}
such that
\psi(j_{\varepsilon,t},i_{\varepsilon,t})=(j^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,t},i^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,t})
for all
(\varepsilon,t)
. By construction, this equivalence relation is coarser that the equivalence relation
\sim
and
\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]
is constant over each equivalence class.
We rewrite (
84
) as

\E[|Z|^{2}]=\sum_{\pi\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}}\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]{{\left(\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\hat{\sim}\pi}M_{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}\right)}},

where
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
with
\pi_{\varepsilon}=(j_{\varepsilon,1},\ldots,i_{\varepsilon,k})
,
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}
is the set of equivalence classes of elements in
P_{k}\times P_{k}
such that each
JI
-edge has even multiplicity (otherwise
\E[X_{\pi_{1}}\bar{X}_{\pi_{2}}]
is zero by assumption
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
and the independence of entries).

We next introduce
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)\subset\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}
as the set of elements
\pi
such that
G=G(\pi)
has
|E_{JI}|=\ell
distinct
JI
-edges and the graph
G_{IJ}
spanned by
IJ
-edges has
c
connected components (beware that
\ell
is not
|I|\wedge|J|
contrary to as above). Observe that
c\leq\ell
(in each connected component, pick a distinguished
I
-vertex, this vertex is neighbor to a
JI
-edge). By Lemma
6.8
, we arrive at

(87)

\E[|Z|^{2}]\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{c=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\pi\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)}C_{k}^{4(k-\ell)}{{\left|\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\hat{\sim}\pi}M_{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{M}_{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}\right|}}.

The graph
G_{JI}=(I\sqcup J,E_{JI})
is a multigraph with
2k
oriented edges. If
e=(i_{\varepsilon,t},j_{\varepsilon,t+1})\in E_{JI}
, we set
M_{e}=B_{t}
if
\varepsilon=1
and
M_{e}=\bar{B}_{t}
otherwise, we also set
e_{-}=i_{\varepsilon,t}
,
e_{+}=j_{\varepsilon,t+1}
. We may then write

\mu_{2}(\pi)=\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\hat{\sim}\pi}M_{\pi_{1}}\bar{M}_{\pi_{2}}=n^{-k+1}\sum_{\bm{i}^{\prime},\bm{j}^{\prime}}\prod_{e\in E_{IJ}}(M_{e})_{i^{\prime}_{e_{-}}j^{\prime}_{e_{+}}},

where the sum is over all
\bm{i}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in I}
,
\bm{j}^{\prime}=(j^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in J}
, with
i^{\prime}_{u},j^{\prime}_{v}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and for all
(\varepsilon_{1},t_{1})
,
(\varepsilon_{2},t_{2})
,
(j^{\prime}_{j_{\varepsilon_{1},t_{1}}},i^{\prime}_{i_{\varepsilon_{1},t_{1}}})\neq(j^{\prime}_{j_{\varepsilon_{2},t_{2}}},i^{\prime}_{i_{\varepsilon_{2},t_{2}}})
if
(j_{\varepsilon_{1},t_{1}},i_{\varepsilon_{1},t_{1}})\neq(j_{\varepsilon_{2},t_{2}},i_{\varepsilon_{2},t_{2}})
(this is the constraint imposed by the equivalence relation
\hat{\sim}
).

We set

\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)=n^{-k+1}\sum_{\bm{i}^{\prime},\bm{j}^{\prime}}\prod_{e\in E_{IJ}}(M_{e})_{i^{\prime}_{e_{-}}j^{\prime}_{e_{+}}},

where the sum is over all
\bm{i}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in I}
,
\bm{j}^{\prime}=(j^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in J}
, with
i^{\prime}_{u},j^{\prime}_{v}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, if we remove iteratively the constraints
(j^{\prime}_{j_{\varepsilon_{1},t_{1}}},i^{\prime}_{i_{\varepsilon_{1},t_{1}}})\neq(j^{\prime}_{j_{\varepsilon_{2},t_{2}}},i^{\prime}_{i_{\varepsilon_{2},t_{2}}})
if
(j_{\varepsilon_{1},t_{1}},i_{\varepsilon_{1},t_{1}})\neq(j_{\varepsilon_{2},t_{2}},i_{\varepsilon_{2},t_{2}})
, we deduce that

\mu_{2}(\pi)=\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)+\sum_{\pi^{\prime}<\pi}\pm\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi^{\prime}),

where the sum is over all
\pi^{\prime}\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell^{\prime},c)
,
\ell^{\prime}>\ell
is obtained from
\pi
choosing a collection of edges in
E_{JI}
and gluing them (that is, identify their end vertices).

We thus obtain from (
87
) that

\E[|Z|^{2}]\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{c=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\pi\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)}C_{k}^{4(k-\ell)}L(\pi){{\left|\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)\right|}},

where
L(\pi)
is the number of
\pi^{\prime}
which in their iterative decomposition from
\mu_{2}(\pi^{\prime})
to
\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi^{\prime})
have a term
\pm\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)
. Note that

L(\pi)\leq\prod_{e\in E_{JI}}K(m_{e}),

where
m_{e}\in 2\mathbb{N}
is the multiplicity
e\in E_{JI}
and
K(m)
is the number of ways to partition
\{1,\ldots,m\}
into blocks of even size. We have
K(2)=1
and for
m\geq 4
,

K(m)\leq\binom{m}{m/2}\mathcal{B}_{m/2},

where
\mathcal{B}_{k}
is the
k
-th Bell’s number (number of partitions of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
). Since
\mathcal{B}_{k}\leq k^{k}
and
m/2\leq m-2
for
m\geq 4
, we get

K(m)\leq(2m)^{m-2}.

Since
m\leq 2k
, we obtain the rough upper bound:

L(\pi)\leq(4k)^{\sum_{e}(m_{e}-2)}=(4k)^{2(k-\ell)}.

We arrive at the expression

\E[|Z|^{2}]\leq\sum_{m=1}^{2k}\sum_{c=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\pi\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)}{{\left(4kC^{2}_{k}\right)}}^{2(k-\ell)}{{\left|\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)\right|}}.

The reason we have introduced the equivalence relation
\hat{\sim}
was to get an upper bound on
L(\pi)
where in the exponent
k-\ell
appears, for the equivalence relation
\sim
the exponent would have been too large for large
\ell
(of order
\ell
).

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
p=1/2
. For any
\pi\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)
, we have

|\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)|\leq n^{c+p(k-\ell)-k}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{k}\|^{2}.

Proof.

We note that
n^{k-1}\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)
is equal to a product over the connected components of
G_{IJ}
. From assumption
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
, all vertices of the oriented graph
G_{IJ}
have even degrees. We may thus apply Theorem
7.2
(in Subsection
7.4
of the Appendix), it implies that

(88)

n^{k-1}{{\left|\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)\right|}}\leq\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{k}\|^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{c}n^{1-\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\delta_{i}>0}}{2}},

where
\delta_{i}\in\{0,1,2\}
is the number of matrices
B_{0}
in this connected component. Note that we arrive at the claim of the lemma. We get

{{\left|\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)\right|}}\leq n^{c+1/2-k}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{k}\|^{2}.

In particular, if
\ell<k
, we obtain the claimed statement since
(k-\ell)/2\leq 1/2
. It remains to check the lemma when
\ell=k
. In this case, all vertices of
G(\pi)
have degree
2
. It follows that each weak connected component of
G(\pi)
is a cycle. In particular
n^{k-1}\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)
is a product of traces of the
B_{t}
’s or their transposes. Since
\Tr(M)\leq\mathrm{rank}(M)\|M\|
, the statement is straighforward.
∎

We have thus proved so far that

(89)

\E[|Z|^{2}]\leq\left(\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{k}\|^{2}\right)\sum_{m=1}^{2k}\sum_{c=1}^{\ell}|\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)|{{\left(4kC^{2}_{k}\right)}}^{2(k-\ell)}n^{c+p(k-\ell)-k}.

Our final lemma is an upper bound on
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)
.

Lemma 6.11
.

For any integers
1\leq c\leq\ell\leq k
, we have

|\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)|\leq(2k)^{12(k-\ell)+4(\ell-c)+12}.

Proof.

The proof is a variant of the proof Lemma
6.9
. We equip
T=\{1,2\}\times\{1,\ldots,k\}
with the lexicographic order. We write
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in\hat{P}_{k,k}
,
\pi_{\varepsilon}=(j_{\varepsilon,1},\ldots,i_{\varepsilon,k})
as
\pi=(\pi_{t})_{t\in T}
with
\pi_{t}=(j_{\varepsilon,l},i_{\varepsilon,l})
for
t=(\varepsilon,l)
. We say that
\pi\in\hat{P}_{k,k}
is
canonical
, if its distinct
JI
-edges are
e_{1}=(1,1),e_{2}=(2,2),\ldots,e_{\ell}=(\ell,\ell)
where
\ell=|E_{JI}|
and if these edges are visited for the first time in increasing order. By construction, there is a unique canonical element in each equivalence class for
\hat{\sim}
. For any canonical element
\pi\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k,k}(\ell,c)
, we have
E_{JI}=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}\}
. The set
C=\{1,\ldots,c\}
is thought as the set of connected components of
G_{IJ}
ordered by the order of appearance of its
I
-vertices. For each
t\in T
, we denote by
c_{t}\in C
the index of the connected component of
i_{t}
.

Similarly to the proof of Lemma
6.9
, we build an non-decreasing sequence of edge-colored spanning trees
\mathcal{T}_{t}
,
t\in T
, on the vertex set
C_{t}=\{c_{s}:s\leq t\}
. A colored edge from
p
to
q
is a triple
(p,e,q)
with
p,q\in C
and
e\in E_{JI}
. At time
t=(1,1)
,
\mathcal{T}_{(1,1)}
has no edge and vertex set
c_{1,1}=\{1\}
. At time
t>(1,1)\in T
, if the addition to
\mathcal{T}_{t-1}
of the oriented edge

(c_{t-1},c_{t})
does not create a cycle, then
\mathcal{T}_{t}
is
\mathcal{T}_{t-1}\cup(c_{t-1},\pi_{t-1},c_{t})
, we then say that

(c_{t-1},\pi_{t-1},c_{t})
is a
tree edge
. Otherwise
\mathcal{T}_{t}=\mathcal{T}_{t-1}
. By construction, the vertex set of
\mathcal{T}_{t}
is
C_{t}
. In particular,
\mathcal{T}_{(2,k)}
is a spanning tree of
C
, and the number of tree edges is
c-1
.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.9
, we divide the elements of
T
into types. We say that
t\in T
is a
first time
if
\mathcal{T}_{t}=\mathcal{T}_{t-1}\cup(c_{t-1},\pi_{t-1},c_{t})
.
We say that

t\in T
is a
tree time
if
(c_{t-1},\pi_{t-1},c_{t})
is a tree edge of
\mathcal{T}_{t-1}
. The other times
t\in T
are
called
important times
. Finally, the
merging time

m_{\star}
is
0
if
E_{JI}(\pi_{2})\cap E_{JI}(\pi_{1})=\emptyset
, otherwise
m_{\star}=(2,l)
where
l
is minimal with the property that
c_{2,l}\in\Gamma_{1,k}

As in the proof of Lemma
6.9
, the sequence
(\pi_{\varepsilon,l}),1\leq l\leq k
, can be decomposed as
the successive repetitions of:
(i)
a sequence of first times (possibly empty),
(ii)
an important time or the merging time,

(iii)
a sequence of tree times (possibly empty).

We mark each important time
t=(\varepsilon,l)
by the vector
(\pi_{t-1},c_{t},c_{\varepsilon,\tau-1})
where
\tau>l
is the next time which is not a tree time if this time exists. Otherwise
t=(\varepsilon,l)
is the last important time of
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and we put the mark
(\pi_{t-1},c_{t},c_{\varepsilon,k})
. Similarly, if the merging time
m_{\star}=(2,l)
,
1\leq l\leq k
, we mark it by the vector
(\pi_{m*-1},c_{m^{*}},c_{2,\tau-1})
where
\tau>l
is is the next time which is not a tree time if this time exists or otherwise we mark it by
(\pi_{m*-1},c_{m^{*}},c_{2,k})
.

Arguing exactly as in the proof of Lemma
6.9
, the canonical sequence
\pi
is uniquely determined by the positions of the important times, their marks, and the value of the merging time
m_{\star}
and its mark.

We are left with the task of giving an upper bound on the number of important times. Assume that
(c_{t-1},\pi_{t-1},c_{t})
is a tree edge and
c_{t-1}=p\in C
,
\pi_{t-1}=e_{i}=(i,i)
,
\pi_{t}=e_{j}=(j,j)
. Then,
i\neq j
are in
p
-th connected component of
G_{IJ}
. In particular, if
j
is the only
J
-vertex in the
p
-th connected component of
G_{IJ}
and since
e_{i}
is visited at least twice, the tree edge
(c_{t-1},\pi_{t-1},c_{t})
will be visited at least twice (indeed, otherwise,
(e_{i},e_{j^{\prime}})=(\pi_{s-1},\pi_{s})
with
s\neq t
and
j\neq j^{\prime}
and we would have
(i,j,j^{\prime})
in the same connected component of
G_{IJ}
). Let
\kappa_{2}
be the number of connected components of
G_{IJ}
which have at least two distinct
J
-vertices. It follows that the number of tree edges which are visited at least twice is
t_{2}\geq c-1-\kappa_{2}
.

If
n_{p}
is the number of
J
-vertices in the
p
-th connected component of
G_{IJ}
, we have

|J|-c=\sum_{p}(n_{p}-1)\geq\kappa_{2}.

As already pointed, we have
|J|\leq\ell
. It follows that
\kappa_{2}\leq\ell-c
and
t_{2}\geq 2c-\ell-1
. Let
t_{1}
be the number of tree edges visited once. We have
t_{2}+t_{1}=c-1
. the number of important times is at most

2k-2t_{2}-t_{1}=2k-t_{2}-c+1\leq 2k+\ell-3c+2.

Now, there are at most
2k
possibilities for the position of an important time and the value of the merging time and
c^{2}\ell
possibilities for its mark. In particular, the number of distinct canonical paths in
\widetilde{P}_{k,k}

with
\ell=|E_{JI}|
and
c
connected components in
G_{IJ}
is upper bounded by

(2kc^{2}\ell)^{2k+\ell-3c+3}.

It gives the requested bound. ∎

Proof of Proposition
6.1
when assumption
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
holds.

From (
89
) and Lemma
6.11
, we have

(90)

\E[|Z|^{2}]\leq\left(\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\|B_{k}\|^{2}\right)(2k)^{12}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{c=1}^{\ell}\varrho_{1}^{k-\ell}\varrho_{2}^{\ell-c},

where

\varrho_{1}=\frac{(4kC^{2}_{k})^{2}(2k)^{12}}{n^{1-p}}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\varrho_{2}=\frac{(2k)^{4}}{n}.

Thanks to assumption
C_{k}\leq n^{0.02}
,
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
are less than
1/2
for all
n
large enough and
k\leq n^{c_{1}}
with
c_{1}>0
small enough. The conclusion follows.
∎

We conclude this subsection with a variant of Proposition
6.1
which will be useful in the sequel. We introduce the normalized Frobenius norm: for
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
,

\|M\|_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\Tr(MM^{*})}.

We have
\|M\|_{2}\leq\|M\|\leq\sqrt{n}\|M\|_{2}
. We consider the assumption : for some
0<\varepsilon<1/2
,

(91)

\hbox{for all $k\geq 1$ : }\|B_{k}\|\leq n^{\varepsilon}\|B_{k}\|_{2}.

We have the following strengthening of Proposition
6.1
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Assume that assumption
(H_{k})
holds and
C_{k}=(\E|X_{ij}|^{k})^{1/k}\leq n^{0.02}
and let
B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that (
58
) and (
91
) hold for some
\varepsilon>0
. We assume also that either
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
or (
83
) holds. Then, there exists a numerical constant
c>0
such that, if
\varepsilon<1/c
, for all
k\leq n^{c}
, we have

\E|Z_{k}(B)|^{2}\leq ck^{12}\|B_{0}\|^{2}\prod_{t=1}^{k-1}\|B_{t}\|^{2}_{2}.

Proof.

We only give the proof when assumption
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
holds. The proof extends immediately to the simpler case when (
83
) holds. The only difference with Proposition
6.1
lies in the application of Lemma
6.10
. As pointed in the proof of Lemma
6.10
,
n^{k-1}\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)
is equal to a product over the connected components of
G_{IJ}
, say

n^{k-1}\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)=\prod_{i=1}^{c}L_{i}.

Since all degrees in
G_{IJ}
are at least two, each connected component has at least two (oriented) edges. If the
i
-th connected component has exactly two edges, say
e,f
then, we may directly upper bound
L_{i}
by

\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}|(M_{e})_{jj^{\prime}}(M_{f})_{jj^{\prime}}|\leq\sqrt{\Tr(M_{e}M_{e}^{*})\Tr(M_{f}M_{f}^{*})},

where the inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. If
M_{e}\notin\{B_{0},\bar{B}_{0}\}
, we use
\Tr(M_{e}M_{e}^{*})\leq n\|M_{e}\|_{2}^{2}
. If
M_{e}\in\{B_{0},\bar{B}_{0}\}
, thanks to (
58
), we have

\Tr(B_{0}B_{0}^{*})=\|B_{0}\|^{2}.

If
L_{i}
has more than
2
edges, we apply Theorem
7.2
instead. Therefore, using (
91
), Equation (
88
) is improved in

n^{k-1}{{\left|\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)\right|}}\leq n^{\varepsilon m}\|B_{0}\|^{2}\prod_{t=1}^{k-1}\|B_{t}\|^{2}_{2}\prod_{i=1}^{c}n^{1-\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\delta_{i}>0}}{2}},

where
m
is the number of edges
e
of
G_{IJ}
such that
M_{e}\notin\{B_{0},\bar{B}_{0}\}
and which are in a connected component with more than two edges. Consequently, the proof of Lemma
6.10
gives the improved bound

|\hat{\mu}_{2}(\pi)|\leq n^{c+p(k-\ell)-k}n^{\varepsilon m}\|B_{0}\|^{2}\prod_{t=1}^{k-1}\|B_{t}\|^{2}_{2}.

We next claim that

(92)

m\leq 6(k-c).

Indeed, let
m^{\prime}\geq m
be the number of edges of
G_{IJ}
which are in a connected component with more than two edges. Let
c_{2}
(resp.
c_{3}
) be the number of connected components with two edges (resp. at least
3
). We have

c_{2}+c_{>2}=c\quad\hbox{ and }\quad 2c_{2}+3c_{>2}\leq 2k.

(thanks to the symmetry assumption
(H_{\mathrm{sym}})
the factor
3c_{>2}
could in fact be improved in
4c_{>2}
). We deduce that
c_{2}\geq 3c-2k
. Since
m^{\prime}=2k-2c_{2}
, we deduce (
92
). We write
k-c=(k-\ell)+(\ell-c)
and then (
90
) becomes

\E[|Z|^{2}]\leq\left(\|B_{0}\|^{2}\prod_{t=1}^{k-1}\|B_{t}\|^{2}_{2}\right)\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{c=1}^{\ell}\varrho_{1}^{k-\ell}\varrho_{2}^{\ell-c},

where

\varrho_{1}=\frac{(4kC^{2}_{k})^{2}(2k)^{12}n^{6\varepsilon}}{n^{1-p}}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\varrho_{2}=\frac{(2k)^{4}n^{6\varepsilon}}{n}.

The conclusion follows.
∎

7
Appendix

7.1
Random analytic functions

We recall here some results from Shirai
[
Shi12
]
. Let
D\subset\mathbb{C}
be a bounded connected open set in
the complex plane. Denote by
{\mathcal{H}}(D)
the space of complex analytic functions in
D
, endowed
with the distance

(93)

d(f,g)=\sum_{j\geq 1}2^{-j}\frac{\|f-g\|_{K_{j}}}{1+\|f-g\|_{K_{j}}},

where
(K_{j})_{j\geq 1}
is an exhaustion by compact sets of
D
and
\|f-g\|_{K_{j}}
denotes the supremum norm of
f-g
on
K_{j}
. We recall that it is a complete separable metric space.

The space
{\mathcal{H}}(D)
is equipped with the (topological)
Borel
\sigma
-field
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}(D))
and the set of probability measures on
({\mathcal{H}}(D),{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}(D)))
is
denoted by
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{H}}(D))
.
By
a random analytic function on D
we mean an
{\mathcal{H}}(D)
-valued
random variable on a probability space. The probability
law of a random analytic function is uniquely determined by its finite dimensional
distributions.

The following proposition from
[
Shi12
]
provides a typical example of random analytic functions.

Proposition 7.1

(Prop. 2.1 in
[
Shi12
]
)
.

Let
\{\Psi_{k}\}_{k}\subset{\mathcal{H}}(D)
be a sequence of independent centered random analytic functions defined on the same probability space. Suppose that
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(|\Psi_{k}|^{2}\right)

is a locally integrable function of
z
in
D
. Then,

X(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Psi_{k}(z)
is convergent in
{\mathcal{H}}(D)
almost surely and thus defines a random analytic function on
D
.

The following lemmas from
[
Shi12
]
turn out to be useful to prove tightness results.

Proposition 7.2

(Prop. 2.5 in
[
Shi12
]
)
.

Let
(f_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of random analytic functions in
D
. If
(\|f_{n}\|_{K})_{n}
is tight for any compact set K, then the sequence of distributions of
f_{n}
,
({\mathcal{L}}(f_{n}))_{n}
, is
tight in
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{H}}(D))
.

Lemma 7.1

(Lemma 2.6 in
[
Shi12
]
)
.

For any compact set K in D, there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(D)
,

\left\|f\right\|_{K}^{p}\leq(\pi\delta^{2})^{-1}\int_{\overline{K_{\delta}}}\left|f(z)\right|^{p}m(dz),

for any
p>0
, where
\overline{K_{\delta}}\subset D
is the closure of the
\delta
-neighborhood of K and
m
denotes the Lebesgue measure.

Using that, by Markov’s inequality, for any
C>0
and any
p>0
,

(94)

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{K}>C\right)\leq\frac{1}{C^{p}}\mathbb{E}\left(\left\|f_{n}\right\|^{p}_{K}\right),

this leads to the following tightness criterion of sequence of random analytic functions.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{C}
be an open connected set in the complex plane.
Let
K\subset D
be a compact set and
U\subset K
be an open set. Let
(f_{n})
be a sequence of random analytic functions on
D
. If there exists
p>0
and
C>0
such that for all large
n
,
\sup_{z\in K}\E|f_{n}(z)|^{p}<C
then
(f_{n})
is a tight sequence of random analytic functions on
U
.

Let us first recall some basic facts on point processes (we refer to Daley and Vere-Jones
[
DVJ03
]
for details and terminology).

Define
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
the set of boundedly finite Borel measures
\mu
on
\mathbb{C}
, that is
\mu(A)<\infty
for every bounded Borel set
A
. We endow
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
with the usual weak topology:
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
if for all continuous function
f
with compact support,
\int fd\mu_{n}\to\int fd\mu
(see
[
DVJ03
, Prop. A.2.6.II]
). The space
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
is a complete separable metric space (see
[
DVJ03
, Thm. A.2.6.III]
). The set
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C}))
of probability measures on
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
is a complete separable metric space and the Lévy-Prokhorov distance is a metric for the weak convergence of measures in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C}))
.

For instance, for any Borel set
B
, and any random matrix
M
,

\sum_{\lambda\in\spectrum M\cap B}\delta_{\lambda}

is an example of a
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
-valued random variable.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{C}
be an open connected set.
For a nonzero analytic function
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(D)
, we denote by
Z_{f}
the set of zeroes of
f
and define

\xi_{f}=\sum_{z\in Z_{f}}m_{z}\delta_{z}

where
m_{z}
is the multiplicity of a zero
z
. If moreover
f
is random, then
\xi_{f}
is
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
-valued.

The following proposition is fundamental for our purpose.

Proposition 7.3

(Prop. 2.3 in
[
Shi12
]
)
.

Suppose that a sequence of random analytic functions
\{X_{n}\}
converges in law to
X
in
\mathcal{H}(D)
. Then the zero process
\xi_{X_{n}}
converges in law to
\xi_{X}
provided that
X\neq 0
almost surely, i.e. for all continuous function
\varphi
with compact support in
D
,

\int\varphi d\xi_{X_{n}}\overset{\text{weakly}}{\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\int\varphi d\xi_{X}.

Following the terminology of Hough, Krishnapur, Peres, Virág
[
HKPV09
]
, we say that
g
is a
Gaussian analytic function
on a domain
D
, if
g
is a random analytic function on
D
,
(g(z),z\in D)
is a Gaussian process and for
z,w\in D
,
\mathbb{E}(g(z)g(w))=0
. The distribution of a Gaussian analytic function is characterized by its kernel
K(z,w)=\mathbb{E}(g(z)\overline{g(w)})
(see
[
HKPV09
, Chapter 2]
). The Edelman-Kostlan’s formula takes a particularly simple form for Gaussian analytic functions: the intensity of zeros of
g
is given by
\frac{1}{4\pi}\Delta\ln K(z,z)
, where
\Delta
is the usual Laplacian.

Remark 7.1
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer. By taking the product topology on
M_{d}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{C}^{d^{2}}
, the topologies defined on
\mathcal{H}(D)
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}(D))
extend immediately for the set
\mathcal{H}_{d}(D)
of complex analytic functions
D\to M_{d}(\mathbb{C})
.

7.2
Stein’s method of exchangeable pairs

We use the following normal approximation theorem which relies on Stein’s method of exchangeable pairs. It is a straightforward extension of
[
Mec09
, Theorem 3]
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
be a centered complex random variable and
X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
such that
(X,X^{\prime})
and
(X^{\prime},X)
have the same law.

We denote by
Z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
be the centered complex Gaussian vector whose covariance is characterized by
\E[ZZ^{*}]=\Sigma_{1}
and
\E[ZZ^{\intercal}]=\Sigma_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
\sigma
-subalgebra of our underlying probability space such that
X
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\Lambda\in M_{d}(\mathbb{C})
be an invertible matrix. Assume that, for some
\mathcal{F}
-measurable
E_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
and
E_{1},E_{2}\in M_{d}(\mathbb{C})
,

\E[X^{\prime}-X|\mathcal{F}]=-\Lambda X+E_{0},

\E[(X^{\prime}-X)(X^{\prime}-X)^{*}|\mathcal{F}]=2\Lambda\Sigma_{1}+E_{1}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E[(X^{\prime}-X)(X^{\prime}-X)^{\intercal}|\mathcal{F}]=2\Lambda\Sigma_{2}+E_{2}.

Then for any function
f\in C^{3}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
,

\displaystyle|\E f(X)-\E f(Z)|\leq

\displaystyle\|\Lambda^{-1}\|\left(M_{1}(f)\E\|E_{0}\|_{2}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{4}M_{2}(f)(\E{{\left\|E_{1}\right\|}}_{\mathrm{{\tiny{HS}}}}+\E{{\left\|E_{2}\right\|}}_{\mathrm{{\tiny{HS}}}})+M_{3}(f)\E\|X-X^{\prime}\|_{2}^{3}\right).

where for integer
k\geq 1
,
M_{k}(f)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{C}^{n}}\|D^{k}f(x)\|
and
D^{k}
denotes the
k
-th derivative.

Proof.

If
X
is real-valued then the claim is contained in
[
Mec09
, Theorem 3]
. In the general complex case, we associate to
X
the real vector of dimension
2d
,
Y=(\Re(X),\Im(X))
of covariance matrix

\Sigma=\E[YY^{\intercal}]=\begin{pmatrix}\E[\Re(X)\Re(X)^{\intercal}]&\E[\Re(X)\Im(X)^{\intercal}]\\
\E[\Im(X)\Re(X)^{\intercal}]&\E[\Im(X)\Im(X)^{\intercal}]\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{11}&\Sigma_{12}\\
\Sigma_{21}&\Sigma_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

By construction, we have
\Sigma_{11}=\Re(\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2})/2
,
\Sigma_{22}=\Re(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})/2
and
\Sigma_{21}=\Sigma_{12}^{\intercal}=\Im(\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2})/2
. Similarly, we have

\E[Y^{\prime}-Y|\mathcal{F}]=-\Lambda^{\prime}Y+E^{\prime}_{0},\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\E[(Y^{\prime}-Y)(Y^{\prime}-Y)^{\intercal}|\mathcal{F}]=2\Lambda^{\prime}\Sigma+E,

with

\Lambda^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\Re(\Lambda)&-\Im(\Lambda)\\
\Im(\Lambda)&\Re(\Lambda)\end{pmatrix}\;,\quad E^{\prime}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\Re(E_{0})\\
\Im(E_{0})\end{pmatrix}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad E=\begin{pmatrix}E_{11}&E_{12}\\
E_{21}&E_{22}\end{pmatrix},

where
E_{11}=\Re(E_{1}+E_{2})/2
,
E_{22}=\Re(E_{1}-E_{2})/2
and
E_{21}=E_{12}^{\intercal}=\Im(E_{1}+E_{2})/2
. The map
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}:z\mapsto(\Re(z),\Im(z))
being an isometry, we find that
\|\Lambda^{\prime-1}\|=\|\Lambda^{-1}\|
,
\|E^{\prime}_{0}\|_{2}=\|E_{0}\|_{2}
and
2{{\left\|E\right\|}}_{\mathrm{{\tiny{HS}}}}^{2}={{\left\|E_{1}\right\|}}_{\mathrm{{\tiny{HS}}}}^{2}+{{\left\|E_{2}\right\|}}_{\mathrm{{\tiny{HS}}}}^{2}
. It then remains to apply
[
Mec09
, Theorem 3]
to
Y
.
∎

7.3
Convergence of Riemann sum of rational functions

In this paragraph, we prove the following improvement on classical rates for the convergence of Riemann sums.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
P,Q
be polynomials in
\mathbb{C}[X]
. Denote by
z_{i}
’s the roots of
Q
,
m_{i}
their multiplicities and set
\delta_{i}=\min(|z_{i}|,1/|z_{i}|)
. Then, if
\max_{i}\delta_{i}<1
, for some constant
C>0
(depending only on the degrees of
P,Q
), for all integers
n\geq\max_{i}m_{i}
,

{{\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{P(e^{\frac{2\mathrm{i}\pi k}{n}})}{Q(e^{\frac{2\mathrm{i}\pi k}{n}})}-\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}\pi}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{P(\omega)}{\omega Q(\omega)}d\omega\right|}}\leq C\max_{i}\left(\frac{n^{m_{i}-1}\delta_{i}^{n}}{\min(|z_{i}|,|z_{i}|^{m_{i}})(1-\delta_{i})}\right).

Proof.

We set
\omega_{n}=e^{\frac{2\mathrm{i}\pi}{n}}
,

I_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{P(\omega_{n}^{k})}{Q(\omega_{n}^{k})}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad I=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}\pi}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{P(\omega)}{\omega Q(\omega)}d\omega=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{P(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})}{Q(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})}d\theta.

We can decompose
P/Q
into simple elements and use the linearity of sums and integrals. To prove the proposition, it follows that it is sufficient to check the claim in the following cases, for some integer
l\geq q\geq 0
,

(i)

P(x)=x^{l},Q(x)=1
,

(ii)

P(x)=x^{q}
,
Q(x)=(z-x)^{l+1}
with
|z|>1
,

(iii)

P(x)=x^{q}
,
Q(x)=(z-x)^{l+1}
with
|z|<1
(and for
z=0
for
q=0
).

Case (i) is trivial. If
l=0
,
I_{n}=I=1
, if
l\geq 1
,
I_{n}=I=0
. We next consider the case (ii). We have, for
|x|<1
, the converging Taylor series:

(95)

(1-x)^{-l-1}=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}x^{p}\binom{l+p}{p}.

Since
\binom{l+p}{p}\sim p^{l}/l!
as
p
goes to infinity, it is standard to check that for all
n\geq 1
,

\sum_{p=n}^{\infty}x^{p}\binom{l+p}{p}\leq Cn^{l}\frac{x^{n}}{1-x},

for some constant
C
depending on
l
.

In particular, for
|z|>1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,

(z-\omega)^{-l-1}=\frac{1}{z^{l+1}}\sum_{p=0}^{n-q-1}\left(\frac{\omega}{z}\right)^{p}\binom{l+p}{p}+R_{n}(\omega),

with
|R_{n}(\omega)|\leq Cn^{l}|z|^{-n+q-l}/(|z|-1)
.
We deduce that

I_{n}=\sum_{p=0}^{n-q-1}\frac{1}{z^{l+1+p}}\binom{l+p}{p}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\omega^{k(p+q)}_{n}+R_{n}=\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{q=0}}{z^{l+1}}+R_{n},

with
|R_{n}|\leq Cn^{l}|z|^{-n+q-l}/(|z|-1)
and we have used that
p+q=0\hbox{ mod}(n)
has no solution on
0\leq p\leq n-q-1
except for
q=p=0
. It concludes the proof in this case since
I=\mathbbm{1}_{q=0}/z^{l+1}
by the Residue Theorem (the worst bound corresponding to
q=l
).

The final case (iii) is similar. From (
95
), for
|z|<1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we have for
n\geq l-q+1
,

(z-\omega)^{-l-1}=\frac{1}{(-\omega)^{l+1}}\sum_{p=0}^{n-l+q-2}\left(\frac{z}{\omega}\right)^{p}\binom{l+p}{p}+\tilde{R}_{n}(\omega),

with
|\tilde{R}_{n}(\omega)|\leq Cn^{l}|z|^{n-l+q-1}/(1-|z|)
.
We deduce that

I_{n}=(-1)^{l+1}\sum_{p=0}^{n-l+q-2}z^{p}\binom{l+p}{p}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\omega^{-k(p-q+l+1)}_{n}+\tilde{R}_{n}=\tilde{R}_{n},

where
|\tilde{R}_{n}|\leq Cn^{l}|z|^{n-l+q-1}/(1-|z|)
and we have used that
p-q+l+1=0\hbox{ mod}(n)
has no solution on
0\leq p\leq n-l+q-2
. It concludes the proof since
I=0
by the Residue Theorem (the worst bound corresponding to
q=0
). ∎

7.4
Norm bound for sum-product matrix factors

In this subsection, we consider the following setting. Let
G=(V,E)
be a finite directed graph on the vertex set
V
. The graph
G
is allowed to be a multigraph, that is, the edge set
E
is a finite set equipped with two maps
o,t:E\to V
where
o(e)
is interpreted as the origin vertex of the edge and
t(e)
as the terminal vertex. Note that
o(e)=t(e)
is not forbidden. For ease of notation, we set
e_{-}=o(e)
and
e_{+}=t(e)
. A (weak)
connected component
of
G
is a connected component of the undirected graph obtained from
G
by removing edge orientations. The degree of
v\in V
is
\deg(v)=\sum_{e}\mathbbm{1}(v=o(e))+\mathbbm{1}(v=t(e))
. We say that
G
is
even
if all vertices have even degree. Note that the degree of a vertex is even if and only if its loop-less degree is even.

We consider a collection
M=(M_{e})_{e\in E}
of elements in
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. We are interested in evaluating the expression

(96)

T_{G}(M)=\sum_{\bm{i}}\prod_{e\in E}(M_{e})_{i_{e_{-}}i_{e_{+}}},

where the sum is over all
\bm{i}=(i_{v})_{v\in V}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{V}
.

For example, if
V=\{1,2\}
and
E=\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
with
e_{\varepsilon,-}=1
and
e_{\varepsilon,+}=2
, then

T_{G}(M)=\sum_{i,j}(M_{e_{1}})_{ij}(M_{e_{2}})_{ij}=\Tr(M_{e_{1}}M_{e_{2}}^{\intercal}).

The main result of this subsection is the following general upper bound on
T_{G}(M)
for even graphs. The statement is tailored for our needs. The first statement is a corollary of
[
BS10
, Theorem A.31]
which built upon
[
YK83
]
, see also
[
MS12
]
for an improved bound. We will give an independent proof for completeness.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a finite weakly connected directed even graph and
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{E}
. We have

|T_{G}(M)|\leq n\prod_{e\in E}\|M_{e}\|.

Moreover, if
f\in E
and
M_{f}
has rank
r
, we have

|T_{G}(M)|\leq\sqrt{rn}\prod_{e\in E}\|M_{e}\|.

The remainder of the subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
7.2
. We introduce the following
\ell^{p}\to\ell^{q}
norms in
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
: for
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
,

\|M\|_{1,\infty}=\max_{i,j}|M_{ij}|\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\|M\|_{2,\infty}=\max_{i}\sqrt{\sum_{j}|M_{ij}|^{2}}.

Note that
\|M\|_{1,\infty}\leq\|M\|_{2,\infty}\leq\|M\|_{2,2}=\|M\|\leq\|M\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{n}\|M\|
.

Now, let
G=(V,E)
be as above, we fix an ordering on
E
and write
E=\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}\}
. We classify elements of
E
as follows. Let
e=e_{t}
. We say that
w\in\{e_{-},e_{+}\}

starts at
e
if
w\notin\cup_{s<t}\{e_{s,-},e_{s,+}\}
. We say that
w

ends at
e
if
w\notin\cup_{t<s}\{e_{s,-},e_{s,+}\}
. We say that
e
is
bad
if
e_{+}
and
e_{-}
both starts at
e
or both ends at
e
. Finally,
w
is
open at
e
otherwise.

Next, for
e\in E
and
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, we set

\|M\|_{e}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\|M\|_{\mathrm{F}}&\hbox{ if $e$ is bad},\\
\|M\|_{2,\infty}\vee\|M^{*}\|_{2,\infty}&\hbox{ if $e_{\pm}$ is open and $e_{\mp}$ is extreme at $e$},\\
\|M\|&\hbox{ if $e_{\pm}$ starts at $e$ and $e_{\mp}$ ends at $e$},\\
\|M\|_{1,\infty}&\hbox{ if $e_{-}$ and $e_{+}$ are open at $e$}\end{array}\right.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a finite connected directed graph such that
|V|\geq 2
and all vertices have degree at least two. Then, for any ordering of the edge set
E=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\}
, we have

T^{+}_{G}(M)\leq\prod_{e\in E}\|M_{e}\|_{e}\leq\prod_{e\in E_{b}}\|M_{e}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\prod_{e\in E\backslash E_{b}}\|M_{e}\|,

where
E_{b}
is the set of bad edges.

Proof.

Note that since
\|M_{e}\|_{e}
only depends on the absolute values of the entries of
M_{e}
, it is sufficient to prove the required bound for
T_{G}(M)
. We define for
e=(e_{-},e_{+})
, the matrix in
M_{n^{|V|}}(\mathbb{C})\simeq M_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\otimes V}
,

\tilde{M}_{e}=\sum_{i_{-},i_{+}}(M_{e})_{i_{-}i_{+}}\bigotimes_{v\in V}J_{v,e,i_{-},i_{+}},

where
J_{v,e,i_{-},i_{+}}=I_{n}
if
v\notin\{e_{-},e_{+}\}
,
J_{v,e,i_{-},i_{+}}=E_{1i_{\pm}}
if
e_{\pm}=v
and
v
starts at
e
,
J_{v,e,i_{-},i_{+}}=E_{i_{\pm}1}
if
e_{\pm}=v
and
v
ends at
e
, finally
J_{v,e,i_{-},i_{+}}=E_{i_{\pm}i_{\pm}}
if
e_{\pm}=v
and
v
is open at
e
(note that the case
e_{-}=e_{+}=v
where
v
starts and ends at
e
is not possible since
G
connected and
|V|\geq 2
). We have the following identity

\tilde{M}_{e_{1}}\cdots\tilde{M}_{e_{m}}=E_{11}^{\otimes V}T_{G}(M).

Hence, using the sub-multiplicativity of the operator norm,

{{\left|T_{G}(M)\right|}}\leq\prod_{e\in E}\|\tilde{M}_{e}\|.

We finally observe that

\|\tilde{M}_{e}\|=\|M\|_{e}.

Indeed, since
\|I\otimes A\|=\|A\|
, it suffices to check to following identities, for
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
,

\displaystyle\|\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}E_{ii}\otimes E_{jj}\|=\|M\|_{1,\infty}\,,\quad\|\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}E_{ii}\otimes E_{1j}\|=\|M\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\|\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}E_{1i}\otimes E_{j1}\|=\|M\|\;\hbox{ and }\;\|\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}E_{1i}\otimes E_{1j}\|=\|M\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

The conclusion follows.
∎

The next lemma uses the probabilistic method to prove the existence of an ordering which does not have too many bad edges.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a finite directed even graph with
|V|\geq 2
. There exists an ordering of
E
with two bad edges. Moreover, if
f\in E
, there exists an ordering of
E
such that
f
is among the two bad edges.

Proof.

Let
\bar{G}=(V,\bar{E})
be the un-directed multi-graph associated to
G
. Let
m=|E|=|\bar{E}|
be the number of edges. Since
\bar{G}
is even, from Euler’s Theorem, there exists an Eulerian circuit on
\bar{G}
, that is a sequence
(\bar{\pi}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\pi}_{m})
in
\bar{E}
such that for all
t=1,\ldots,m

\bar{\pi}_{t}
and
\bar{\pi}_{t+1}
share at least one neighboring vertex with
\bar{\pi}_{m+1}=\bar{\pi}_{1}
. For each
t
, let
\pi_{t}\in E
be the corresponding edge in
E
. We consider the ordering
\pi_{1},ldots,\pi_{m}
. By construction,
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{m}
are the only two bad edges. Moreover, using the cyclicity of the Eulerian circuit, we can choose the circuit such that
\pi_{1}=f
.
∎

We are ready for the proof of Theorem
7.2
.

Proof of Theorem
7.2
.

If
|V|=1
then the statement is a straightforward consequence of the bounds
\|M\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{rank}(M)}\|M\|
. If
|V|\geq 2
, it is a consequence of Lemma
7.3
and Lemma
7.4
.
∎
